How to read this deck

Every card answers something the final study guide actually asks — nothing beyond scope.

One card per study guide item

Why it mattersThe RCP 203 final study guide lists 34 topic lines, many with several parts. Each section is one line; each card answers one part of it, kept short on purpose.

The panels are colour-coded

Why it mattersWhy the fact behaves as it does.
Exam anchorThe pearl, the trap, the number to memorise.
GapHonestly absent from the decks and the book — go look it up.
CautionSources disagree; the panel says which one to answer.

Every card has a source line

Why it mattersThe grey line at the foot of each card names the deck and slide (or speaker note), or the Hess chapter and section. Where the book adds to a deck, the text says Hess adds. Where no deck covers an item, the card says so first and answers from Hess.

140 Questions / 100 points / 150 Minutes

Two sources only: the Study Brain lecture decks (slide text and speaker notes) and Hess, MacIntyre & Galvin, Respiratory Care: Principles and Practice, 4th ed. Nothing comes from general knowledge. Every number was checked word for word against those sources, and every section was re-read by an independent fact-checker and corrected.

Updated 19 Oct 2026 with the new Lssn 7 (Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation) PowerPoint, plus the RCP 202 Trauma and Bronchoscopy PowerPoints and the revised RCP 202 Advanced Modes PowerPoint where they answer an RCP 203 ask. 01

Pneumonia, CXRs, Pleural Effusions, Accessory Muscles, Extubation Parameters

7 items · PNA · CXRs · pleural effusions · accessory muscles · extubation parameters · + extubation readiness checks (Lssn 8b)

1Pneumonia (PNA): what it is, signs and symptoms, diagnosis2Pneumonia categories, and typical vs atypical pneumonia3Reading a CXR4Pleural effusion5Accessory muscles6Extubation (weaning) parameters7Extubation Readiness — the Checks That Come After the SBT

1Pneumonia (PNA): what it is, signs and symptoms, diagnosis

The RCP 103 deck defines pneumonia as "an infectious inflammatory process that primarily affects the gas exchange area of the lungs causing capillary fluid to pour into the alveoli." Causes are bacteria, viruses, fungi or aspiration. Hess calls it "inflammation and consolidation of lung tissue, most frequently due to infection."

Finding What the decks say
Vital signs Fever (bacterial > 101 °F, viral < 101 °F), ↑ HR, RR, BP. Tachypnea comes from hypoxemia (peripheral chemoreceptors) and poor lung compliance
Cough and sputum Starts dry and hacking, then becomes productive (yellow/green). It may be rust-colored or show hemoptysis
Exam (consolidation) ↑ vocal fremitus + dull percussion + bronchial breath sounds/crackles, plus whispered pectoriloquy. Chest expansion is ↓ on the affected side in lobar PNA
CXR ↑ density (consolidation/atelectasis), air bronchograms, and possibly an abscess (air-fluid level) or effusion/empyema
ABG Early: respiratory alkalosis with hypoxemia. Severe: respiratory acidosis with hypoxemia
CBC, sputum, PFT ↑ WBC in bacterial PNA, ↓ WBC in viral PNA. Sputum culture & sensitivity. Restrictive PFT pattern

Hess adds: "Pneumonia is a clinical diagnosis." No single test makes it, but an infiltrate on imaging is a necessary part of the diagnosis. A pathogen is isolated in only 40% of cases. RCP 203: BAL is indicated for non-resolving pneumonias and is a key tool for diagnosing bacterial VAP.

SourceRCP 103 Infectious Pulmonary Disease, slides 2, 26, 27 (+ notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slides 38, 39, 50 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5 Recommend Diagnostics, slide 15 · Hess Ch 36 — Definition and Classification of Pneumonia; Diagnostic Workup

2Pneumonia categories, and typical vs atypical pneumonia

Category Definition (deck)
CAP "Acquired from normal social contact." Hess: S. pneumoniae is the most common bacterium
HAP ≥ 48 hours after hospital admission and not present at admission. Main organisms: Pseudomonas aeruginosa, MSSA, MRSA
VAP Develops after intubation. RCP 103 says > 48–72 h, the RCP 105 updated notes say ≥ 48 h, and RCP 203 Lssn 6a says ventilated > 48 h. Use ≥ 48 h
HCAP Hospitalized within 90 days, a nursing-home resident, or IV antibiotics, chemo or wound care within 30 days. Hess says the term was "retired in 2016"

Typical vs atypical (Hess Ch 36):

  • Typical pneumonia has an abrupt onset with rigors, high fever and pleuritic chest pain. The sputum is rusty or purulent, the WBC is usually elevated, and the CXR shows lobar/segmental consolidation with air bronchograms. The main organism is S. pneumoniae.
  • Atypical pneumonia is a flu-like illness: dry cough, moderate fever and no rigors. It is more common in young adults. The WBC is usually normal, and the CXR shows diffuse interstitial or peribronchial opacities. The organisms (Mycoplasma, Chlamydia, Legionella and others) are not seen on Gram stain.
  • Mycoplasma is the most common atypical cause and produces "walking pneumonia." For treatment, the deck says "Atypical pneumonia? → Think macrolide."
SourceRCP 103 Infectious Pulmonary Disease, slides 7, 9, 12, 13, 16, 17, 18 · RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slide 59 · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 43 (notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 14 — Antimicrobials, slide 29 (notes) · Hess Ch 36 — Definition and Classification; Etiology

3Reading a CXR

RCP 203 steps:

  1. Verify the patient ID, orientation and image quality.
  2. Identify the view: AP or PA.
  3. Check the hemidiaphragms and costophrenic angles (sharp or blunted).
  4. Check the tracheal position.
  5. Estimate the cardiothoracic ratio.
  6. Note any tubes and lines.
  7. Compare with previous films.

Image quality = RIPE: Rotation, Inspiration, Position (AP or PA), Exposure. An underexposed film is too white; an overexposed film is too dark.

Finding Means
Opacity = white ("more stuff") Atelectasis, consolidation/pneumonia, pulmonary edema, pleural effusion
Lucency = black ("more air") Emphysema, pneumothorax
Blunted costophrenic angle Fluid (pleural effusion)
Flattened diaphragm Hyperinflation (COPD/emphysema, acute asthma)
Elevated diaphragm Volume loss or diaphragm weakness (atelectasis, phrenic nerve paralysis)
Heart width > 50% of chest width on a PA film Cardiomegaly. Don't apply this rule to an AP film, because AP magnifies the heart
Kerley B lines Interstitial pulmonary edema (heart failure)
ETT tip Should sit 4–6 cm above the carina. Too deep → usually the right mainstem

Hess adds:

  • A lateral decubitus film identifies free pleural fluid.
  • An expiratory film shows a small pneumothorax.
  • An air bronchogram means the opacity is in the lung, not the pleura or mediastinum.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slides 52–57 (+ notes) · Hess Ch 7 — Positioning; Air Bronchogram

4Pleural effusion

A pleural effusion is an accumulation of fluid in the pleural space. The common causes are CHF, cancer and pneumonia. It produces a restrictive disorder because it limits lung expansion and reduces compliance.

Transudate Exudate
Mechanism Systemic pressure changes Pleural inflammation → ↑ capillary permeability
Fluid Thin, watery, low protein, few cells; clear, light straw High protein, many cells; cloudy/opaque
Causes CHF (most common), cirrhosis, nephrotic syndrome Infection (pneumonia, TB), malignancy, autoimmune disease
Lab — Fluid pH < 7.30 suggests an exudate (deck). Hess (Light): fluid:serum protein ≥ 0.5 or LDH ≥ 0.6
  • Exam: the trachea shifts away from the effusion (if large). Tactile fremitus is ↓, percussion is dull, and breath sounds are diminished or absent.
  • CXR: blunting of the costophrenic angle is the earliest and most common sign. Larger effusions add a meniscus sign and a mediastinal shift to the unaffected side. Effusions less than about 300 mL may not show on a standard CXR.
  • Thoracentesis (RCP 203): diagnostic to tell transudate from exudate, or therapeutic when the effusion is large enough to cause respiratory distress. Up to about 1500 mL may be removed. Complications: pneumothorax, bleeding, infection, re-expansion pulmonary edema.
SourceRCP 103 Disorders of the Pleura & Chest Wall, slides 2, 5, 6, 7, 8, 14, 20 (+ notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slides 66, 67 (+ notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slide 50 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5 Recommend Diagnostics, slide 35 · Hess Ch 9 — Thoracentesis

5Accessory muscles

Muscles
Inspiration RCP 203: sternocleidomastoid, scalenes, upper trapezius, pectoralis major. Hess also lists the external intercostals
Expiration (Hess Ch 49) Internal intercostals, rectus abdominis, external and internal abdominal obliques, transversus abdominis

What their use means:

  • The deck's main takeaway: accessory muscle use at rest = increased WOB. It is common in emphysema and in any patient with increased WOB.
  • Expiration is normally passive. The expiratory accessory muscles join in when resistance to expiration is significant (severe obstructive disease). Abdominal contraction = active exhalation.

Clinical uses:

  • Accessory muscle use and thoracoabdominal paradox are signs of increased WOB that mean consider discontinuing the SBT (RCP 203 Ch 11; the Ch 4 slide says discontinue). Accessory muscle use is also an early sign of weaning failure. NHLBI protocol: marked accessory muscle use is one distress sign (two or more = distress).
  • NBRC pearl: RR 38 + accessory muscle use + diaphoresis + declining VT + rising PaCO₂ = impending ventilatory failure due to muscle fatigue.
  • Exam trap: neuromuscular blockade (paralysis) masks accessory muscle use.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slide 37 (+ notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 60 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 93, 94 · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation (Chang Ch 13, 16), slide 26 (notes) · RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 5 (notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 18 — NMBA, slide 34 (notes) · Hess Ch 49 — Accessory Muscles

6Extubation (weaning) parameters

Parameter Use this (deck) Other values
RSBI = f ÷ VT (L) < 100 (RCP 105) < 105 (Hess; the deck says the NBRC commonly uses < 105)
NIF / MIP −20 to −30 cm H₂O in 20 sec. RCP 203: a minimum of −20 to extubate Normal is > −60 ("more negative"); −40 in COPD, −20 in MG (RCP 203)
VC > 10 mL/kg (RCP 105) 15 mL/kg (about 1 L) (RCP 203 notes, Hess)
Spontaneous VT ≥ 5 mL/kg: the general weaning predictor (RCP 105, Hess) ≥ 4 mL/kg PBW only as an NHLBI ARDS-protocol SBT tolerance criterion (RCP 203 slide 94)
V̇E Normal 6–10 L/min; > 10 L/min is often linked to poor weaning outcomes Hess: 12 L/min
Oxygenation RCP 203: P/F ≥ 150–200, PEEP ≤ 5–8, FiO₂ ≤ 0.4–0.5. RCP 105: P/F ≥ 150; PaO₂ > 60 on FiO₂ ≤ 0.40 —
pH ≥ 7.25 to screen —
Compliance > 30 mL/cm H₂O (RCP 105). RCP 203: < 25–30 → weaning difficulty —
SBT passed (RCP 203, after ≥ 30 min) SpO₂ ≥ 85–90% or PaO₂ ≥ 50–60; pH ≥ 7.30; PaCO₂ rise ≤ 10; RR ≤ 30–35 (change < 50%); HR < 120–140; SBP < 180–200 and > 90; no vasopressors SBT length: RCP 105 says up to 30 min; RCP 203 says 30–120 min, after a 2–5 min screening phase
NHLBI "ARDS protocol" (RCP 203) Ready for an SBT: FiO₂ ≤ 0.40 and PEEP ≤ 8, or FiO₂ ≤ 0.50 and PEEP ≤ 5. SBT up to 120 min on T-piece, trach collar or CPAP ≤ 5. Tolerated = SpO₂ ≥ 90% and/or PaO₂ ≥ 60, VT ≥ 4 mL/kg PBW, RR ≤ 35, pH ≥ 7.30, no distress; tolerated ≥ 30 min → consider extubation —

Spontaneous VT trap: answer ≥ 5 mL/kg for a general weaning predictor; use ≥ 4 mL/kg PBW only when the question names the NHLBI/ARDS-protocol SBT.

Worked RSBI (RCP 203): RR 38, VT 300 mL → 38 ÷ 0.3 = 127, above 100, so the patient is not ready. If an SBT fails, find the cause and repeat the SBT every 24 hours.

RCP 203 Lssn 6a (criteria, no numbers): extubation is "considered only" in patients who can maintain oxygenation and ventilation without support (shown by an SBT), are at minimal risk of upper-airway obstruction and aspiration, and can clear their own secretions. The most serious extubation complication is laryngospasm.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slides 16, 18, 19 (notes), 45 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 52, 59, 60 · RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slides 62, 63 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 90–94 · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 11–19 · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slide 30 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Assessing Readiness for Liberation

7Extubation Readiness — the Checks That Come After the SBT

Passing a spontaneous breathing trial and being ready to pull the tube are two different decisions. The Ch 13 deck is explicit: the decision to extubate a patient who successfully completes an SBT should be made separately from the weaning assessment. Item 6 covers the weaning numbers; this card is what you check afterwards.

Before the trial: the sedation vacation

  • A daily sedation interruption (DSI) — a “sedation vacation,” called an SAT (spontaneous awakening trial) in some hospitals — should be performed for all mechanically ventilated patients stable enough to tolerate being off sedation.
  • Why: it allows a more accurate assessment of readiness for extubation, including a neurologic assessment, and may facilitate the daily SBT.
  • If the SBT is not tolerated, return the patient to the previous settings.

Ready for extubation if all four hold

  1. The upper airway is patent — this is what the cuff-leak test is for.
  2. The airway can be adequately protected: a positive gag reflex, and the ability to raise the head off the bed.
  3. Adequate artificial airway cuff leak — no sign of significant anatomic airway swelling.
  4. Secretions can be effectively cleared: a deep cough when suctioned, and, if the patient is alert, MEP > 60 cm H₂O and/or peak cough flow > 60 L/min.
Exam anchor — peak cough flow has three bandsPeak cough flow (PCF) is the maximum expiratory flow generated during a cough, measured on a peak flow meter or spirometer after a deep breath and a maximal cough. > 160 L/min — normal, strong cough. 60–160 L/min — weak but possibly adequate; borderline, may need assistance. < 60 L/min — inadequate; the patient is not ready for extubation because of the high risk of secretion retention. The deck gives > 60 L/min as the extubation threshold, which is the bottom of that middle band. For context the notes put normal expiratory muscle strength (MEP) at +80 to +150 cm H₂O, so the 60 cm H₂O extubation figure is well below normal — it is a floor, not a target.
Caution — a mode this guide still teachesThe same slide states that “SIMV is no longer recommended” for weaning — and then adds, in the instructor’s own parenthesis, “but you may be tested on it!” Section 20 covers SIMV in full because the study guide asks for it. Treat both as true: know SIMV for the exam, and know that it is not current practice for liberation.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 8b — Ch 13 Ensure Modifications Are Made to the Respiratory Care Plan, 65 slides (added 2026-10-25), slides 22–25 and speaker notes

Pharmacology Calculations

5 items · Pharmacology calculations (the class handout)

1Units and Conversions2The Proportion Method and mg/kg Dosing3Percentage-Strength Solutions: % → mg/mL → Dose4Diluent Does Not Change the Dose5Ratio Solutions, w/w – w/v – v/v, and Diluting a Stock Solution

Still not covered: the pharmacology class's calculations handout itself is not in the vault, and its mg/kg worked examples (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 4 slides 10–11) are image-only with no extractable text — check the paper handout. IV drip-rate / mcg/kg/min infusion formulas are in no deck or Hess chapter searched.

1Units and Conversions

Most drugs are expressed in milligrams (mg) or micrograms (mcg); the metric units are meter/cm (length), liter/mL (volume) and gram/mg (mass). Key conversions:

Conversion Value
mL vs cc exactly the same volume (3 mL = 3 cc)
mg → g 1,000 mg = 1 g (move the decimal 3 places); 4,500 mg = 4.5 g
milli- one-thousandth (1/1000) of the base unit
1 teaspoon 5 mL = 60 drops (gtts)
1 tablespoon 15 mL (3 tsp) = 180 drops
1 cup 240 mL (8 fl oz)
SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 4 — Calculating Drug Doses, slides 4, 14 (notes)

2The Proportion Method and mg/kg Dosing

First convert to consistent units, then set up the proportion: Known Dose ÷ Known Amount = Desired Dose ÷ Desired Amount (or Original Dose/Per Amount = Desired Dose/Per Amount) and cross-multiply. For a dosage schedule (mg/kg): step 1, calculate the dose needed (mg/kg × weight); step 2, calculate the amount of the preparation with the same proportion. The deck's own worked mg/kg examples are on the handout (slides 10–11 are images).

Worked example (ours, applying the deck's mg/kg rule): caffeine citrate loading dose is 20 mg/kg, maintenance 5 mg/kg. For a 1.5-kg infant: 20 × 1.5 = 30 mg loading; 5 × 1.5 = 7.5 mg daily. Other mg/kg doses in the decks: beractant 4 mg/kg birth weight; poractant 2.5 mL/kg (maximum total 5 mL/kg). The deck's rule: write the calculation out — patient safety outranks pride in mental math.

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 4 — Calculating Drug Doses, slides 8, 9 (notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 8 — Xanthines, slide 28 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 10 — Surfactant Agents, slides 17, 21

3Percentage-Strength Solutions: % → mg/mL → Dose

A percent means grams per 100 mL. Since 1% = 1 g/100 mL = 1000 mg/100 mL, the deck's shortcut is Drug % × 10 = mg/mL. Then use two steps: (1) convert % → mg/mL (the concentration), and (2) multiply by the volume to get the dose, or divide the dose by the concentration to get the volume.

Problem Step 1 Step 2
mg in 3 mL of 0.083% albuterol? 0.083 × 10 = 0.83 mg/mL 0.83 × 3 = 2.49 mg (deck prints ~2.5 mg = the standard neb dose)
mL of 0.5% albuterol for 2.5 mg? 0.5 × 10 = 5 mg/mL 2.5 ÷ 5 = 0.5 mL
Racemic epi 2.25%, 0.25–0.5 mL 2.25 × 10 = 22.5 mg/mL 5.625–11.25 mg (deck prints 5.63–11.25 mg)

Trap: the most common mistake is forgetting to convert the % first, or mixing up mg (weight) and mL (volume).

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 4 — Calculating Drug Doses, slides 12–15 (notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 6 — Adrenergic Bronchodilators, slide 18 (notes)

4Diluent Does Not Change the Dose

The amount of diluent changes only the time to give the drug; the amount of active drug is the same. 2.5 mg of albuterol is 2.5 mg whether it is mixed with 2, 5 or 10 mL of saline. Diluent is added because most nebulizers won't aerosolize well below ~1 mL (dead volume) and treatment time matters, so the aim is about 2.5–3 mL total volume. Exam trap: "more saline = weaker treatment" is wrong.

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 4 — Calculating Drug Doses, slides 16, 17 (notes)

5Ratio Solutions, w/w – w/v – v/v, and Diluting a Stock Solution

No deck in the vault works these calculations; the answer below is from Hess Ch 55. The three percent types are w/w, w/v and v/v; w/v (grams per mL × 100%) is the one used in pharmacology. Ratio: 1:100 = 1 g in 100 mL. Hess's example: 0.5 mL of 1:200 epinephrine → 1:200 = 1000 mg/200 mL = 5 mg/mL × 0.5 mL = 2.5 mg. By the same rule (our calculation), 1:1000 = 1 mg/mL, so the deck's 0.3–0.5 mg of 1:1000 epinephrine is 0.3–0.5 mL. Dilution: V₁ × C₁ = V₂ × C₂. To make 5 mL of 10% from 20% stock: x × 20 = 5 × 10 → x = 2.5 mL of stock + 2.5 mL water. ppm: 10,000 ppm = 1%.

Hess's w/v example, albuterol 2.5 mg/0.5 mL, prints 5%, but 2.5 mg ÷ 0.5 mL = 5 mg/mL = 0.5% (reversing the deck's % × 10 = mg/mL rule gives the same). Use 0.5%.

SourceHess Ch 55 — Equations 55-5 to 55-9 · Hess Ch 14 — Box 14-1 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 73 (notes)
03

Ventilator Changes After ABGs

6 items · Ventilator changes after ABGs · + the 60-60 rule: FiO₂ vs PEEP (Lssn 8b)

1The decision table: which setting fixes which ABG value2Calculating the new rate for a target PaCO₂3When the ABG says change the vent but you should not4Oxygenation targets, FiO₂ limits, FiO₂ vs PEEP5Adjusting BiPAP after the ABG6Which Knob for Hypoxemia — the 60-60 Rule, and When to Lower Which

1The decision table: which setting fixes which ABG value

RCP 203 rule: normalize the pH by adjusting the minute ventilation, which alters the PaCO₂. Goal pH 7.35–7.45, but you may allow 7.25 to 7.50. Oxygenation is fixed with FiO₂ and PEEP (item 4). RCP 105: "Rate controls CO₂. FiO₂ and PEEP control oxygenation."

ABG finding Change
PaCO₂ high / respiratory acidosis (↑ PaCO₂, ↓ pH) RCP 203: ↑ the set rate first, keeping it 12–24/min (up to 35/min in acute lung injury). If rate alone fails, ↑ VT, keeping it 4–8 mL/kg PBW with Pplat ≤ 30–35 cm H₂O (slide 38; slide 31: if VC needs Pplat > 30, probably switch to PC at 30 or less). In PC, VT changes with the driving pressure, ∆P (PIP − PEEP). RCP 105: ↑ frequency is the most common approach; also ↑ PS
PaCO₂ low / respiratory alkalosis ↓ frequency or ↓ VT (only if the cause is overventilation; see item 3)
PaO₂ low, PaCO₂ near normal (V/Q mismatch) ↑ FiO₂ (the most common approach to regulating PaO₂)
PaO₂ low with PaCO₂ high (hypoventilation) Improve ventilation first
Refractory hypoxemia, P/F < 200 Add or ↑ PEEP. As the O₂ need rises, raise FiO₂ and PEEP together (ARDSNet example: FiO₂ 30% → PEEP ~5; 60% → ~10; 100% → 20–24)
  • Targets: PaCO₂ near 40, or near 50 for CO₂ retainers. In chronic CO₂ retention a normal pH may come with PaCO₂ ≥ 55 (RCP 203), so a COPD patient at 55 / pH 7.37 need not be driven to 40.
  • Exam trap: raising FiO₂ does not lower PaCO₂.
  • VT range is narrow: too much → lung injury; too little → hypoventilation and atelectasis. In ARDS with a rising Pplat, the safest first move is often to ↓ VT, not ↑ it.
  • Permissive hypercapnia (RCP 203): if the pH stays below 7.30 with rate, VT and Pplat at their high limits, let the PaCO₂ rise as long as the pH stays above 7.20–7.25. RCP 105: VT is cut on purpose to 4–7 mL/kg, and the acidosis is managed with bicarbonate or tromethamine.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 31, 36, 38 · RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slides 12, 13 (+ notes) · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 4, 6 (+ notes), 8, 11, 26 (notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slides 30, 34 (notes) · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 37 (notes)

2Calculating the new rate for a target PaCO₂

The slide prints the calculation only as numbers: X = 14 × 60 ÷ 40 = 840 ÷ 40 = 21 is the new f.

Read it as set rate (14) × current PaCO₂ (60) ÷ desired PaCO₂ (40). The slide doesn't label the terms. The setup matches the Initiation deck's question, "If my PaCO₂ is 60 mmHg and I want it to be 40 mmHg, which ventilator setting am I most likely to change?", whose answer is "Respiratory Rate (or VT)."

Why it mattersWhy it works (Hess): if CO₂ production is constant, PaCO₂ varies inversely with alveolar ventilation. An increase in V̇E decreases PaCO₂, and a decrease in V̇E increases it. Checked: 14 × 60 = 840, and 840 ÷ 40 = 21 breaths/min.

Limit: in COPD, too high a rate shortens expiratory time and causes air trapping and auto-PEEP.

Gap · still not covereda written (labeled) new-rate formula and any new-VT formula. No deck or Hess text states either; the deck shows only the numeric example above — check the instructor's Management of MV slide image (Chang Ch 12).
SourceRCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 4 · RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 12 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Ventilation (PaCO₂)

3When the ABG says change the vent but you should not

ABG picture Deck's action
↓ PaCO₂ from overventilation (improper settings) Reduce f, VT or PS
↓ PaCO₂ as a response to hypoxia Do not reduce ventilation. Fix the hypoxia first
↓ PaCO₂ compensating for metabolic acidosis (DKA, lactic acidosis) Do not reduce ventilation. The patient needs to hyperventilate
↓ PaCO₂ from anxiety Do not reduce support. Find the cause
↑ PaCO₂ (e.g., pH 7.30 / PaCO₂ 50) from oversedation or respiratory muscle fatigue Find and correct the cause (e.g., delay weaning). Increasing the frequency is "not the proper action"
Metabolic acidosis or alkalosis No ventilator adjustment. Don't ↑ support for metabolic acidosis or ↓ it for metabolic alkalosis

The rule: a compensatory PaCO₂ must not be "normalized." Lowering the rate on a patient who is compensating would likely lead to worse outcomes.

SourceRCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 24, 26, 27 (+ notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slides 29, 30 (notes)

4Oxygenation targets, FiO₂ limits, FiO₂ vs PEEP

Target Source
In healthy pts: PaO₂ 80–100 with SpO₂ ≥ 92% on the lowest FiO₂ that keeps it. If that can't be kept on < 50% O₂, accept PaO₂ ≥ 55–60 with SpO₂ ≥ 88%. Chronic hypercapnia: PaO₂ 55–60 RCP 203 Ch 11 (FiO₂ titration)
PaO₂ 80–100 mm Hg or SpO₂ > 95% (lower for COPD); FiO₂ < 50% to avoid O₂ toxicity RCP 105 Initiation of MV
PaO₂ < 80 mm Hg (< 60 for COPD) or FiO₂ < 60% to prevent O₂ toxicity RCP 105 Management of MV
PaO₂ 55–80, SpO₂ 88–95% (ARDSNet lung-protective goals) RCP 105 Procedures; Hess Ch 20
SpO₂ > 92% (= PaO₂ > 60) lets you wean FiO₂ using pulse oximetry RCP 105 Monitoring in MV

FiO₂ or PEEP? (RCP 203): if an acceptable PaO₂/SaO₂ holds on < 50% O₂, change the FiO₂; if it cannot, change the PEEP (baseline pressure). If P/F ≤ 200, or ≥ 50% O₂ with PEEP ≥ 12 fails, recommend the NHLBI ARDS protocol. When oxygenation improves, lower FiO₂ to ≤ 50% first, then PEEP. Also confirm Hb ≥ 10 g/dL and adequate circulation.

  • PEEP increases FRC and corrects refractory hypoxemia due to intrapulmonary shunting. Too little lets alveoli collapse; too much overdistends them and reduces cardiac output.
  • Hess: ventilation is commonly started on FiO₂ 1.0 and then titrated to the SpO₂.
  • Conflicts: FiO₂ ceiling < 50% (RCP 105 Initiation; also RCP 203's switch point) vs < 60% (Management) → use < 50%. Routine goal in healthy pts PaO₂ 80–100 with SpO₂ ≥ 92% (RCP 203) vs SpO₂ > 95% (RCP 105 Initiation) vs PaO₂ < 80 (Management) → use 80–100 / ≥ 92%; for ARDS lung-protective ventilation use 55–80 / 88–95%.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 7, 39 · RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 17 · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 11, 16 · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 37, 38 (notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slide 38 · Hess Ch 20 — Oxygenation

5Adjusting BiPAP after the ABG

IPAP − EPAP = ∆P (the pressure support). Adjust ventilation (PaCO₂, pH) via IPAP and ∆P; adjust oxygenation (PaO₂/SaO₂) via FiO₂ and EPAP.

Patient on 10/5, needs… Change (deck's NBRC examples)
Oxygenation only ↑ IPAP and EPAP equally → 12/7. ∆P stays the same, so ventilation doesn't change
Ventilation only ↑ IPAP only → 15/5. ∆P rises; the baseline is unchanged
Both ↑ both, IPAP more than EPAP → 15/7
  • Rule: you can raise IPAP without raising EPAP, but never raise EPAP without raising IPAP by the same amount.
  • Keep IPAP < 20–25 cm H₂O to avoid gastric insufflation. If ventilation or oxygenation worsens, consider intubation.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 52, 54, 57

6Which Knob for Hypoxemia — the 60-60 Rule, and When to Lower Which

During mechanical ventilation, arterial oxygenation is affected mainly by two parameters: the FiO₂ and the PEEP. This card is the decision rule the Ch 13 deck gives for choosing between them, in both directions.

Too much oxygen — PaO₂ > 100 torr

  • Lower whichever parameter is potentially most dangerous to the patient at that moment.
  • PEEP 10 cm H₂O with FiO₂ 75% → the FiO₂ is the concern — oxygen toxicity.
  • PEEP 18 cm H₂O with FiO₂ 45% → the PEEP is the concern — barotrauma.
  • When oxygenation improves, first decrease FiO₂ until it is 0.50 or less, and then lower PEEP.

Too little — PaO₂ < 60 torr or SaO₂ < 90%

  • Increase the FiO₂ if it is below 0.60.
  • Increase PEEP if the FiO₂ is already at or above 0.60.

The 60-60 rule — it tells you the mechanism, and the mechanism picks the knob

  • PaO₂ > 60 torr on FiO₂ < 0.6 → the problem is mainly a V̇/Q̇ imbalance, which responds to a simple increase in FiO₂.
  • PaO₂ < 60 torr on FiO₂ > 0.6 → the problem is shunting, and PEEP or CPAP must be added or increased.
  • The 50-50 rule is the same logic with rounder numbers: PaO₂ < 50 on FiO₂ > 0.5 → significant shunt, add PEEP; PaO₂ > 50 on FiO₂ < 0.5 → mostly V̇/Q̇ mismatch, increase FiO₂. The notes call it “a more conservative version of the 60/60 rule.”
  • Beyond the two knobs, recruitment maneuvers can be recommended where ARDS and severe atelectasis are suspected.
Why it mattersThis is section 19 turned into an order. A shunt does not respond to oxygen because the blood never meets ventilated alveoli — so raising FiO₂ past 0.6 buys almost nothing and starts costing toxicity. PEEP is the shunt treatment because it recruits the collapsed units that are causing the shunt in the first place. V̇/Q̇ mismatch does respond to oxygen, which is why the low-FiO₂ case is solved by simply turning it up.

And for the CO₂ side: which control to move

  • Abnormal alveolar ventilation shows up as an abnormal pH driven by an abnormal PaCO₂. Low PaCO₂ → decrease V̇E; high PaCO₂ → increase V̇E, using the formula already in item 2.
  • As long as the VT is properly set up — 6–8 mL/kg PBW with a Pplat < 30 cm H₂O — RR changes are the preferred method to alter V̇E.
  • Be careful specifying the desired PaCO₂: the goal is the PaCO₂ that normalises the pH, which is usually 40 torr but not always — in a COPD patient who develops ventilatory failure on top of a compensated respiratory acidosis (acute-on-chronic), the desired PaCO₂ could be substantially higher than normal.
Caution — do not confuse this with the other number ruleSection 31 of this guide covers the 40-50-60 / 70-80-90 rule. That is a different thing entirely — it is about matching PaO₂ values to SaO₂ values. 60-60 and 50-50 are about choosing FiO₂ versus PEEP. Same shape, different job.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 8b — Ch 13 Ensure Modifications Are Made to the Respiratory Care Plan, 65 slides (added 2026-10-25), slides 41, 43 and speaker notes
04

Lung Sounds: All of Them, and What Causes Each

6 items · Every lung sound and what causes it

1Normal Breath Sounds and Where They Belong2Diminished or Absent Breath Sounds3Crackles (Rales): Fine and Coarse4Wheezes, Rhonchi and Stridor5Pleural Friction Rub, Grunting and Voice Sounds6Breath Sounds by Condition

1Normal Breath Sounds and Where They Belong

A breath sound is normal only at its specified location; a normal sound in an abnormal location is abnormal. The deck says to learn the location more than the description.

Sound Normal location (deck) Character
Vesicular Peripheral lung fields Soft, low-pitched; inspiration lasts longer than expiration (Hess)
Bronchovesicular Upper sternum / between scapulae (over main bronchi) Moderate pitch and intensity; inspiration and expiration equally long (Hess)
Bronchial (Hess: "bronchial/tracheal (tubular)") Trachea Loud, high-pitched; expiration as long as or slightly longer than inspiration (Hess)

Bronchial sounds out in the lung fields suggest consolidation or atelectasis. Cause: consolidated lung fills with fluid, mucus, pus or cellular debris, and the dense tissue carries the large-airway sound to the chest wall.

Caution · sources differConflict: a Hess figure description calls vesicular sounds "high-pitched, breezy"; Hess Table 1-2 and the RCP 103 deck say low-pitched. Use low-pitched (deck).
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2, slides 46, 47 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 41 (and notes) · RCP 103 ARDS, slide 16 (notes) · Hess Ch 1 — Table 1-2, Auscultation; Hess Ch 62 (Fig 1-11 description)

2Diminished or Absent Breath Sounds

Breath sounds get quieter when less air moves or sound is blocked from the chest wall. Deck causes: shallow or slow breathing, obstructed airways or a hyperinflated lung (asthma, COPD), air or fluid in the pleural space, and obesity.

  • Diffuse ↓ (Hess): shallow breathing; hyperinflation with low airflow (emphysema, acute asthma).
  • Localized ↓ (Hess): airway obstruction, atelectasis, main-stem intubation; ↓ at the bases is common with postoperative atelectasis.
  • Unilateral ↓ (Hess Ch 20): bronchial intubation, pneumothorax, atelectasis, pleural effusion.
  • Absent: the deck pairs absent sounds with pneumothorax, pleural effusion and atelectasis (item 6).
  • Silent chest in asthma: airflow so low the wheeze disappears. Never improvement; a late, critical warning (respiratory arrest imminent).
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2, slide 46 (and notes), slide 50 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 43 (notes) · RCP 103 Asthma, slide 28 (and notes) · Hess Ch 1 — Auscultation · Hess Ch 20

3Crackles (Rales): Fine and Coarse

Adventitious sounds are extra sounds superimposed on the normal breath sounds: rhonchi, wheezes, crackles (rales) and stridor (RCP 103 Clinical Data slide 42 calls rhonchi an outdated term; RCP 203 Ch 2 still lists it). "Crackles" is the preferred modern term. Crackles are discontinuous, popping sounds; deck cause: airway/alveolar opening or secretions, depending on the type.

  • Fine crackles: high-pitched, end of inspiration, most often over the bases. Cause: terminal airways or fluid-filled/collapsed alveoli popping open. Seen in pulmonary edema (the deck's "hallmark sign"; crackles climbing higher up the lung fields = worsening CHF, Hess), ARDS, atelectasis and ILD (small airways reopening in stiff lungs; Hess Ch 34: bibasilar "Velcro-like" crackles in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis).
  • Pneumonia: crackles over the involved lobe (Hess Ch 1; RCP 103 Infectious slide 26); fine crackles in infants with RDS or pneumonia (Hess Ch 22, neonatal/pediatric).
  • Coarse crackles: excessive airway secretions (RCP 103 Bronchiectasis, COPD and Pulm Edema cases). Hess Ch 22 (neonatal/pediatric): air moving through fluid in the large airways, usually cleared by suctioning or coughing. Coarse crackles over the trachea = retained secretions, an indication to suction (Hess Box 17-3).
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2, slides 46, 47 (notes) · RCP 103 Pulm Edema, slide 16 (notes) · RCP 103 ARDS, slide 16 (notes) · RCP 103 ILD, slide 41 (notes) · RCP 103 Atelectasis, slide 13 (crackles) and slide 26 (case: fine crackles at the bases) · RCP 103 Infectious, slide 26 · RCP 103 Bronchiectasis, slides 21, 22 · RCP 103 COPD, slide 32 · RCP 103 Pulm Edema, slide 37 · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 42 (+ notes) · Hess Ch 1 — Adventitious Breath Sounds · Hess Ch 17 — Box 17-3 · Hess Ch 22 · Hess Ch 34 — Idiopathic Pulmonary Fibrosis

4Wheezes, Rhonchi and Stridor

Sound What it is Cause (deck) Typical conditions
Wheeze Continuous, musical/whistling; mainly expiratory (Hess: can be heard throughout the cycle) Narrowed/obstructed airways: smooth-muscle constriction, wall edema, mucus Asthma, COPD, bronchiectasis; Hess adds CHF and foreign-body aspiration (wheeze over one isolated area; Hess Ch 22, infants/children)
Rhonchi Deep, low-pitched, rumbling, more on expiration Secretions/mucus; may clear with coughing Thick secretions in larger airways. Hess adds: airway spasm or tumor
Stridor Crowing, high-pitched, inspiratory Upper-airway obstruction Croup, post-extubation laryngeal edema, tracheomalacia (Hess Ch 22, infants/children)

Think location: wheezing comes from the bronchi → bronchodilator; stridor comes from the upper airway (swelling) → racemic epinephrine (RCP 105, after bronchoscopy). In a severe asthma attack the wheeze becomes inspiratory and expiratory (biphasic).

RCP 203 Lssn 6b contrast: wheezes are high-pitched, musical, usually from narrowed small airways; rhonchi are low-pitched, snoring-like, usually from larger airways with secretions, and may clear or change after a cough.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2, slide 47 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 43 (notes) · RCP 103 Asthma, slides 20, 28 · RCP 103 COPD, slide 12 · RCP 103 Bronchiectasis · RCP 105 Procedures, slide 15 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion, slide 9 (notes) · Hess Ch 1 — Adventitious Breath Sounds · Hess Ch 22 · Hess Ch 62 glossary

5Pleural Friction Rub, Grunting and Voice Sounds

  • Pleural friction rub: cause = inflamed pleural surfaces that no longer glide smoothly (deck). Hess: a grating sound like two pieces of leather rubbing together, localized to the irritated area, may be intermittent. Seen with pleural effusion (occasionally), pulmonary embolism with infarction reaching the pleura, ILD, pleural infection and after thoracic surgery.
  • Grunting (Hess): newborns in respiratory distress close the glottis to maintain lung volume.
  • Voice sounds (Hess) get louder and clearer over consolidation, because consolidated tissue transmits sound better: bronchophony ("ninety-nine" clearly audible), egophony ("e" sounds like "a"), whispered pectoriloquy (whispered "1, 2, 3" clearly heard). In atelectasis, whispered pectoriloquy is diminished or absent (RCP 103).
Gap · still not coveredthe slide 47 normal/adventitious tables (whose "disease examples in the right column" the notes say to memorize) are images, and no source describes monophonic vs polyphonic wheezes — see the Ch 2 textbook pages 45–46 cited on the slide.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2, slide 47 (notes) · RCP 103 Pleura, slide 14 · RCP 103 Pulm Edema & PVD, slide 54 (and notes) · RCP 103 ILD, slide 41 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 43 (notes) · RCP 103 Atelectasis, slide 13 (notes) · Hess Ch 1 — Voice Sounds, Pleural Friction Rub, Grunting

6Breath Sounds by Condition

Condition Breath sounds Other clues
Asthma / COPD Wheezing, diminished sounds, prolonged expiration Hyperresonance
Consolidation / pneumonia Bronchial sounds, crackles, whispered pectoriloquy ↑ fremitus, dull percussion
Pneumothorax Absent Trachea away, hyperresonance
Pleural effusion Absent/diminished; occasionally a rub Trachea away, dull, ↓/absent fremitus
Atelectasis Absent/diminished (bronchial in some); crackles Trachea toward, dull
Upper-airway obstruction Stridor —
Pulmonary edema Crackles ("called rales"); wheezes. RCP 203 notes list grunting, gurgling or wheezing sounds among the symptoms —

Hess Table 1-3 adds emphysema (decreased or absent, no adventitious sounds) and pulmonary fibrosis (vesicular with crackles).

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2, slide 50 (notes) · RCP 103 Infectious, slide 26 · RCP 103 COPD, slide 12 · RCP 103 Asthma, slide 20 · RCP 103 Pleura, slide 14 · RCP 103 Atelectasis, slide 13 · RCP 103 Pulm Edema, slide 16 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 14 (notes) · Hess Ch 1 — Table 1-3
05

Orthopnea, Kussmaul Breathing, Cheyne-Stokes and Biot's Respirations

3 items · Orthopnea · Kussmaul · Cheyne-Stokes · Biot’s

1Orthopnea2Kussmaul vs Cheyne-Stokes vs Biot: pattern and cause3Why each pattern happens

1Orthopnea

Orthopnea is "a patient's sensation of uncomfortable breathing when lying down, which typically is relieved by sitting or standing up" (RCP 203). Like dyspnea, it is associated with a variety of cardiac and pulmonary disorders.

  • Cardiac cause: it is commonly linked to fluid overload or cardiac conditions (CHF, pulmonary edema). Lying flat increases venous return and redistributes fluid into the lungs. Hess Ch 40: less blood pools in the legs when supine, so blood shifts into the central circulation, filling pressures rise and interstitial edema forms.
  • Other conditions (Hess): severe COPD (from hyperinflation, without necessarily worse cardiac function), sleep apnea and other chronic respiratory diseases.
  • Bedside question: ask how many pillows the patient sleeps on. Hess notes that orthopnea may be hidden in a patient who sleeps on two or three pillows.

Related terms:

  • Paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea (PND): an "air hunger" or "drowning" feeling that wakes the patient 1 to 2 hours after falling asleep (RCP 203, pulmonary edema). RCP 103 says after several hours of sleep; Hess, several hours after lying down; use 1–2 hours on this exam. It commonly suggests cardiac dysfunction.
  • Platypnea (Hess): the opposite of orthopnea, difficulty breathing unless lying flat.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slide 10 (+ notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 14 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 17 (notes) · RCP 103 Pulmonary Edema & PVD, slide 15 (+ notes) · Hess Ch 1 — Breathing Patterns · Hess Ch 40 · Hess Ch 35 · Hess Ch 27 · Hess Ch 52

2Kussmaul vs Cheyne-Stokes vs Biot: pattern and cause

Pattern What it looks like Cause
Kussmaul Deep and fast, labored. Regular, with no apnea. The patient looks "air hungry" DKA (classic) or any severe metabolic acidosis (e.g., renal failure). It is compensatory: the patient blows off CO₂
Cheyne-Stokes Waxes and wanes with apnea: breaths get gradually deeper and faster, then shallower, then a brief apnea, and the cycle repeats (crescendo-decrescendo) CHF, stroke, brain injury/↑ ICP, central sleep apnea
Biot's Irregular breaths with equal tidal volumes, followed by periods of apnea CNS injury, ↑ ICP, brainstem damage. Hess adds meningitis
  • Hess on Biot's: a short burst of uniform, deep breaths, then apnea lasting 10 to 30 seconds.
  • Biot's vs Cheyne-Stokes: Biot's breaths are equal in size; Cheyne-Stokes breaths build and fade. Biot's points to brainstem involvement and more severe neurologic dysfunction.
  • Conflict: the RCP 103 deck describes Kussmaul as deep and fast, but Hess Ch 4 describes it as deep and labored with a normal or reduced frequency. Use the deck (Hess Ch 52 also says "rapid").
SourceRCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 25 (+ notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slide 21 (notes) · RCP 202 Lssn 3 — Renal A&P, slide 24 (notes) · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 40 · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slide 60 (notes) · Hess Ch 1 — Breathing Patterns · Hess Ch 4 · Hess Ch 52

3Why each pattern happens

Kussmaul. Hyperventilation is the body's compensation for metabolic acidosis: it expels CO₂, which is an acid when dissolved in blood. In the RCP 103 DKA case, the patient is "deep and fast (Kussmaul respiration), 24 breaths/min" with a very low PaCO₂, but the pH stays critically low (6.98), so the compensation is only partial.

Cheyne-Stokes. The cause is delayed communication between the lungs and the brain about CO₂. Because of the delayed circulation time (CHF, low cardiac output), the brain responds to CO₂ changes too late and keeps overcorrecting.

  • Hess Ch 45: apnea starts when PaCO₂ drops below the apneic threshold. CSB is seen in 25–40% of CHF patients. Treatment is aimed mainly at improving cardiac function.
  • RCP 103 Sleep Apnea: ASV helps this type of central sleep apnea.

Biot's. Comes from impaired central respiratory control: brainstem injury, ↑ ICP, or meningitis (Hess).

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 3 — Renal A&P, slide 24 (notes) · RCP 103 ABG Assessments, slide 36 (+ notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 25 (notes) · RCP 103 Pulmonary Edema & PVD, slide 15 (notes) · RCP 103 Sleep Apnea, slides 8, 36 (notes) · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slide 60 (notes) · Hess Ch 45 — Cheyne-Stokes Breathing
06

Volume, Flow and Pressure Measuring Devices; Spirometer Calibration

7 items · Vane and Wright respirometers · peak flowmeter · pressure manometer · capnometer · 3-L syringe and SD ranges

1Vane Respirometer: What It Measures and Its Errors2Wright Respirometer: Bedside Setup and Use3Peak Flowmeter4Pressure Manometer5Capnometer6The 3-L Syringe in PFTs: When and Why7Standard Deviation (SD) Accepted Ranges in QC

1Vane Respirometer: What It Measures and Its Errors

A mechanical "vane-type" respirometer (the Wright or Haloscale) measures gas volume: gas spins a rotating vane, and a gear mechanism turns the rotations into movement of hands on a watch-like dial. The on/off switch locks and unlocks the gears; Reset returns the hands to zero. Readings are unidirectional (always inlet → outlet).

  • Accuracy: within 2% of its recommended flow range.
  • Error: like any turbine, it overreads at high flows (the vane keeps spinning from momentum) and underreads at low flows (too little force to overcome vane and gear friction). Hess: the Wright is not linear at low flows because gas can enter the tube before the vanes rotate.
  • NBRC takeaway: it is easily damaged by flows outside its range, so never use it to measure forced flows or volumes. It measures volume at normal/controlled flows, not forced spirometry.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6, slide 95 (and notes) · Hess Ch 54 — rotating vane anemometer

2Wright Respirometer: Bedside Setup and Use

The Wright respirometer is the bedside device for measuring VT, f and V̇E on spontaneously breathing patients, attached to a one-way valve; it can also measure slow vital capacity (SVC) in neuromuscular disease.

  • Setup: a one-way breathing valve separates inspiratory and expiratory volumes; a HEPA filter protects patient and device. Normal use is expiratory volumes: attach it to the exhalation side of the valve.
  • Steps: switch ON during patient inhalation, have the patient do the maneuver (timed for minute volume), switch OFF when done. OFF freezes the reading; do not reset to 0 before reading.
  • Calculation: measure exhaled volume over 1 minute and count breaths, then VT = V̇E ÷ f. Deck example: V̇E 8 L/min, f 16/min → VT = 8 ÷ 16 = 0.5 L = 500 mL.
  • Troubleshooting: if it will not register, recheck the on/off switch and its position relative to the valve. If contaminated, gas sterilize and aerate it.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6, slides 96, 97 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3, slides 15, 18

3Peak Flowmeter

A peak flowmeter measures PEFR, the maximum flow generated on forced expiration (L/sec or L/min); it reflects the larger airways. Because PEFR is highly effort dependent, it is not used for diagnosis; it is a monitoring tool.

  • Uses: monitor bronchodilator effect (pre/post), assess asthma severity, detect early changes in asthma control; Hess: home monitoring of asthma.
  • Normals (deck notes): male 450–550 L/min, female 320–470 L/min.
  • % predicted = actual ÷ predicted × 100; < 80% predicted = expiratory flow obstruction. Example: 7 ÷ 10 L/sec = 70%. Always consider poor effort or technique when PEFR is well below normal.
  • Zones (personal best): green 80–100%, yellow 50–80%, red < 50% (RCP 103). Hess (NAEPP) gives green > 80%, yellow 50–79%; use the deck. Hess: personal best = highest of three successive readings when asymptomatic; use the same device routinely.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3, slide 14 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4, slide 71 · RCP 103 Asthma, slide 34 · Hess Ch 8 — Peak expiratory flow · Hess Ch 32 — peak flow meters

4Pressure Manometer

A manometer measures pressure. Hess: Pascal's law (pressure is transmitted without reduction through an enclosed static fluid) is the basis for manometric measurement. Bedside uses:

  • MIP/NIF: a manometer on a one-way valve that allows exhalation but not inspiration; flip the valve for MEP. Measures inspiratory/expiratory muscle strength; only in an alert, cooperative patient. Deck normal NIF > −60 cm H₂O; > −40 COPD; > −20 myasthenia gravis (magnitude notation: −60 or more negative). The more negative the NIF, the stronger the muscles (RCP 105 Weaning). Weaning deck: the negative pressure generated in 20 sec against an occluded device.
  • Cuff pressure: calibrated manometer + three-way stopcock + 10- or 20-mL syringe; goal 20–30 cm H₂O. Attaching the manometer causes volume loss and lowers cuff pressure, so always adjust, never just measure.
  • Manual ventilation: in children and infants, monitor airway pressure with a manometer.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3, slides 19 (and notes), 57, 58, 59 · RCP 105 Weaning, slides 15, 50 · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6, slide 59 · Hess Ch 54 — Key Points

5Capnometer

A capnometer (capnograph) is the device that measures exhaled CO₂ breath to breath. Capnometry = the number; capnography = number + waveform; capnogram = the waveform. Most measure CO₂ by infrared absorption (Hess). See section 24 for waveforms.

  • Setup: use a calibrated device; confirm an inspiratory baseline of PETCO₂ = 0 torr; PETCO₂ runs 1–5 torr less than PaCO₂ (more with high deadspace).
  • Mainstream: sensor at the airway, mainly intubated patients; heated (allow warm-up); placed distal to the airway → rebreathing error. On a non-intubated patient (mask/mouthpiece) it needs supplemental O₂ 6 L/min to prevent rebreathing from the added deadspace.
  • Sidestream: most useful for non-intubated patients (Ch 8 slide 38 text answers "Vented pts", but its notes and slide 42 say non-intubated); main problem = condensate occluding the sampling tube; slower response alters the waveform.
  • Calibration: 5% CO₂ (PCO₂ 38 torr) high point, standard ±0.3% (4.7–5.3%); auto-zero corrects offset only, not gain.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3, slide 10 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5, slide 22 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 5b — Ch 8, slides 38 (and notes), 39, 41, 42 · Hess Ch 2 — Capnometry

6The 3-L Syringe in PFTs: When and Why

Why: volume calibration of PFT equipment requires a 3.0-L calibration syringe: inject a known volume and confirm the spirometer reads it. When: daily, before patient testing (Hess Table 8-1; Box 8-3), after the daily leak check on volume spirometers (pressurize to 3 cm H₂O for 1 minute; acceptable leak < 10 mL).

Check Deck accepted range
Volume accuracy (ATS) ±3% or ±50 mL, whichever is larger → for 3 L, 2.91–3.09 L
Flow/linearity (inject 3 L at 0.5 s, 6 s and in between) Within 100 mL of 3.0 L
BTPS Must be OFF, or the reading is ~10% high

Low (< 2.91 L) → think leak: repeat the leak check. High (> 3.09 L) → think setup/correction: recheck zeroing, BTPS OFF, equal syringe/spirometer temperature. No patient testing until the cause is found and a repeat calibration is accurate. Record results on a daily QC log.

Caution · sources differConflict: Hess Box 8-4 allows ±3.5% (2.90–3.10 L; 3.5% of 3 L is exactly 105 mL, so 2.895–3.105 L). Use the deck's ±3% (2.91–3.09 L). Hess adds: check the syringe itself at least quarterly.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 5b — Ch 8, slides 26 (notes), 27, 28 · Hess Ch 8 — Table 8-1, Box 8-3, Box 8-4

7Standard Deviation (SD) Accepted Ranges in QC

QC results are plotted on a Levey-Jennings chart against the mean and SD; bounding limits are ±2 SD.

  • All controls within ±2 SD → analyzer "in control", ready for patients.
  • Outside ±2 SD → analytical error. RCP 103: one control out = warning; two or more consecutive out = out of control → recalibrate or replace the electrode.
  • Single random error (one point out, later points back to normal): evaluate it by the lab's QC rules; it does not necessarily mean a persistent problem. If repeat QC is acceptable and established QC criteria are satisfied, patient testing may continue; if the problem persists, stop patient testing and troubleshoot.
  • Systematic (bias) errors are more serious than random ones; with bias or recurrent random errors, no patient samples until corrected. Out of control: identify the problem, correct it and confirm control before reporting any patient results.
  • Hess adds: PFT biological controls (body box, DLCO) are tracked on a Levey-Jennings plot; outside 2 SD of the mean → corrective action.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 5b — Ch 8, slides 16, 17, 18 (and notes) · RCP 103 ABG, slides 66, 67 (and notes) · Hess Ch 8 — Plethysmograph and DLCO quality control
07

Tracheal Shift, Paradoxical Breathing, Flail Chest

4 items · Tracheal shifts and their causes · paradoxical breathing · flail chest · + what to recommend for a tension pneumothorax (Lssn 8b)

1Tracheal shift: which way, and what causes it2Paradoxical breathing3Flail chest4Tension Pneumothorax — What to Recommend, and in What Order

1Tracheal shift: which way, and what causes it

The deck's memory trick: "Toward loss / Away from pressure."

Shift Cause Conditions
Toward the affected side Decreased lung volume (volume loss pulls the trachea) Atelectasis, fibrosis, surgical resection
Away from the affected side A space-occupying process (pressure pushes the trachea) Large/tension pneumothorax, large pleural effusion, large mass

How to check it (RCP 103): put an index finger at the sternal notch and move it gently side to side. Normally the trachea is midline above the sternal notch.

Bedside patterns (RCP 203 notes):

  • Pneumothorax: trachea away + hyperresonance + absent breath sounds.
  • Pleural effusion: trachea away + ↓/absent fremitus + dullness + absent breath sounds.
  • Atelectasis: trachea toward + dullness + absent breath sounds.

Nuance: a tracheal shift with a pneumothorax "generally implies a large/tension pneumothorax, not every pneumothorax," and an effusion "needs to be substantial" to shift the trachea. The Trauma deck agrees: in pneumothorax, deviation away from the affected side is a late and unreliable finding, so don't wait for it (or a CXR) before treating a suspected tension pneumothorax in an unstable patient (hemodynamic instability).

Hess adds:

  • In a tension pneumothorax, the pressure may displace the mediastinum. The diagnosis is clinical (tracheal deviation, hyperresonance, neck vein distention), and treatment should not be delayed for imaging.
  • On a CXR, chest rotation can also make the trachea look deviated.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slides 39, 50 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 31 (+ notes) · RCP 103 Disorders of the Pleura & Chest Wall, slide 14 · RCP 103 Atelectasis, slide 9 · RCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slides 8 (notes), 9 (notes), 11 · Hess Ch 1 — Shape of the Chest · Hess Ch 7 · Hess Ch 41 — Pneumothorax

2Paradoxical breathing

Definition (RCP 203 notes): paradoxical breathing occurs when the chest wall or the abdominal wall moves in when taking a breath and moves out when exhaling, which is the opposite of normal. It is a sign of respiratory distress associated with damage to the structures involved in breathing. With dyspnea, tachypnea and accessory muscle use, thoracic-abdominal paradox ("see-saw") signals an abnormally high WOB.

What moves in on inspiration Meaning (Hess Ch 1)
Abdomen Diaphragm weakness or paralysis
Chest wall Paralysis of the chest wall muscles (high thoracic or low cervical spinal cord injury). Also a flail segment

Clinical uses:

  • Thoracoabdominal paradox means you should stop an SBT. Paradoxical abdominal movement is an early sign of weaning failure.
  • In spinal cord injury and severe neuromuscular disease, the chest moves inward during inhalation.
  • Hess Ch 44: paradoxical motion of the rib cage and abdomen may indicate impending respiratory failure or diaphragm weakness.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slide 11 (+ notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 60 · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation (Chang Ch 13, 16), slide 26 (notes) · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slides 49, 50, 68 · Hess Ch 1 — Flail Chest and Paradoxical Respiration · Hess Ch 44 · Hess Ch 62 glossary

3Flail chest

Definition (RCP 202 Trauma deck): a flail chest results from three or more ribs fractured in two or more locations, a sternal fracture, or a costochondral separation, creating an unstable chest wall segment. "Flail" refers to the paradoxical motion of that segment:

  • On inspiration, the segment is drawn in (negative intrapleural pressure) while the rest of the thorax expands.
  • On exhalation, the opposite occurs.
Why it mattersWhy it causes respiratory failure: pain → shallow breaths and a poor cough → atelectasis and retained secretions. Pulmonary contusions frequently accompany flail chest and may worsen over the first 24–48 hours (impaired gas exchange, V/Q mismatch). Treatment: pain management, pulmonary hygiene, oxygenation, and ventilatory support when necessary; not every flail patient needs intubation or MV.

Hess adds: contusion is almost pathognomonic once a flail segment is found and often "blossoms" at 48 hours, so elective intubation before respiratory failure should be considered early.

Other decks: a CMV indication (crushed/flail chest) and a contraindication to prone positioning.

Caution · sources differConflict: the Trauma deck says three or more ribs; Hess Ch 41 says two or more (Hess Ch 1: "multiple"). Use three or more (deck).
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slides 20 (+ notes), 30 · Hess Ch 41 — Pulmonary Contusion · Hess Ch 1 — Flail Chest and Paradoxical Respiration · RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slide 15 (+ notes) · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 66

4Tension Pneumothorax — What to Recommend, and in What Order

Simple versus tension, in one line from the deck: “A simple pneumothorax primarily compromises breathing; a tension pneumothorax compromises both breathing AND circulation.” The difference is not how much lung is down — it is the one-way valve effect: air enters the pleural space but cannot adequately escape, so pressure rises progressively, the mediastinum shifts, venous return falls, cardiac output drops, and the end point is obstructive shock and cardiac arrest.

Clinical signs of a tension pneumothorax

  • Sudden respiratory distress or increased work of breathing
  • Decreased chest expansion on the affected side
  • Decreased or absent breath sounds on the affected side
  • Tracheal deviation away from the affected side
  • Hyperresonant percussion note on the affected side
  • Absence of lung markings and radiolucency on the chest radiograph
  • On the ventilator: a sudden increase in airway pressure in volume control, or a decrease in VT in pressure control.

What to recommend

  1. A stat chest radiograph, and place the patient on 100% O₂.
  2. If the patient is on positive-pressure ventilation, change the settings to minimise peak inspiratory pressures — decrease PIP or lower VT.
  3. If tension is suspected and the situation appears life threatening: immediate needle decompression, or insertion of a chest tube, on the affected side.
Why 100% O₂ helps the gas go awayThis is nitrogen washout applied to the pleural space. Pleural gas is mostly nitrogen (~78%). Breathing 100% O₂ strips nitrogen out of the alveoli, which lowers the partial pressure of nitrogen on the blood side. Oxygen is absorbed by blood far faster than nitrogen, and because the patient is breathing pure O₂ the nitrogen is not being replenished. The net pressure of gas in the pleural space falls and the air is reabsorbed faster than it would be on room air. It is especially effective for small, uncomplicated pneumothoraces — and it is no substitute for decompression when the thing is under tension.
Exam anchor — the NBRC pearl the deck prints verbatim“Tracheal deviation is not required to diagnose tension pneumothorax. It is a late and unreliable clinical finding.” Recognise the combination of respiratory distress, unilateral diminished breath sounds, and hemodynamic instability rather than waiting for tracheal deviation — or for the chest film. Item 1 of this section makes the same point from the other direction: deviation is away from a tension pneumothorax, but it arrives late.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 8b — Ch 13 Ensure Modifications Are Made to the Respiratory Care Plan, 65 slides (added 2026-10-25), slides 13, 14 and speaker notes
08

ETT Size by Height/Weight

3 items · ETT size by height and/or weight

1Adult ETT Size2Children: Age Formula and Length (Height)-Based Tape3Neonates: Size by Weight or Gestational Age

Still not covered: no deck or Hess chapter gives an adult ETT size by height or weight (adult size is by sex only, including RCP 203 Lssn 6a), and no source gives the Broselow tape's color-to-size values. The RCP 203 Lssn 4 airway selection tables (slides 80–83) and the Lssn 6a slide 31 sizing table have no table text — check the slides.

1Adult ETT Size

Adults are sized by sex, not by height or weight. RCP 203 Lssn 6a (use on this exam): women 7.0–8.0 mm ID, men 8.0–9.0 mm ID; RCP 105 and Hess give the narrower 7–7.5 mm (women) and 8–8.5 mm (men). ETT size is the internal diameter (ID) in mm; tubes come in sizes 2 to 10. Oral depth at the teeth (deck): women 19–21 cm, men 21–23 cm, which are initial estimates, not confirmation; Hess secures at 21 cm (women) / 23 cm (men) until the chest x-ray. On that film the tip should sit 4–6 cm above the carina. Have at least one size larger and one size smaller available.

Why bigger is better: use an ETT of size 8 or larger when the patient's size allows; an ETT raises the work of breathing by 54% to 240%, depending on tube size and flow. A tube that is too small is a cause of high cuff pressure. Trap: the deck sizes the LMA by weight and the King LT by height, not the ETT.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slides 24 (notes), 25 (notes), 31 (+ notes), 33, 34 · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slides 34, 42 (notes), 86 · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy, slide 28 · RCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slide 57 · Hess Ch 17 — Endotracheal intubation

2Children: Age Formula and Length (Height)-Based Tape

RCP 203 Lssn 6a: uncuffed ID ≈ (age ÷ 4) + 4; cuffed ID ≈ (age ÷ 4) + 3.5 (cuffed is a half size smaller). Deck example, a 4-year-old: 5.0 mm uncuffed, 4.5 mm cuffed. Hess Ch 22 gives the same uncuffed formula for children 1 to 10 years, and insertion depth from the lips = age + 10 (cm). For height, the Broselow Tape is a color-coded, length-based tape that recommends emergency equipment, including airways; length-based tapes are useful for children weighing 35 kg or less.

Age Uncuffed (Age/4 + 4) Cuffed (Age/4 + 3.5) Depth (age + 10)
2 y 4.5 mm 4.0 mm 12 cm
4 y 5.0 mm 4.5 mm 14 cm
8 y 6.0 mm 5.5 mm 18 cm

(The 4-year-old sizes are the deck's; the other rows are our calculations.) Trap: "[Age/4] + 4" in the RCP 105 Initiation of MV deck is the normal P(A–a)O₂ estimate, not an ETT size.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slide 31 (notes) · Hess Ch 22 — Endotracheal intubation (neonatal/pediatric) · RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 7

3Neonates: Size by Weight or Gestational Age

No deck gives a neonatal size table; the table below is from Hess Ch 22. Estimate the tube size and depth from the infant's weight; if the weight isn't known, use gestational age.

Weight (g) ETT (mm ID) Gestational age (wk) ETT (mm ID)
< 1000 2.5 < 30 2.5
1000–2000 3.0 30–35 3.0
2000–3000 3.5 > 35 3.5
> 3000 3.5–4

Depth from the lips = weight (kg) + 6 cm (e.g., 1 kg → 7 cm, our calculation), then confirm by bilateral auscultation and chest x-ray.

Cuffed or not (RCP 203 Lssn 6a): uncuffed tubes for premature infants or < 3 kg; otherwise both cuffed and uncuffed are acceptable in infants and children, and cuffed tubes are now routinely used. Hess: neonatal tubes are uncuffed (the cricoid acts as a functional cuff); pediatric tubes are sometimes cuffed. Child cuff pressure: deck usually no more than 20–30 cm H₂O, Hess < 20 cm H₂O; use the deck.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slide 31 (+ notes) · Hess Ch 22 — Tables 22-8, 22-9 and Endotracheal intubation · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slide 22 (notes)
09

Obstructive vs Central Sleep Apnea

4 items · Obstructive vs central sleep apnea

1OSA vs CSA: the core difference2Risk factors and symptoms3Diagnosis: PSG and AHI4Treatment: OSA vs CSA

1OSA vs CSA: the core difference

The deck's one-line rule: effort but no airflow = obstructive; no effort + no airflow = central. An apnea that starts central and ends obstructive = mixed.

Obstructive (OSA) Central (CSA)
What happens Recurring collapse of the upper airway during sleep. Airflow is absent or nearly absent Repetitive stopping or reduction of airflow AND ventilatory effort. The airway is open, but the brain does not send signals to breathe
Effort on PSG Ventilatory effort persists. Esophageal pressure becomes more negative (increased effort) No effort: no thoracoabdominal movement, no esophageal pressure swing
Mechanism Sleep reduces muscle tone. The genioglossus fails to oppose collapsing forces, so the tongue moves into the oropharynx. Hess: narrowing is worst in REM sleep and the supine position Hyperventilation-related (most common: primary CSA, Cheyne-Stokes in heart failure, high altitude). Hypoventilation-related: CNS disorders, opioids/alcohol/benzodiazepines, neuromuscular disease, severe COPD

Mixed apnea is an event, not a separate condition. Patients with predominantly mixed apnea are classified and treated as OSA.

Hess adds: CSA is uncommon, usually occurs in patients with cardiac or neurologic problems, and these patients are usually not obese.

SourceRCP 103 Sleep Apnea, slides 5–11 (+ notes) · Hess Ch 45 — Respiratory Recap; Pathogenesis; Central Sleep Apnea

2Risk factors and symptoms

OSA risk factors (deck):

  • Obesity, which the notes call the "strongest predictor"
  • Large neck: > 17 inches in men, > 16 inches in women
  • Male sex, age > 65
  • Hypertension, diabetes, chronic nasal congestion, family history
  • Alcohol, sedatives, smoking, ethnicity

Hess Table 45-2 adds that most OSA patients are obese (BMI > 30).

OSA symptoms: loud snoring, witnessed apneas, abrupt awakenings with shortness of breath, morning headache, dry mouth, excessive daytime sleepiness, and mood or memory changes.

CSA:

  • Its main causes are heart failure (Cheyne-Stokes), CNS disorders or injury, drug or substance use, and high altitude. Atrial fibrillation is often associated, so evaluate for cardiac disease and brainstem/cortex lesions.
  • Hess: CSA patients have milder symptoms, and snoring and daytime sleepiness are less common than in OSA.
SourceRCP 103 Sleep Apnea, slides 8, 20, 23–25 (+ notes) · Hess Ch 45 — Table 45-2; Central Sleep Apnea

3Diagnosis: PSG and AHI

Full-night, attended, in-lab polysomnography (PSG) is the gold standard.

AHI = (apneas + hypopneas) ÷ total sleep time in hours. Deck example: 100 events ÷ 8 h = 12.5/hr (checked), which is mild.

Severity AHI (events/hr)
Mild 5–15
Moderate 15–30
Severe > 30, or SpO₂ < 90% for > 20% of sleep

OSA is confirmed when:

  • AHI ≥ 15/hr in an asymptomatic patient, or AHI ≥ 5/hr with symptoms (events = apneas, hypopneas or respiratory-effort-related arousals), and
  • 75% of events are obstructive.

CSA is diagnosed when the majority of events are central, with no airflow and no thoracoabdominal movement. Hess sets this at more than 50% central.

A hypopnea is airflow ↓ ≥ 30% for ≥ 10 s with a ≥ 3% SpO₂ drop or arousal (Hess Table 45-1: airflow reduced by 30% with desaturation of at least 4%; use the deck's 3% or arousal).

SourceRCP 103 Sleep Apnea, slides 6, 14, 18, 19 (+ notes), 20 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 15 (notes) · Hess Ch 45 — Table 45-1; Central Sleep Apnea

4Treatment: OSA vs CSA

OSA CSA
First step Behavior modification: weight loss, avoid alcohol and sedatives, avoid supine sleeping Treat the underlying cause first (e.g., CHF, encephalitis)
PAP First-line. CPAP (most common and effective; constant pressure splints the airway open). BiPAP for CPAP intolerance or hypoventilation, or APAP. RCP 203: set CPAP by prescription, or use auto-CPAP (max 15 cm H₂O); optimum control = AHI < 5/hr. If CPAP isn't tolerated: other masks, humidification, ramp, auto-titration, pressure relief (C-flex) or BiPAP If CSA persists: CPAP/BiPAP, initial CPAP +5 to 10 cm H₂O. ASV (adjusts pressure support breath by breath) for hyperventilation-related CSA. RCP 203 lists sleep apnea, "especially central sleep apnea," as a BiPAP indication
Other Oral appliances, surgery, implantable upper-airway stimulator (Inspire) Respiratory stimulants: acetazolamide, theophylline. O₂ reduces desaturation but doesn't improve sleep quality

Hess adds:

  • The OSA CPAP pressure is usually 8–10 cm H₂O.
  • The S/T (spontaneous/timed) bilevel mode supplies a backup rate if the patient is apneic (RCP 203: set 2–5 breaths below the patient's spontaneous rate).
  • Conflict: the deck says ASV is particularly helpful for hyperventilation-related CSA (e.g., Cheyne-Stokes in heart failure, slide 36 notes); Hess Ch 45 says ASV in Cheyne-Stokes has been associated with harm and the AASM says to stop ASV for CSA in symptomatic HF with LVEF ≤ 45%. Use the deck for "which CSA type does ASV treat"; know the Hess caveat.
SourceRCP 103 Sleep Apnea, slides 35, 36, 37 (+ notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 15, 49, 56 · Hess Ch 45 — Treatment (CPAP, BPAP, ASV) · Hess Ch 21 — S/T mode
10

Heliox

4 items · Heliox concentrations and delivery methods · + oxyhood, hypothermia, and nebulizer flows (Lssn 8a)

1Heliox Concentrations2Methods of Delivering Heliox3Flowmeter Correction Factor (worked example)4Heliox in Infants, and Heliox Driving a Nebulizer

1Heliox Concentrations

Most centers use premixed cylinders in one of three combinations: 80% He/20% O₂, 70% He/30% O₂, 60% He/40% O₂. Always use helium with at least 20% O₂. Tanks of 100% helium exist, but they need a blending system; if the O₂ source failed, the patient could get 100% He and quickly suffer anoxia, which is why premixed cylinders are used. Heliox decreases the work of breathing, especially in large airway obstruction or severe asthma, because helium is much less dense than nitrogen, so it flows more easily through narrowed airways. It is not a replacement for O₂ therapy in hypoxemic patients.

Hess adds: the most popular mixtures are 80/20 and 70/30; if an FIO₂ > 0.40 is needed, the limited helium is unlikely to produce a clinical benefit. Hess Ch 32 uses 60% to 80% helium for asthma.

Caution · sources differWhere the sources differ. The deck lists three mixtures (80/20, 70/30, 60/40); its notes and Hess name 80/20 and 70/30 as typical. Use the deck's three.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide 92 (and notes) · Hess Ch 14 — Helium–Oxygen Therapy · Hess Ch 32 — Heliox

2Methods of Delivering Heliox

Patient Deck method
Spontaneously breathing Tight-fitting nonrebreathing mask at a flow that meets inspiratory demand, or a high-flow cannula
Mechanically ventilated With cuffed artificial airways or by the noninvasive route
Via a flowmeter Use a flowmeter calibrated for that He%, or apply a correction factor (item 3)

The slide 92 notes also list face masks, nasal cannulas and ventilators. Monitor every patient closely with an O₂ analyzer with active alarms measuring the FiO₂ continuously.

Hess adds: a well-fitting simple mask or a mask with a reservoir bag, with a Y-piece for aerosolized drugs; flow often 12 to 15 L/min keeps the bag inflated. A tightly sealed closed system is required because helium leaks through small holes. When heliox powers a nebulizer, raise the flow 50% to 100%. On a ventilator, helium can alter the delivered and measured exhaled tidal volume.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 92 (and notes), 93 · Hess Ch 14 — Helium–Oxygen Therapy

3Flowmeter Correction Factor (worked example)

An O₂ flowmeter under-reads heliox flow, so multiply the indicated flow by the factor to get the actual flow.

Mixture Correction factor
80/20 1.8
70/30 1.6
60/40 1.4

Deck example: 10 L/min indicated on an O₂ flowmeter delivering 80/20 → 10 × 1.8 = 18 L/min actual heliox flow. Reverse it to find the setting: for 15 L/min of 80/20, set 15 ÷ 1.8 = 8.3 L/min. The more helium, the larger the factor and the lower the FiO₂.

Hess adds: Hess gives only the 80/20 factor, 1.8 (Graham's law: 80/20 heliox diffuses 1.8 times faster than O₂, which is why its flow through an O₂ flowmeter is 1.8 times the indicated flow). Hess's 1.805 and 1.586 are density ratios, not flowmeter factors.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide 93 · Hess Ch 14 — Helium–Oxygen Therapy

4Heliox in Infants, and Heliox Driving a Nebulizer

Delivery, by patient type

  • Spontaneously breathing: premixed cylinders via a tight-fitting nonrebreathing mask, at a flow sufficient to meet or exceed the patient’s minute ventilation and peak inspiratory flow requirements — or via a high-flow nasal cannula.
  • Invasively ventilated: through a cuffed tracheal airway, using a heliox-capable ventilator.
  • Infants with bronchiolitis: heliox can be given via an oxyhood — but see the caution below.
Caution — why the oxyhood fell out of favourThe heat loss and hypothermia associated with helium’s high thermal conductivity has limited oxyhood use in this population, with preference now being given to a high-flow nasal cannula. Helium conducts heat away from the infant far better than air does, so a gas that is therapeutically ideal for the airway is thermally hostile to a small patient in a hood. If a stem offers “heliox by oxyhood” for an infant, the better answer is usually HFNC.

Heliox plus a bronchodilator — and the flow correction that goes with it

  • Heliox has been combined with jet-nebulized bronchodilator therapy to treat severe acute obstructive disorders such as status asthmaticus, especially when standard therapy fails. Nebulisation with heliox may improve the delivery and deposition of aerosolised drugs.
  • Flows must be increased by 50% to 100% when using heliox to power a drug nebulizer.
  • Why: heliox’s low density reduces nebulizer efficiency at lower flow rates, which decreases the inhaled dose. Raising the flow compensates, increasing the inhaled mass of medication and the output of respirable particles.
Exam anchorTwo different heliox corrections exist in this guide and they are easy to mix up. Item 3 is the flowmeter correction factor — an oxygen flowmeter under-reads heliox, so you multiply the set flow to get the actual flow. This one is different: it is a deliberate 50–100% increase in driving flow to make a nebulizer work properly. One corrects a reading; the other changes a setting. And in both cases, monitor every patient with an O₂ analyzer with active alarms.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 8a — Ch 12 Administer Medications and Specialty Gases, 32 slides (added 2026-10-25), slides 25, 26 and speaker notes
11

Bronchoscopy: Diagnostic vs Therapeutic; Medications Used for the Procedure

2 items · Diagnostic vs therapeutic bronchoscopy · medications for the procedure

1Diagnostic vs Therapeutic Bronchoscopy: the Difference2Medications Used for the Procedure

1Diagnostic vs Therapeutic Bronchoscopy: the Difference

Diagnostic bronchoscopy answers "What's wrong?" = Look: it is done to gather additional information or to confirm a preliminary diagnosis, and biopsy and cytology samples are obtained. Therapeutic bronchoscopy answers "How can we fix it?" = Treat: it is done to treat airway problems rather than simply diagnose them. The RCP 203 notes: "Diagnostics relate to finding a suspected disease, while Therapeutics relate to treatment."

Diagnostic (RCP 203 Ch 5, slide 12) Therapeutic (RCP 203 Ch 5, slide 13)
Lesions of unknown etiology on chest x-ray Facilitating ETT insertion in difficult intubations
Recurrent atelectasis or pulmonary infiltrates Selective intubation of a mainstem bronchus
Patency of the upper airway; source of hemoptysis Clearing mucus plugs causing lobar or segmental atelectasis
Unexplained cough, localized/unilateral wheeze, or stridor; suspicious sputum cytology Removing abnormal endobronchial tissue or foreign bodies
Obtaining secretions, cell washings, or biopsies; injury from toxic inhalation or aspiration Placing and assessing an airway stent
Problems with artificial airways (tube placement, tracheal damage) Airway balloon dilatation for tracheobronchial stenosis

NBRC tip (RCP 105; the RCP 202 deck gives the same split, adding airway dilation and stents): diagnostic = biopsy, BAL, washing, airway inspection; therapeutic = remove mucus plugs, remove foreign bodies, control bleeding, relieve airway obstruction. Therapeutic bronchoscopy is most commonly used for central airway obstruction (RCP 202). If asked which procedure is both, fiberoptic bronchoscopy is "an excellent answer."

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5 Recommend Diagnostics, slides 12, 13 (and notes) · RCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slides 4 (notes), 22 · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 3, 5 (notes), 6 (notes)

2Medications Used for the Procedure

The goal: a patient who is comfortable, coughs less, has fewer secretions, and tolerates the scope, but is not completely asleep. Many hospitals now use midazolam, propofol or fentanyl instead of diazepam (RCP 105), and the RCP 202 deck says to focus on these three and the reversal agents:

Drug Effect Reversal
Midazolam (Versed), a benzodiazepine Sedation, anxiolysis, amnesia; no analgesia Flumazenil (Romazicon). It can precipitate seizures in susceptible patients, so it isn't routinely given just to speed recovery
Fentanyl, an opioid Analgesia. Combined with midazolam, the risk of respiratory depression rises Naloxone (Narcan)
Propofol (Diprivan), neither opioid nor benzo Rapid sedation; respiratory depression and hypotension; no analgesia None

Premedications: lidocaine, a local anesthetic that suppresses cough (RCP 105: 5–10 mL of 1% to 4% via aerosol, or down the working channel starting at the vocal cords); atropine to dry secretions and reduce vagal response and bronchospasm (RCP 105: IM), though many centers have abandoned it (RCP 202 notes, Hess); morphine IM for cough and pain; diazepam or another benzo IV for sedation; antibiotics if prescribed. RCP 203 key equipment lists lidocaine, lidocaine-epinephrine, acetylcysteine and thrombin.

Bleeding: epinephrine or thrombin, or iced saline, all via the working channel. RCP 105 gives topical 1:1000 epinephrine; thrombin "helps blood clot."

Hess differs: lidocaine as 2–3 mL aliquots of 1–2% (total ≤ 5–7 mg/kg), and 20 mL of 1:20,000 epinephrine if iced-saline boluses fail; use the deck on this exam.

Gap · still not covereda dose or concentration for thrombin or acetylcysteine given through the scope — RCP 105 Ch 14, RCP 203 Ch 6, Hess Ch 9.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slides 9 (notes), 10 (notes), 17 (+ notes) · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 6 (notes), 8 (and notes), 14 · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide 118 · Hess Ch 9 — Patient Sedation; Bleeding Complications
12

Rapid Response Team (RRT) Activation Criteria

3 items · Vital signs that trigger a Rapid Response Team call · + terminating therapy for an adverse response (Lssn 8b)

1Vital signs and findings that trigger a Rapid Response Team call2What the RRT is and who is on it3When to Stop a Therapy — the Triple-S Rule

1Vital signs and findings that trigger a Rapid Response Team call

No deck in the vault covers this. The answer below is from Hess Ch 28, Table 28-5, which gives the model criteria; each hospital's own policy governs. Any staff member may call the team if one of the following is met:

Trigger Call the RRT if
Heart rate > 140 beats/min or < 40 beats/min
Respiratory rate > 28 breaths/min or < 8 breaths/min
Systolic BP > 180 mm Hg or < 90 mm Hg
SpO₂ < 89% despite oxygen supplementation
Mental status Acute change
Urine output < 50 mL over 4 hours
Other Staff member has significant concern about the patient's condition; chest pain unrelieved by nitroglycerin; airway patency; seizure; symptoms of stroke
Exam anchorExam traps:
  • Both the high and the low value trigger a call.
  • The SpO₂ trigger applies despite O₂.
  • Staff concern alone is a valid trigger, even with normal vital signs.
Gap · still not coveredhow the call is placed "overhead" (paging procedure), and any temperature, pediatric or early-warning-score (MEWS/NEWS) triggers — no deck or Hess section gives them; check the hospital's RRT policy.
SourceHess Ch 28 — Rapid Response Teams, Table 28-5
