2What the RRT is and who is on it

Hess Ch 28: rapid response teams (also called medical emergency teams) intervene during the critical pre-arrest period, when patients show clinical warning signs of pending demise. The goal is to prevent transfer to the ICU, cardiac arrest, or death. At some hospitals, patients or family may also activate the team.

Team (Table 28-5): a critical care nurse, a respiratory therapist, and a physician (critical care specialist) as backup. Hess calls the RT "a valuable member of the rapid response team."

Highest-risk patients for an RRT call:

  • Postoperative patients, in the first 12 to 24 hours (analgesic-related events). Risk is higher with preoperative opioid use, heart disease, hemodynamic instability and OSA risk.
  • Patients transferred out of the ICU within the past 24 hours.
  • Nighttime activation is associated with increased mortality.
SourceHess Ch 28 — Rapid Response Teams, Table 28-5 · Hess Ch 18

3When to Stop a Therapy — the Triple-S Rule

A rapid response call is the escalation. This is the step before it: the Ch 13 deck opens with termination of therapy based on an adverse response, and it is the single most testable “what do you do first” content in the chapter.

Two reasons to stop or recommend discontinuing a therapy

  • The patient’s safety or well-being appears to be in jeopardy, or
  • the goals and objectives of the therapy have been achieved.

The adverse effects common to many procedures — patients should be monitored before, during and after every treatment

  • Patient complaint of dyspnea, pain, or dizziness
  • Marked changes in vital signs, above or below the initial baseline
  • Development or worsening of a cardiac arrhythmia
  • Cyanosis, pallor, or diaphoresis
  • A greater than 4% drop in SpO₂, or any fall below 90%
  • Severe paroxysmal coughing
  • Any change in level of consciousness or responsiveness
  • Nausea or vomiting
  • Development or worsening of wheezing or stridor
  • A marked decrease in the intensity of breath sounds
  • Hemoptysis
Exam anchor — the triple-S ruleIf a serious adverse effect is observed during therapy, the deck says always follow Stop, Stay, Stabilize: Stop the therapy. Stay with and monitor the patient. Stabilize the patient by providing oxygen therapy, calling for assistance if necessary. Then notify the nurse and the physician — and if the adverse effect appears life threatening, call either a MET / rapid response team or a Code Blue, which is where items 1 and 2 of this section pick up. The ordering is the point: you stop the therapy and stay at the bedside before you go looking for anyone.
Why it mattersBeyond these general effects, the RT must know the adverse effects specific to each therapy administered — the deck’s own example is an increased heart rate from β1 (cardiac) medications. A 4% SpO₂ drop during a treatment is not a number to chart and move past; on this list it is a reason to stop.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 8b — Ch 13 Ensure Modifications Are Made to the Respiratory Care Plan, 65 slides (added 2026-10-25), slides 6, 7, 8
13

PFTs: Lung Volumes and Capacities, Bronchoprovocation, Lung-Volume Measurement, Obstructive vs Restrictive

6 items · All lung volumes and capacities · bronchoprovocation and methacholine · lung-volume measurement · obstructive vs restrictive

1All Lung Volumes and Capacities2Spirometry Values: FVC, FEV1, FEV1/FVC, FEF25–75%, PEFR3Pre/Post Bronchodilator Response4Lung-Volume Measurement (FRC → RV, TLC)5Bronchoprovocation and the Methacholine Challenge6Obstructive vs Restrictive Disease

1All Lung Volumes and Capacities

There are four volumes; every capacity is made up of two or more volumes.

Term Definition (Hess Ch 8) Normal (deck)
VT (tidal volume) Gas inhaled or exhaled in normal breathing 0.5 L
IRV Maximum gas inspired from the end of a normal inspiration 3.1 L
ERV Maximum gas expired from the end of a resting expiration 1.2 L
RV Gas left after a maximal expiration; cannot be exhaled 1.2 L
IC = VT + IRV Maximum inspiration from the resting end-expiratory position No slide prints it (3.1 + 0.5 = 3.6 L)
FRC = RV + ERV Gas left at the end of a resting expiration 2.4 L
VC = VT + IRV + ERV Maximum exhaled after a maximal inspiration 4.8 L (~50 mL/kg IBW)
TLC (= RV + VC = FRC + IC) Gas in the lungs at the end of a maximal inspiration 6.0 L

FRC is the key static volume: a spirometer can measure IC and ERV, but FRC must be measured separately (item 4); then RV = FRC − ERV and TLC = RV + VC (or FRC + IC). The Ch 3 notes give a typical adult VC of 3–5 L and a critical minimum of ~1 L (15 mL/kg).

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4, slide 77 · RCP 105 Weaning, slide 11 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3, slides 18 (notes), 75 · Hess Ch 8 — Lung Volumes and Capacities

2Spirometry Values: FVC, FEV1, FEV1/FVC, FEF25–75%, PEFR

Value What it is Deck cutoff
FVC Total air rapidly exhaled from TLC, for ≥ 6 seconds (Hess; Ch 8 deck: traditionally < 6 s in adults = early termination, not a universal ATS/ERS minimum now) Normal ≥ 80% predicted
FEV1 Volume exhaled in the first second of the FVC Normal ≥ 80% predicted
FEV1/FVC (FEV1%) % of the FVC exhaled in the first second Normal ≥ 70–80% (varies with age); < 70% = obstructive
FEF25–75% Average flow over the middle half of the FVC; small airways ≤ 70% predicted = small-airway obstruction
PEFR Highest flow during the FVC < 80% predicted = expiratory flow obstruction

% predicted = actual ÷ predicted × 100 (deck: PEFR 7 ÷ 10 L/sec = 70%). A low FEF25–75% can show early small-airway obstruction even when FEV1 is normal. Quality: at least three error-free maneuvers (no slow/false start, i.e. back-extrapolated volume ≥ 5% of FVC or 150 mL; no cough, breathing or early termination); the two largest FVC and the two largest FEV1, judged separately, within 150 mL.

Hess adds: normal FEV1/FVC is 0.75–0.85, declining after age 30; Hess says the "80% of predicted rule" should be abandoned in favor of the LLN (5th percentile, Z = −1.64). Use the deck's 80% / 70% cutoffs on this exam.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4, slides 71, 75, 79 · RCP 203 Lssn 5b — Ch 8, slide 29 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3, slide 20 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6, slide 98 (notes) · Hess Ch 8 — Spirometry; Interpretation

3Pre/Post Bronchodilator Response

When obstruction is suspected, repeat spirometry at the drug's peak response time, usually 15–30 minutes after the bronchodilator.

% change = (post − pre) ÷ pre × 100. Deck example: FEV1 3.0 L → 3.3 L: (3.3 − 3.0) ÷ 3.0 = 0.3 ÷ 3.0 = 0.10 × 100 = 10%, so not reversible.

Reversible (as in asthma) = FEV1 improves ≥ 12–15% and by ≥ 200 mL in adults (RCP 203). The RCP 103 Asthma deck says ≥ 12% and/or ≥ 200 mL (children: 12% of predicted); Hess says 12% (minimum 200 mL) in FEV1 or FVC and a 10–15 minute wait after albuterol. Use the RCP 203 slide: 12–15% and 200 mL.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4, slide 76 (and notes) · RCP 103 Asthma, slide 23 (and notes) · Hess Ch 8 — Bronchodilator Response

4Lung-Volume Measurement (FRC → RV, TLC)

Method How it works (Hess) Key points
Nitrogen washout (open circuit) Breathe 100% O₂; measure the N₂ washed out of the FRC (room air ~79% N₂) Ends when exhaled N₂ < 1.5% for 3 breaths; usually < 5 min, stop at 7 min (deck notes: ~7 min in a healthy person). A rise in N₂ = leak
Helium dilution (closed circuit) Rebreathe a known volume with ~10% He; FRC is calculated from how much the He is diluted CO₂ absorbed (sodium or barium hydroxide); leak → falsely high FRC
Body plethysmography (body box) Shutter closes at FRC; patient pants; Boyle's law gives thoracic gas volume (TGV) Fast; measures all gas, including trapped gas and bullae
Exam anchorTrap: He dilution and N₂ washout measure only gas communicating with the airways, so they can underestimate FRC in obstruction. Normally FRC = TGV; TGV > FRC = trapped gas (bullous emphysema, air trapping).
Gap · still not coveredthe N₂-washout, He-dilution and body-box equations (Hess Equations 8-1 to 8-3) are images in the text; only their variables are extractable.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3, slide 75 · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6, slide 32 (notes) · Hess Ch 8 — Nitrogen Washout, Helium Dilution, Body Plethysmography

5Bronchoprovocation and the Methacholine Challenge

Bronchoprovocation (bronchial challenge) deliberately provokes bronchospasm in patients with airway hyperresponsiveness. It is used when spirometry is nondiagnostic but asthma is suspected; mild asthma often has normal spirometry between episodes (Hess). Hess's three common tests: methacholine, mannitol, exercise.

  • Methacholine (a direct-acting cholinergic / parasympathomimetic): inhaled in rising doses with spirometry after each. Positive = FEV1 falls ≥ 20% = hyperresponsiveness consistent with asthma (deck). Hess: keep stepping up while FEV1 stays > 80% of baseline; report the PD20 (dose causing a 20% fall); give albuterol afterward.
  • Mannitol (Hess): positive if FEV1 falls ≥ 15% from baseline or ≥ 10% between doses.
  • Exercise (Hess): ~8 min hard exercise; 10% fall in FEV1 = positive.
  • Contraindicated in moderate to severe obstruction; no bronchodilators or exercise on test day; reschedule 4–6 weeks after a chest infection (Hess).
Gap · still not covereda PC20 (concentration) cutoff for a positive methacholine test; no source gives one.
SourceRCP 103 Asthma, slide 23 (and notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 5, slide 18 (and notes) · Hess Ch 8 — Bronchial Challenge Testing; Methacholine Challenge

6Obstructive vs Restrictive Disease

Obstructive (↓ airflow) Restrictive (↓ lung volume)
FEV1/FVC < 70% (deck) Normal or ↑
FVC, FEV1 FEV1 ↓; FVC normal or ↓ FVC ↓; FEV1 normal or ↓
FEF25–75% ↓ (≤ 70% = small airways) Normal or ↓
TLC ↑ with hyperinflation ↓ (< 80% predicted)
RV, RV/TLC ↑ = air trapping All volumes ↓; RV/TLC normal
DLCO ↓ emphysema; normal/↑ asthma, chronic bronchitis ↓ ILD; normal = chest-wall cause (Hess)
Examples Asthma, COPD, bronchiectasis ILD, atelectasis, pleural effusion, Guillain-Barré

Deck cutoffs: < 70% for FEV1%, < 80% predicted for VC, TLC and DLCO; all three ↓ = combined. Spirometry only suggests restriction; a low TLC confirms it (Hess). DLCO normal: 25–30 (Ch 1) or 25 ± 5 mL/min/mm Hg (Ch 3).

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4, slides 75, 79 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3, slide 76 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1, slide 20 · RCP 203 Lssn 5b — Ch 8, slide 29 (notes) · RCP 103 COPD, ILD, Bronchiectasis, Atelectasis, Pleura, Neuro decks · Hess Ch 8 — Box 8-7, Respiratory Recap
14

Artificial Airways and the Transtracheal O₂ Catheter

5 items · Transtracheal catheter · OPA · NPA · LMA · tracheostomy · trach button

1Transtracheal Catheter2Oropharyngeal Airway (OPA)3Nasopharyngeal Airway (NPA)4Laryngeal Mask Airway (LMA)5Tracheostomy and Tracheostomy Button

1Transtracheal Catheter

No deck in the vault covers this; the answer below is from Hess Ch 25. Transtracheal oxygen uses a catheter placed during a surgical procedure to bypass the upper airway: it goes through a small hole in the front of the neck into the trachea. It is used for O₂ conservation: the patient gets the equivalent of continuous-flow O₂ by nasal breathing at a lower continuous-flow setting, because the upper-airway dead space is bypassed. Hess lists it with the O₂-conserving devices (reservoir cannulas, intermittent-flow, pulse-flow, demand delivery) and classes it as a low-flow (variable FIO₂) device. Its other benefit is better adherence, since the catheter can be hidden from view, unlike a nasal cannula.

Trap: Hess's emergency needle cricothyrotomy (a 12- to 16-gauge over-the-needle catheter through the cricothyroid membrane, attached to an O₂/ventilation source) is a different device; Hess does not call it "transtracheal."

SourceHess Ch 25 — O₂-conserving devices (transtracheal oxygen); Respiratory Recap · Hess Ch 14 — low-flow O₂ devices · Hess Ch 17 — Cricothyrotomy · Hess Ch 62 — Glossary

2Oropharyngeal Airway (OPA)

An OPA keeps the tongue off the posterior pharynx. Use it only in an unconscious patient without an intact gag reflex; in a conscious/semi-conscious patient with a gag reflex it causes gagging, vomiting and aspiration (consider an NPA). It also serves as a bite block, may make BVM ventilation more effective, and does not enter the larynx or esophagus. Types: Berman (rigid throughout) and Guedel (reinforced in the bite region).

Size (RCP 203): corner of the mouth to the angle of the jaw, by anatomy, not age alone. Hess sizes it on the cheek, tip no farther than the pinna of the ear; use the deck. Too large may press the epiglottis against the larynx (RCP 105) or traumatize the airway (RCP 203); too small may push the tongue back. Adult insertion: curve up, advance to the base of the tongue, rotate 180° (or insert from the side and rotate 90°). Infants and small children: no 180° rotation; RCP 203 says depress the tongue with a tongue depressor and insert directly (RCP 105: lateral insertion is often preferred). Don't tape over the center opening (suction catheter path).

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slides 14 (+ notes), 15 · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slides 3, 4, 5 (notes), 7 (and notes) · Hess Ch 17 — Oropharyngeal Airways

3Nasopharyngeal Airway (NPA)

The NPA is the alternative to the OPA: usable in conscious or semi-conscious patients with an intact gag reflex, and better tolerated because it doesn't stimulate the posterior pharynx. Its tip rests behind the tongue just above the epiglottis. Uses: bypass an upper airway obstruction, aid passage of a bronchoscope, and serve as a path for nasotracheal suctioning (less trauma from repeated suctioning). Deck "Stop and Think": a patient who does not arouse but does cough, no intubation equipment → NPA.

Size (RCP 203): nares to earlobe (or tragus); choose the largest diameter that passes the inferior meatus without force. Hess measures nose tip to the ear meatus, or to the tragus plus 2 cm; use the deck. Insertion: lubricate with water-soluble jelly, advance along the floor of the nose, bevel toward the septum, never force it (try the other naris, then a smaller airway). Major contraindication: suspected basilar skull fracture or significant midface trauma. Complications: laryngospasm, coughing, nosebleeds, and sinus infection with prolonged use.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slides 15 (+ notes), 16, 17 (+ notes) · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slides 10 (and notes), 12, 14 (notes) · Hess Ch 17 — Nasopharyngeal Airways

4Laryngeal Mask Airway (LMA)

The LMA is a supraglottic airway: a tube and mask with an inflatable cuff, inserted blindly into the pharynx. The mask seals around the laryngeal inlet above the glottis and does not enter the trachea. Uses: routine airway management during general anesthesia and a rescue device in the difficult or emergency airway; it ventilates better than a BVM with less gastric insufflation.

Point RCP 203 deck
Size By weight: size 4 = 50–70 kg, size 5 = 70–100 kg
Cuff ≤ 60 cm H₂O (an ETT is 20–30)
PPV Squeeze the bag slowly; keep pressures below 20–30 cm H₂O
Leak Check position; don't just add cuff air
Fixation Bite block; no OPA (causes malposition)
Regurgitation Don't remove it: head-down or side-lying, disconnect ventilation, suction, prepare to intubate

Main limit: less aspiration protection than a cuffed ETT. Hess sizes by build (3 and 4 small/average adults, 5 large adults); use the deck's weights.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slides 25 (+ notes), 28, 29, 30 (+ notes), 31 (notes) · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slides 45, 55, 56–57 (notes) · Hess Ch 17 — Extraglottic Airways

5Tracheostomy and Tracheostomy Button

A tracheostomy is the surgical introduction of a tube into the trachea through the neck (tracheotomy = the procedure; tracheostomy = the opening). Three main reasons: bypass an upper airway obstruction, aid removal of secretions, long-term mechanical ventilation (probably the most common). It bypasses the upper airway, so warming, humidification and filtration are lost. RCP 203: tubes are sized by ID, but fit depends on the OD: no more than ⅔ to ¾ of the tracheal ID. Remove the obturator immediately after insertion and keep it at the bedside; keep a spare inner cannula there too. A speaking valve or a capped fenestrated tube needs the cuff fully deflated: never cap with the cuff up.

A tracheostomy button maintains the stoma without a full tube: a short cannula with a flared outer end, flanged inner end and spacers for depth. Plugged, the patient breathes and coughs via the upper airway; a one-way valve lets them talk, eat and cough. PPV through it leaks: it is not a secure cuffed airway (Hess: no cuff).

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slides 35 (+ notes), 41, 44, 45, 46 (+ notes) · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slides 65 (and notes), 67, 80, 101 · Hess Ch 17 — Tracheostomy; Trach and Stoma Buttons
15

Pulmonary Hygiene: Percussion, IPPB, Vibratory PEP

4 items · Manual and mechanical percussion · IPPB · vibratory PEP

1Manual Percussion, Vibration and Postural Drainage (CPT)2Mechanical Percussion3PEP and Vibratory (Oscillatory) PEP4IPPB

1Manual Percussion, Vibration and Postural Drainage (CPT)

CPT / PDPV = postural drainage, percussion and vibration to loosen and clear secretions. Postural drainage uses gravity: the affected segment is placed "up" so mucus moves to the larger airways. Each position is held 5–10 min (deck and Hess); sessions run 20–40 min, before meals or 2 h after (Hess: > 1 h after; use the deck). Percussion = rapid thumps with cupped hands over the segment; vibration = rapid shaking of the chest wall during expiration.

When to start and stop: sputum > 30 mL/day indicates airway clearance; stop PDPV when sputum drops below 30 mL/day and the patient has an effective cough (Hess: < 25 mL/day does not justify PD). CF patients are generally no longer placed head-down (reflux risk). Deck contraindications: unstable head injury, recent spinal surgery, uncontrolled hypertension, certain cardiac conditions. Hess adds: no head-down with gross hemoptysis; percussion can worsen burns, open wounds, skin grafts, subcutaneous emphysema or a recent pacemaker.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion, slides 5 (notes), 8 (notes) · RCP 103 Atelectasis, slide 18 · RCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slide 12 · Hess Ch 16 — Conventional Chest Physiotherapy; Box 16-3

2Mechanical Percussion

RCP 203 Lssn 6b: handheld percussors and vibrators (pneumatic or electrical) give a consistent rate and impact force with no user fatigue, and the deck calls them equally effective as cupped hands. Hess: more convenient for the caregiver, but data are lacking that they improve clearance; use the deck.

  • HFCWO (vest): an inflatable vest + air-pulse generator making rapid positive-pressure bursts, adjustable 5–20 Hz (deck); used mainly for CF and bronchiectasis, especially home care without caregiver support. Hess: the vest presses 5 to 20 cm H₂O on the chest, makes "mini-coughs" and runs at 2 to 25 Hz.
  • IPV: high-flow mini-bursts with aerosol at 300 to 400 pulses/min; the deck says IPV replaced IPPB (topic 25).
  • Metaneb (RCP 203): CPEP (lung expansion) + CHFO (secretion clearance) with aerosol, about 10 min alternating modes; can run in-line with a ventilator.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion, slides 8 (notes), 15 (+ notes), 19 · RCP 103 Atelectasis, slide 20 (notes) · Hess Ch 16 — Conventional Chest Physiotherapy; High-Frequency Chest Wall Compression; Intrapulmonary Percussive Ventilation

3PEP and Vibratory (Oscillatory) PEP

RCP 203 Lssn 6b: PEP devices typically make 6–25 cm H₂O during exhalation with unrestricted inspiration; PEP also helps prevent or treat atelectasis and reduces air trapping in asthma and COPD. To mobilize secretions, a vibratory PEP device is probably the best choice.

Technique (deck): sit upright or semi-Fowler's; start at the lowest PEP and raise it to 10–20 cm H₂O; do sets of 10–20 slow, moderately deep breaths with short holds and active (not forced) exhalation, with a directed cough after each set; repeat 4–8 times, session ≤ 20 min. Hess says 4–6 times, 10–20 min; use the deck. Contraindications: can't tolerate ↑ WOB, hemodynamic instability, bullous emphysema, high ICP, untreated pneumothorax, sinusitis, epistaxis, middle-ear problems.

Device How it vibrates
Flutter Steel ball; 10–25 cm H₂O (deck; Hess ≈ 10); PEP rises with device angle and flow; gravity-dependent (Hess)
Acapella Counterweighted lever/plug and magnet; knob clockwise ↑ PEP; works independently of gravity (Hess)
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion, slides 15 (+ notes), 17, 18 · Hess Ch 16 — Positive Expiratory Pressure; Oscillatory (or Vibratory) Positive Expiratory Pressure

4IPPB

IPPB (intermittent positive pressure breathing) is short-term or episodic mechanical ventilation to assist ventilation and give short-duration hyperinflation. The deck says it was used for lung expansion but has been removed from NBRC exams, is no longer recommended, and has been replaced by IPV.

Hess adds (how): a pneumatically driven, pressure-triggered, pressure-cycled ventilator. The patient triggers it and it passively inflates the lungs. Set flow for an I:E of about 1:3, aim for breaths at least 25% larger than the patient's VT, and run no more than 20 minutes. When: atelectasis, a cough too weak to work, neuromuscular disease or kyphoscoliosis, and only after other lung-expansion options are exhausted. It should not be used for aerosol therapy.

Caution · sources differWhere the sources differ. Hess still gives IPPB indications; the deck says it is off the NBRC exam and replaced by IPV. Use the deck.
SourceRCP 103 Atelectasis, slides 17, 20 (notes) · Hess Ch 16 — Intermittent Positive Pressure Breathing; Box 16-9 · Hess Ch 15
16

CVP and PA Catheters, Normal Pressures, Diuretics

5 items · CVP and PA catheters · normal CVP, RAP, PCWP, CI, CO · diuretics

1CVP Catheter2PA (Swan-Ganz) Catheter3Normal Ranges: CVP, RAP, PACP/PCWP, CO, CI4Diuretic Classes5The Role of Diuretics

Still not covered: the RCP 203 Chptr 16 PowerPoint is not in the vault. This section answers from RCP 105 Hemodynamic Monitoring, RCP 104 Pharm Ch 19 and Hess Ch 3 and 57.

1CVP Catheter

A central venous (CVP) catheter goes in through the subclavian or internal jugular vein. Its tip sits in the superior vena cava near the right atrium, and the pressure there is right atrial pressure (RAP), commonly called CVP. Use: it measures right-heart filling pressure (RV preload) and helps judge fluid status and right heart function, but it is often late to reflect left-heart changes. The line can also give mixed venous samples and meds/fluids. A low CVP suggests hypovolemia; a high CVP suggests fluid overload. Venous return = MAP − CVP, so a higher CVP (PPV, RV failure) lowers venous return and cardiac output.

Reading it: level the transducer at the phlebostatic axis (4th intercostal space, midaxillary line) and read at end-expiration. Do not take the patient off PEEP/CPAP; if PEEP is > 10 cm H₂O, use corrected pressure = measured pressure − [0.5 × (PEEP ÷ 1.36)].

Hess adds: adult catheters are usually 7 French, 16 to 20 cm. A normal or high CVP correlates poorly with volume status.

SourceRCP 105 Hemodynamic Monitoring (Chang Ch 10), slides 12 (and notes), 13, 14, 26 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 12 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 33 · Hess Ch 3 — Central Venous Pressure Monitoring

2PA (Swan-Ganz) Catheter

The pulmonary artery (Swan-Ganz) catheter is flow-directed and balloon-tipped. It measures CVP, PA pressure, PCWP, cardiac output and mixed venous O₂ saturation (SvO₂). It is usually 110 cm long with three lumens. The proximal port, about 30 cm from the tip, sits in the right atrium; the distal port at the tip sits in the PA. The balloon takes 1.5 mL at most.

Path: subclavian or IJ → SVC → RA → balloon inflated → RV → PA → wedges in a smaller PA branch → balloon deflated.

Readings: PAP is read with the balloon deflated (normal 15–25 / 6–12 mm Hg; Hess Normal Values page: 15–30 / 4–12, mean 9–16; use the deck). PCWP is read at end-expiration with the balloon inflated momentarily, then deflated immediately. PCWP reflects left atrial pressure / LV preload: > 18 mm Hg = cardiogenic pulmonary edema; ARDS is normal or low. Thermodilution CO: 10 mL of iced or room-temperature fluid goes into the proximal port, and the tip thermistor senses the temperature change.

Hess adds: PA catheters have fallen out of favor in the ICU. They are now kept for left heart failure, pulmonary hypertension and CO measurement.

SourceRCP 105 Hemodynamic Monitoring (Chang Ch 10), slides 16, 17 (notes), 18, 19, 21 (and notes), 25 (notes) · Hess Ch 3 — Pulmonary Artery Catheters · Hess — Normal Values (front matter)

3Normal Ranges: CVP, RAP, PACP/PCWP, CO, CI

Value RCP 105 deck (use this) Other sources
CVP 0–6 mm Hg (vena cava) 2–6 (RCP 103 Clinical Data); 0–8 (Hess Table 3-1)
Right atrial pressure 2–7 mm Hg, slightly higher than CVP 0–7 (Hess text); averages < 5 (RCP 104 Ch 21 notes)
PCWP ("PACP") 8–12 mm Hg (slide 23) 6–12, "some sources 4–12" (slide 21 notes); 2–12 (Hess PAWP); mean 8–10 (RCP 104 Ch 21 notes)
PA pressure (if "PACP" = PAP) 15–25 / 6–12 mm Hg (slide 19) 15–30 / 4–12, mean 9–16 (Hess Normal Values page)
Cardiac output 4–8 L/min; RCP 203 gives the same range (CO = SV × HR) Hess Table 3-1: varies with size of patient; Hess Normal Values page: 5–8 L/min
Cardiac index (CO ÷ BSA) 2.5–3.5 L/min/m² Hess: 2.5–3.5 (same)

"PACP" is not a term any source uses. The decks say PCWP (pulmonary capillary wedge pressure, "also called pulmonary artery wedge pressure"). Hess says PA occlusion pressure or PA (pulmonary capillary) wedge pressure (PAWP). PACP is most likely this wedge/occlusion pressure; if your instructor means PA pressure, use the PAP row. Where the sources differ: the ranges above differ slightly by source; use the RCP 105 slide values.

SourceRCP 105 Hemodynamic Monitoring (Chang Ch 10), slides 14 (and notes), 19, 21 (and notes), 23, 25 · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 12 (notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 21 — Vasopressors, Inotropes, slide 10 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 41 (notes) · Hess Ch 3 — Table 3-1; Pulmonary Artery Catheters · Hess — Normal Values (front matter)

4Diuretic Classes

All diuretics block Na⁺ reabsorption; water follows salt, and the nephron site sets the strength and electrolyte effects. Deck memory trick: "Loops for Lungs, Thiazides for BP, K-sparing for Potassium." Osmotic diuretics and CAIs are low yield.

Class Site Examples Main use Key adverse effect
Loop (most potent) Ascending loop of Henle Furosemide (Lasix), bumetanide, torsemide, ethacrynic acid Drug of choice for pulmonary edema; CHF, HTN Hypokalemia, metabolic alkalosis, volume depletion; ototoxicity
Thiazide Distal tubule Hydrochlorothiazide, chlorthalidone, metolazone (Hess) First-line mild HTN; chronic CHF; not for acute pulmonary edema Hypokalemia; raise glucose
K-sparing Distal tubule / collecting duct Spironolactone, eplerenone; triamterene, amiloride Protect K⁺ (often with loops); cirrhosis and ascites Hyperkalemia
Osmotic Proximal tubule and loop Mannitol TBI with cerebral edema, ARF Can worsen pulmonary edema and CHF
CAI Proximal tubule Acetazolamide Glaucoma, metabolic alkalosis, altitude sickness Metabolic acidosis
SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 19 — Diuretics, slides 4, 17, 18 (notes), 20, 21, 23 (and notes), 26 (and notes), 28, 29, 31, 33 (notes) · Hess Ch 57 — Table 57-9

5The Role of Diuretics

Diuretics raise urine output to reduce extracellular fluid volume, which lowers BP or removes excess fluid. The RT tie-in: less fluid → less pulmonary edema → better oxygenation → lower work of breathing. In cardiogenic pulmonary edema the goal is preload reduction, and loop diuretics (furosemide) are the cornerstone. IV loop diuretics cause vasodilation within 5 minutes, lowering PCWP, BP and SVR before urine output rises. In ARDS a drop in PCWP is linked to better survival, but diuretics are not the main treatment. In preterm CLD, routine or sustained use is not recommended. Mannitol should not be given for diuresis, because it first raises plasma volume. On the ventilator, non-potassium-sparing diuretics cause K⁺ loss and metabolic alkalosis; for diuretic-induced alkalosis, stop or reduce the diuretic and give chloride and potassium. Top 3 adverse effects: hypovolemia, hypokalemia, metabolic alkalosis.

Hess adds: diuretics mainly treat hypertension and edema from CHF or renal insufficiency.

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 19 — Diuretics, slides 4 (and notes), 17 (notes), 25, 31 (notes), 35 (and notes) · RCP 103 Pulmonary Edema & PVD, slide 31 (notes) · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 27 (notes), 47 (notes), 54 · Hess Ch 57 — Diuretics
17

C(a-v)O₂

2 items · C(a-v)O2: how to calculate and normal range

1How to Calculate C(a-v)O₂, With a Worked Example2What Widens or Narrows It

Hess's Normal Values page (front matter) lists the normal C(a–v̄)O₂ as 5 mL/dL (venous blood gases, with PvO₂ 35–45 and SvO₂ 65–75%), which matches the worked example below (20 − 15 = 5). No range is given, and the RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 36 table is an image.

1How to Calculate C(a-v)O₂, With a Worked Example

C(a-v)O₂ is the difference between arterial and mixed venous O₂ content, which reflects the O₂ used by the tissues: C(a-v)O₂ = CaO₂ − CvO₂.

  • CaO₂ = (1.34 × Hb × SaO₂) + (0.003 × PaO₂), in mL O₂/100 mL blood. RCP 203 Lssn 7 gives a CaO₂ range of 16–22 mL O₂/dL and prints the dissolved factor as 0.0031 (same quantity; use 0.003 in arithmetic, as RCP 203 Lssn 3a and Hess do).
  • CvO₂ = (Hb × 1.34 × SvO₂) + (PvO₂ × 0.003), using a mixed venous sample from the pulmonary artery.
Hess's resting example Hb-bound Dissolved Content
CaO₂: Hb 15, SaO₂ 97%, PaO₂ 100 15 × 1.34 × 0.97 = 19.497 100 × 0.003 = 0.3 19.797 ≈ 20
CvO₂: Hb 15, SvO₂ 75%, PvO₂ 40 15 × 1.34 × 0.75 = 15.075 40 × 0.003 = 0.12 15.195 ≈ 15

C(a-v)O₂ = 20 − 15 = 5 mL/100 mL (4.6 unrounded). This is our subtraction from Hess's numbers. Hess's text prints the dissolved part as "0.03", but 100 × 0.003 = 0.3, and its own total of 19.797 uses 0.3. The difference feeds Fick: V̇O₂ = (CaO₂ − CvO₂) × Q̇ = 5 × 10 × 5 L/min ≈ 250 mL/min, and tissues extract about 25% of the O₂ delivered at rest.

SourceHess — Normal Values (front matter) · Hess Ch 50 — Oxygen content, mixed venous O₂, O₂ consumption · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 15 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 41 (notes) · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy, slide 14

2What Widens or Narrows It

From Hess Ch 50: O₂ extraction rises (CvO₂ falls) with exercise, congestive heart failure and severe anemia, and falls (CvO₂ rises) when cardiac output rises greatly, e.g., sepsis. Hess does not state the effect on C(a-v)O₂ itself; since C(a-v)O₂ = CaO₂ − CvO₂, a lower CvO₂ at an unchanged CaO₂ widens it (our inference). PvO₂ < 35 mm Hg in a critically ill patient suggests increased extraction and tissue hypoxia. Normal mixed venous values are PvO₂ 35–45 mm Hg and SvO₂ 65–75% (Hess). The RCP 105 Hemodynamic deck gives SvO₂ 75% (68–77%), so use the deck's range.

The RCP 103 disease decks: a large pleural effusion → C(a-v)O₂ ↑ (tissues extract more). In ARDS, ILD, lung cancer, pulmonary embolism and severe OSA, C(a-v)O₂ stays normal, even where the O₂ extraction ratio rises. Trap: don't call it widened in those diseases.

SourceHess Ch 50 — O₂ consumption, mixed venous O₂ · Hess Ch 3 · RCP 105 Hemodynamic Monitoring (Chang Ch 10), slide 32 · RCP 103 Disorders of the Pleura & Chest Wall, slide 18 · RCP 103 Diffuse Alveolar Disease — ARDS, slide 20 · RCP 103 ILD, slide 47 · RCP 103 Lung Cancer, slide 29 · RCP 103 Pulmonary Edema & PVD (pulmonary embolism section), slide 56 · RCP 103 Sleep Apnea, slide 30
18

Partial Rebreathing Mask, Nonrebreathing Mask, Aerosol Mask, Air-Entrainment Mask

3 items · Partial NRB · NRB · aerosol mask · air-entrainment mask

1The Four Masks Side by Side2Partial Rebreathing vs Nonrebreathing Mask: The Valve Difference3Air-Entrainment Mask and Aerosol Mask: How the FiO₂ and Flow Are Set

1The Four Masks Side by Side

The deck's teaching line: low-flow = variable FiO₂ (depends on the patient's breathing); high-flow = fixed FiO₂ (flow meets or exceeds the patient's inspiratory flow). The deck lists partial/non-rebreather masks as low-flow and the Venturi (air-entrainment) mask and air-entrainment nebulizer as high-flow. No deck slide gives FiO₂ or flow numbers for the reservoir masks or the aerosol mask; those below are Hess Ch 14.

Partial rebreathing mask Nonrebreathing mask (NRB) Aerosol mask (on a large-volume nebulizer) Air-entrainment (Venturi) mask
Flow type Low-flow Low-flow High-flow (deck); Hess: only at FiO₂ ≤ 0.4 High-flow
FiO₂ 0.40–0.70 0.6–0.8 (text); recap 0.60–0.90 ~0.3–1.0 (nebulizer collar) Precise, set by jet/port; limited flow above 0.4
O₂ flow 8–15 L/min 10–15 L/min (> 12 often needed) Typically 15 L/min input Minimum jet flow printed on the device
Reservoir/valves 300–600 mL bag, no valves Same bag plus one-way valves (bag-to-mask and exhalation port) No bag; open system via 22-mm corrugated tubing; visible aerosol Jet nozzle + entrainment ports
Key check Bag stays at least partly inflated Bag must not empty by more than half / fully collapse Mist must not disappear on inspiration Ports must stay uncovered; total flow ≥ 40 L/min

Use the NRB text range 0.6–0.8 (Hess's own table also says 60–80% in reality, because the mask does not seal).

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6, slides 8 (and notes), 10, 13, 21 · Hess Ch 14 — Low-Flow Systems, Reservoir Systems, Large-Volume Nebulizers, Air-Entrainment Masks, Respiratory Recap; Hess Ch 62 — Table 14 device descriptions

2Partial Rebreathing vs Nonrebreathing Mask: The Valve Difference

Both are a simple mask with a 300- to 600-mL reservoir bag. The partial rebreathing mask has no valves, so the patient breathes a mixture of source O₂, bag O₂ and some room air through the side ports. The NRB adds one-way valves between the bag and mask and on at least one exhalation port. On inspiration the exhalation valves close and the bag valve opens, so the patient draws 300–500 mL of O₂ from the bag. Removing the valves turns an NRB into a partial rebreather.

  • Flow rule: set the flow high enough that the bag never collapses (NRB: not emptying by more than half).
  • Safety: if the valves stick or O₂ fails, the patient can suffocate; some masks have a spring-loaded antisuffocation valve.
  • Uses: NRB for patients who need a high FiO₂ with a fairly normal breathing pattern (trauma, MI, CO exposure) (Hess); the RCP 105 Airway deck uses an NRB to preoxygenate before intubation.
SourceRCP 105 Airway, slide 36 · Hess Ch 14 — Partial Rebreathing Mask, Nonrebreathing Mask; Hess Ch 62 — Table 14 description

3Air-Entrainment Mask and Aerosol Mask: How the FiO₂ and Flow Are Set

An air-entrainment mask has a jet nozzle that speeds 100% O₂, which entrains room air through side ports. Deck rules:

  • Bigger ports / smaller jets → more air, lower FiO₂, higher total flow; smaller ports / bigger jets → higher FiO₂, lower flow.
  • Blocking the ports (hands, sheets) → higher FiO₂, lower total flow.
  • Changing the O₂ L/min does not change the FiO₂, only the total flow.
  • Total flow = (air parts + O₂ parts) × O₂ flow. 40% = 3:1 → 4 × 10 L/min = 40 L/min; the same patient on 5 L/min = 20 L/min. 60% = 1:1 → 2 × 10 = 20 L/min. Aim for ≥ 40 L/min.

The aerosol mask gets its gas from an air-entrainment large-volume nebulizer, which follows the same rules. If the mist disappears from the mask during inspiration, room air is diluting the gas: the "high-flow" system has become low-flow with a variable FiO₂. Hess adds: the nebulizer counts as high-flow only at FiO₂ ≤ 0.4; at 0.6 its total flow is only 30 L/min (15 L/min input).

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6, slides 12, 13, 14, 16 (notes) · Hess Ch 14 — Air-Entrainment Masks, Large-Volume Nebulizers
19

V̇/Q̇ Mismatch vs Shunt

4 items · V/Q mismatch vs shunt · + good lung down, and the three exceptions (Lssn 8b)

1V/Q Mismatch vs Shunt (vs Dead Space): The Differences2Telling Them Apart: the 100% O₂ Test and the Shunt Equation3RCP 203 Bedside Rules: When It's Shunt, and What to Do4Good Lung Down — Positioning as a V̇/Q̇ Intervention

1V/Q Mismatch vs Shunt (vs Dead Space): The Differences

V/Q mismatch means ventilation and perfusion are both present but not matched. A shunt is perfusion without ventilation: blood passes alveoli that get no air and "never sees oxygen." Dead space is the opposite, ventilation without perfusion. In the deck's words: "Dead space is wasted air. Shunt is wasted blood."

V/Q mismatch (low or high V/Q) Shunt Dead space
V/Q (Hess; normal 0.8) low (↓; ventilation reduced, e.g., airflow obstruction) or high (↑; perfusion reduced, e.g., PE) 0 infinity (↑ V/Q)
Causes (deck) COPD, asthma, PNA, pulmonary edema (low V/Q); PE (high V/Q); the deck's slide 19 list atelectasis, PNA, ARDS, pulmonary edema PE (most common board answer), shock, cardiac arrest, excessive PEEP
Response to O₂ usually improves with O₂ responds poorly → refractory hypoxemia —
Fix O₂ recruitment (PEEP, CPAP), not just more O₂ —

V/Q mismatch is the most common cause of hypoxemia, a huge NBRC favorite. The mechanism least responsive to O₂ is intrapulmonary shunt. Hess adds that high-V/Q units don't cause hypoxemia; they hinder CO₂ removal instead. Trap: PE is high V/Q (dead space), not shunt.

SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 16, 19, 21, 22 (notes) · Hess Ch 50 — Ventilation-perfusion ratio, Shunt, Mechanisms of hypoxemia · RCP 103 Pulmonary Edema & PVD, slide 52 (notes)

2Telling Them Apart: the 100% O₂ Test and the Shunt Equation

Test (Hess): have the patient breathe 100% O₂, then draw an ABG. Low-V/Q units raise their PaO₂ maximally, but shunt units don't. Normal adults reach PaO₂ > 600 mm Hg; if PaO₂ only reaches 250 mm Hg, the shunt is about 25% of cardiac output.

Shunt equation: Qs/Qt = (CcO₂ − CaO₂) ÷ (CcO₂ − CvO₂). The deck's example (FiO₂ 1.0, PaCO₂ 40, PaO₂ 60, SaO₂ 90%, PvO₂ 40, SvO₂ 75%, Hb 15):

  • PAO₂ = 1.0 × (760 − 47) − 40/0.8 = 663 mm Hg
  • CcO₂ = 15 × 1.34 × 1.0 + 663 × 0.003 = 22.1; CaO₂ = 18.3; CvO₂ = 15.2 mL/dL
  • Qs/Qt = 3.8 ÷ 6.9 = 0.55 = 55%, a severe shunt (ARDS, large atelectasis). The slide prints "3.8 ≈ 0.55" without showing the ÷ 6.9.

Normal shunt: the RCP 203 deck says < 5% (>20–30% significant in the critically ill). The RCP 105 Weaning deck says ≤ 10% normal, 10–20% mild, 20–30% significant, > 30% critical. Use < 5% (RCP 203). For weaning, Qs/Qt must be < 20%.

SourceHess Ch 50 — Shunt · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy, slides 13, 14 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 35 (notes) · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 23

3RCP 203 Bedside Rules: When It's Shunt, and What to Do

  • Significant shunt (RCP 203) generally exists when P/F < 200, or you can't keep satisfactory oxygenation on ≥ 50% O₂ (PaO₂ ≤ 50 on FiO₂ ≥ 50%). O₂ alone is insufficient; treatment is normally CPAP or PEEP. In refractory hypoxemia, proning decreases shunt by re-expanding the dorsal lung and improving V/Q matching.
A-a gradient (PAO₂ − PaO₂) Means Do
5–15 mm Hg on room air Normal —
66–300 mm Hg V/Q mismatch O₂ up to 60%
> 300 mm Hg Shunting Ventilator with PEEP

RCP 105 Weaning gives the same bands on room air. On 100% O₂, RCP 105 Initiation calls > 450 severe hypoxemia or shunting. PAO₂ at FiO₂ 0.21 / 0.50 / 1.0 ≈ 100 / 306 / 663 mm Hg (PaCO₂ 40).

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 6 (+ notes), 30 (notes) · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy, slide 13 · RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 7

4Good Lung Down — Positioning as a V̇/Q̇ Intervention

The core principle. When a patient lies on their side, gravity pulls more blood flow to the lung that is positioned downward — the dependent lung. By placing the good (healthy) lung down, you match perfusion with the area that also has the best ventilation, which improves V̇/Q̇ matching and therefore oxygenation.

How it works, in two steps

  1. Gravity’s effect on perfusion: in the lateral position the lower (dependent) lung receives more blood flow; the upper (nondependent) lung receives less.
  2. Ventilation distribution: in healthy lungs, ventilation is also somewhat better in the dependent areas, because the diaphragm pushes downward more effectively on that side. So both ventilation and perfusion favour the dependent lung — which is exactly where you want the healthy tissue.
Exam anchor — the worked example the deck givesA patient has pneumonia in the left lung. Best position: right lateral decubitus — right (good) lung down. Most of the blood and air then go to the right lung, which is the one actually capable of oxygenating. Read the stem carefully: the side named is usually the diseased side, and the answer is the opposite decubitus.
Caution — three times the good lung goes UPThe rule reverses when the danger is spilling something into the healthy lung or over-inflating it:
• Lung abscess — good lung up, to prevent drainage of infected material into the healthy lung.
• Unilateral pulmonary interstitial emphysema (PIE) in infants — good lung up, to prevent overdistention of the healthy lung.
• Massive hemoptysis or bleeding — good lung up, to avoid blood flowing into the good lung.
In all three the goal shifts from optimising gas exchange to protecting the one good lung you have left. That shift is what the question is testing.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 8b — Ch 13 Ensure Modifications Are Made to the Respiratory Care Plan, 65 slides (added 2026-10-25), slide 29 and speaker notes
