Mechanical Ventilation

32 items · Modes and asynchrony · variables · PEEP and auto-PEEP · high and low pressures · trigger and cycle · circuits and valves · compliance · VE vs VA · humidity and rain-out · cuff pressures · equation of motion · + I:E computation, ATC, permissive hypercapnia floor (Lssn 8b)

1How to Tell Any Two Modes Apart2VC vs PC: the Difference and When to Use Each3CMV (and How It Differs from Assist/Control)4IMV vs SIMV5Pressure Support (the Spontaneous Breath in SIMV)6MMV (Mandatory/Minimum Minute Ventilation)7PRVC (Pressure-Regulated Volume Control)8VV+ (Volume Ventilation Plus, PB 840)9PAV (Proportional Assist Ventilation)10APRV (Airway Pressure Release Ventilation)11HFOV (High-Frequency Oscillatory Ventilation)12All the Listed Modes Side by Side13Patient–Ventilator Asynchrony: What It Is and Trigger Asynchrony14Flow and Cycle Asynchrony15Mode Asynchrony and the Fix Sequence16Ventilator Variables: Pressure, Volume, Flow and Time17Independent vs Dependent Variables18Trigger: What Starts the Breath19Cycle: What Ends the Breath20PEEP: What It Is, What It Does, How Much21PEEP Complications and the Dangerous Level22Auto-PEEP23High Pressures and Their Causes24Low Pressures and Their Causes25Ventilator Circuits26Exhalation Valves27Static and Dynamic Compliance: Calculation and Normal Ranges28Minute Ventilation vs Alveolar Ventilation29Relative Humidity and Rain-Out30Cuff Pressures31The Equation of Motion32I:E Ratio — Computing It, Changing It, and the Flow That Follows

1How to Tell Any Two Modes Apart

Mode names are manufacturer-dependent: "Learn how the mode works – not just the name!" (Servo = PRVC; Dräger = AC/VC with AutoFlow; PB840 = VC+). Ask three questions: what is controlled (volume or pressure)? what starts and ends the breath (trigger, cycle)? which breath types occur (CMV, IMV, CSV)?

  • Golden rule: "Control" only one item; the other is the "Variable" (Hess: "can control only one variable at a time — either pressure or volume").
  • Deck ranking: VC = 1st choice, PC = 2nd, PS = "help" (SIMV, SBT).
  • Spontaneous = patient triggers and cycles; mandatory = machine triggers or cycles. So patient-triggered ≠ spontaneous.
  • Breath sequences: CMV (all mandatory), IMV (mandatory + spontaneous), CSV (all spontaneous, e.g. CPAP, PS).
  • Open-loop delivers what you set (VC); closed-loop adjusts to the patient (PSV, MMV, ASV, PAV, volume-assured PS, PRVC, NAVA).
  • NBRC (Ch 11): control (aka independent) variable = volume or pressure, × A/C (machine breaths only) or SIMV (+ spontaneous) → VC-A/C, VC-SIMV, PC-A/C, PC-SIMV ("KNOW THESE"). PS, PEEP = add-ons; PRVC, APRV less common but tested.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 2; RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 20 (and notes), 21; RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 4, 5 (notes), 7 (notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 83, 84 · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#6, #8)

2VC vs PC: the Difference and When to Use Each

Volume Control (VC) Pressure Control (PC)
Guaranteed (independent) VT (Hess: flow and VT) Pressure
Varies (dependent) Pressure VT (and flow)
Patient develops ARDS VT still 500 mL, pressure increases Pressure still 20, VT decreases
Breath Volume-cycled; constant (square) flow; pressure "looks like a mountain" Time-triggered, pressure-limited, time-cycled (may be patient-triggered); descending flow; "looks like a table"
When to use Constant VT needed to maintain a desired PaCO₂ (Hess) Severe ARDS: VC pressures ≥ 50 cm H₂O can cause barotrauma; PC gives lower PIP; sedate
Weakness (Hess Table 20-1) ↑ Pplat as compliance falls; fixed flow → flow mismatch, esp. if set too low VT changes with compliance, resistance, effort

PC strengths: peak alveolar pressure cannot exceed the set pressure (less overdistention); variable flow improves flow matching. PIP = PC + PEEP: 20 + 10 → 30 cm H₂O. Ch 11 (NBRC): for mandatory breaths either VC or PC is satisfactory; if VC needs Pplat > 30, probably switch to PC at a limit ≤ 30.

SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 4 (notes), 30, 31 (notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 20 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slide 31; RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slide 4 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Volume Control Versus Pressure Control, Box 20-4, Table 20-1

3CMV (and How It Differs from Assist/Control)

The decks use "CMV" two ways (conflict file); decide by whether the patient can trigger.

  • CMV = control mode (Chang slide 14; RCP 202 slide 4): preset VT at a time-triggered rate; patient triggering is not possible. "A/C allows patient-triggered breaths; CMV does not." Needs sedation, ± neuromuscular blockade; an awake patient "fights the ventilator." Indications: tetanus or seizures, complete rest, crushed chest, paradoxical chest wall. Complications: prolonged-sedation problems, apnea and hypoxia if disconnected, disuse atrophy (18 to 69 hours of diaphragm inactivity). Key alarms: low exhaled volume and low inspiratory pressure.
  • A/C: assist = patient-triggered (negative deflection); control = time-triggered. Every breath gets full support: set 12, patient triggers 24 → 24 full breaths. Risk: respiratory alkalosis.
  • Hess and the Principles deck: CMV = A/C; the set rate is a minimum. Good at initiation (near-complete muscle rest); disadvantages: alkalosis and auto-PEEP with rapid triggering, muscle atrophy.
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 14, 15 (notes), 16, 17; RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 4 (and notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 84 · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#7); Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes, Table 20-2

4IMV vs SIMV

  • IMV: mandatory breaths at a fixed interval, spontaneous breaths between. Problem: breath stacking (mandatory breath before the patient finishes exhaling) → auto-PEEP, hyperinflation, ↑ pressures, ↑ WOB, barotrauma. No new adult ventilators offer IMV.
  • SIMV = IMV with synchronization: the ventilator waits in a synchronization window and delivers the mandatory breath with the patient's effort. Example: SIMV 10 → a mandatory breath every 6 s; between them the patient breathes any VT, and PS applies only to those spontaneous breaths.
  • vs A/C: rate 10, patient triggers 20 → A/C = 20 mandatory; SIMV = 10 mandatory + 10 spontaneous.
  • When to use: partial support, preserve muscle strength, weaning (SIMV 12 → 8 → 4 → CPAP/PS). Without PS → fatigue and weaning failure, hence SIMV + PS.
  • Hess: asynchrony if the rate is set too low; the mandatory rate can never exceed the set rate.
  • Instructor word-play: "SIMV = IMV."
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 18, 19, 20, 21; RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 85 (and notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#5, #7); Hess Ch 20 — Table 20-2 · Instructor's Final Exam Study Guide, word-play list

5Pressure Support (the Spontaneous Breath in SIMV)

PSV is patient-triggered, pressure-limited and flow-cycled (an NBRC phrase). It works only on spontaneous breaths; VT depends on patient effort, compliance and resistance; it cycles off at a set % of peak flow (often around 25%); it cannot be used in an apneic patient.

  • Indications: low spontaneous VT, high spontaneous rate, ↑ WOB.
  • Titrate to VT 8–10 mL/kg IBW (the slide's target; the notes call this the historical figure, and modern lung-protective practice targets closer to 6–8 mL/kg IBW, especially in ARDS) and spontaneous rate < 25/min. Example (5′10″ male): IBW = 50 + 2.3 × 10 = 73 kg → 584–730 mL.
  • Hess (Table 20-2): too low → fatigue and tachypnea; too high → expiratory-muscle activation.
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 22, 23 (notes), 24 (and notes), 25 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Table 20-2

6MMV (Mandatory/Minimum Minute Ventilation)

MMV guarantees a preset minimum minute ventilation. If spontaneous V̇E falls below it, the ventilator automatically increases the mandatory (volume-cycled) rate. Example: target 8 L/min; patient makes 9 → nothing added; drops to 6 → mandatory breaths fill the gap. Catch: it doesn't guarantee an adequate VT on each breath, only total V̇E.

  • When to use: weaning, a transition from full support, variable drive (neuromuscular disease, coming off sedation). The notes add it needs reliable patient effort.
  • Contrast: MMV ↑ the mandatory rate; ASV picks rate and VT; PAV amplifies effort.
  • Conflict: Hess and Principles slide 85 call MMV an IMV type where mandatory breaths come only when V̇E drops below threshold and the set rate is a maximum. Use the RCP 202 deck.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 9 (and notes), slides 14 (notes), 15 (notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 85 (and notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#7)

7PRVC (Pressure-Regulated Volume Control)

A hybrid: pressure-controlled breaths with automatic pressure adjustment to reach a target VT at the lowest PIP possible. Clinician sets RR, VT, FiO₂, PEEP; the ventilator adjusts pressure.

  • Compliance worsens → PRVC raises pressure; compliance improves → it lowers pressure.
  • When to use: you want VC's volume guarantee with PC's lower pressures. Board pearl: the mode that adjusts pressure to a target VT while minimizing airway pressure = PRVC.
  • Other names: VC+ (PB), AutoFlow (Dräger), Adaptive Pressure Control.
  • Conflict: the 105 slide says PRVC gives "Volume-Controlled breaths"; Hess says the breath is PC. Answer by function.
  • Hess cautions: worsening mechanics raise pressure; if demand rises (pain) and VT exceeds target, support is taken away — a cause of mode asynchrony.
  • Automode (Siemens 300A): no trigger for 12 s adult / 8 s pediatric / 5 s neonatal → PRVC; two patient-triggered breaths → Volume Support.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 4 (and notes); RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 26 (and notes), 27 (and notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes, Table 20-2

8VV+ (Volume Ventilation Plus, PB 840)

VV+ combines VC+ and Volume Support (VS).

  • VC+ (mandatory breaths, A/C or SIMV): "Puritan Bennett's version of PRVC". Preset VT and I-time, variable pressure and flow, a test breath for compliance; cough pressure is vented through the active exhalation valve. Designed for better synchrony than VC.
  • VS (spontaneous breaths): "Guarantees volume and lets pressure vary." Set target VT only: less effort → more PS; more effort → less. Common use: weaning from anesthesia.
  • PSV vs VS: PSV guarantees pressure, volume varies; VS guarantees volume, pressure varies. Both are patient-triggered, pressure-limited, flow-cycled.
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 28 (and notes), 29 (and notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slides 18 (notes), 19 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes

9PAV (Proportional Assist Ventilation)

"With PAV there is no target flow, volume, or pressure." Support is proportional to the patient's elastance, resistance and demand: pull harder → more assist; no effort → no assist. The clinician sets a % assist (e.g. 50%, 70%); the Chang notes say PAV is set to overcome 80% of elastance and resistance (Hess: 5% to 95%).

  • When to use: spontaneously breathing patients needing variable assist (COPD, neuromuscular disease, coming off sedation), weaning. Advantage: better synchrony.
  • Not for apneic patients; leaks reduce accuracy; overshoot can occur.
  • Hess: PAV is flow-cycled; too high a % → "runaway"; it has no minimum support level, so alarms and backup matter.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 15 (and notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes, Table 20-2

10APRV (Airway Pressure Release Ventilation)

"APRV is inverse ratio, pressure controlled, intermittent mandatory ventilation with unrestricted spontaneous breathing."

  • Settings: FiO₂, Phigh, Plow, Thigh, Tlow. No VT and no set RR. Ch 11 starting values: Plow 0–8; Phigh for 4–8 mL/kg PBW, kept < 30–35 cm H₂O; Thigh 3–5 s; Tlow 0.2–0.8 s (≈ 10–20 cycles/min). (Its recruitment notes' "open-lung" APRV: Phigh 25–30, Thigh 4–6 s, Tlow 0.4–0.8 s.)
  • Timing: most time at Phigh; < 1.5 s at Plow (both decks). Hess: 80% to 95% at Phigh → incomplete exhalation (intrinsic PEEP).
  • How: the release to Plow removes CO₂; VT = Phigh − Plow gradient, ↑ with compliance, ↓ with resistance. Spontaneous breathing at both levels.
  • When to use: hypoxemic failure/ARDS with an intact drive: better oxygenation, less VILI, recruitment at lower peak pressures. Ch 11: only if the patient can trigger enough breaths for an adequate V̇E.
  • Weaning (Chang): lower the release frequency; at zero it is CPAP.
  • Hess: "upside-down SIMV"; may cause large VT and large transpulmonary pressure swings; no large trials show benefit.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slides 22 (and notes), 23; RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 12 (notes), 26, 28; RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation (Chang Ch 13, 16), slide 24 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes, Table 20-2; Hess Ch 38 — Mechanical Ventilation

11HFOV (High-Frequency Oscillatory Ventilation)

Tiny VT (around or below anatomic dead space) at a very fast rate, to minimize lung injury. It actively pushes gas in and pulls it out.

  • Settings: amplitude (ΔP), frequency (Hz), mean airway pressure, FiO₂.
  • ↑ ventilation (↓ CO₂): ↑ amplitude or ↓ Hz (backwards from conventional: ↓ Hz → larger VT). ↑ oxygenation: ↑ mPaw or ↑ FiO₂.
  • Frequency: Ch 11: HFV (all types) 150–900/min, VT as small as 1–2 mL/kg; Ch 11 recruitment notes: HFOV 3–10 Hz. RCP 105: HFOV 3–15 Hz = 180–900/min; Hess: 1–15 Hz.
  • Use (Ch 11): hypoxemic failure not responding to conventional methods; severe IRDS or ARDS ± air leak; neonates also BPD, MAS, PIE, CDH, pulmonary hypoplasia. Relative contraindications: hypotension, air trapping, neonatal ICH. Hess: strong recommendation against routine HFOV in moderate/severe ARDS (RCTs: no benefit or ↑ mortality).
  • Adult weaning (Ch 11): FiO₂ to 0.40 first; ↓ mPaw 2–3 cm H₂O every 4–6 h until ≤ 20; then consider switching to PC (limit ≤ 30, PEEP 10–12); then a standard SBT. (RCP 105: 22–24; use 20.)
Gap · still not coveredstarting settings for adult HFOV (amplitude, Hz, mPaw); Ch 11 and RCP 105 Management of MV give only weaning steps (Ch 11's HFV comparison tables are images) — look in Chang Ch 12.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 26 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 12 (notes), 61, 63, 96; RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 20 (and notes) · Hess Ch 38 — Mechanical Ventilation, CPG 38-1

12All the Listed Modes Side by Side

Mode What is set / guaranteed Trigger → cycle When to use
VC VT guaranteed, pressure varies Patient or time → volume 1st choice; constant VT for PaCO₂
PC Pressure guaranteed, VT varies Time (or patient) → time 2nd choice; severe ARDS
CMV (Chang) Preset VT and rate; no patient trigger Time → machine Sedated/paralyzed; tetanus, seizures, crushed chest
CMV = A/C (Hess) Every breath full support; rate = minimum Patient or time → machine Full support at initiation
IMV Fixed-interval mandatory + spontaneous Time-triggered Historical; breath stacking
SIMV Synchronized mandatory; spontaneous ± PS Mandatory: time/patient; PS: patient → flow Partial support, weaning
MMV Minimum V̇E Low V̇E → ↑ mandatory breaths Weaning, variable drive
PRVC Target VT at lowest PIP Time or patient → time VC volume + PC pressures
VV+ VC+ (VT + I-time) / VS (VT only) VS: patient → flow Synchrony; weaning from anesthesia
PAV No target; % assist Patient → flow Variable drive; not apneic
APRV Phigh/Plow, Thigh/Tlow; no VT, no RR Time-cycled PC ARDS with intact drive
HFOV Amplitude, Hz, mPaw, FiO₂ Oscillator-timed Lung protection; refractory hypoxemia; neonates; not routine in ARDS (Hess)
Sourceas cited in items 2–11

13Patient–Ventilator Asynchrony: What It Is and Trigger Asynchrony

Asynchrony = breath delivery out of phase with the patient's effort ("fighting/bucking the ventilator"): ↓ comfort, ↑ WOB and O₂ cost, ↑ sedation, longer ventilation and weaning (Ch 11: ↑ MV duration and ICU stay). Ch 11's three categories: trigger, flow, cycle (Hess: trigger, cycle, mode; use the deck's). Ch 11 slide 79 (Fig 11-13) puts double triggering under trigger asynchrony; Hess, under premature cycling (item 14).

  • Missed triggers — causes: insensitive trigger (e.g. −5 cm H₂O; Ch 11 Fig 11-11: a drop of 10 below baseline needed), muscle weakness, commonly auto-PEEP (raises the baseline). Sign: patient rate > ventilator rate. Fix: more sensitive trigger (closer to zero, Ch 11; Hess typical 1–2 cm H₂O below PEEP); treat auto-PEEP (↓ V̇E, shorter I:E, bronchodilators); applied PEEP can help in COPD, rarely when the cause is high V̇E with too little expiratory time.
  • Auto-triggering (autocycling) — causes: over-sensitive trigger (Ch 11: −1 cm H₂O or 1 L/min may be too low; RCP 105: −0.5 or 0.5 L/min), leaks (effortless breaths → think leak), circuit condensate, cardiac oscillations. Ch 11 fix: decrease sensitivity slightly (−1 → −2 cm H₂O, 1 → 2 L/min), remove leaks/condensate, ensure proper cuff inflation.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 78, 79 (and notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 38 (and notes), 54 (and notes); RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 33 (and notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Patient–Ventilator Interaction; Breath Triggering

14Flow and Cycle Asynchrony

  • Flow starvation: the ventilator doesn't meet inspiratory flow demand (more common in VC). Signs: pressure waveform pulled downward (Ch 11: "scalloping" (concavity) — the patient's effort on top of the ventilator's; "dip early = I need more flow"), tachypnea, retractions, paradox. Fix: ↑ flow or change flow pattern; switch VC → PC/PS; ↑ pressure or rise time; treat pain/anxiety.
  • Premature cycling (I-time too short): effort continues → double triggering → breath stacking, up to twice the set VT (one study found it more common with PC than VC, Hess). Late pressure dip = "I want more volume".
  • Delayed cycling (I-time too long): patient actively exhales — abdominal muscle activation, pressure spike at end-inspiration. Causes: PS in obstructive disease, leaks. Fix: ↓ PS, raise the cycle (termination-flow) setting, or switch to PC. Keep patient-triggered I-time ≤ 1.5 s.
  • Air trapping: expiratory flow doesn't return to baseline → breath stacking, auto-PEEP (item 22).
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slide 80 (and notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 39 (and notes), 40 (and notes), 44, 50 (and notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Patient–Ventilator Interaction; Inspiratory Time

15Mode Asynchrony and the Fix Sequence

Mode-related causes: SIMV (mixed breath types; rate too low), adaptive pressure control/PRVC (cuts support when effort raises VT), the control mode in a patient who wakes, IRV ("significant patient-ventilator dyssynchrony"), old IMV, PS too low or too high. Hess: PAV and NAVA may improve synchrony.

Fix sequence:

  1. Read the waveform, then adjust settings: VT, rate, peak flow, PEEP, sensitivity, rise time, cycle.
  2. Change the mode: some patients synchronize better with pressure-targeted modes (PCV, APRV).
  3. Sedation/analgesia: "Anxiety is a common cause" (Hess); propofol for short-term sedation.
  4. Paralysis last: "DO NOT jump to paralytics"; troubleshoot first (kinked tube, secretions, settings). "Paralytics don't fix problems – they hide them."
SourceRCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slide 38 (and notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 24; RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 14 (notes), 18; RCP 104 Pharm Ch 18 — NMBAs, slides 28, 34 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Patient–Ventilator Interaction; Sedation; Table 20-2

16Ventilator Variables: Pressure, Volume, Flow and Time

Variable Definition Unit / number
Volume Amount of air delivered each breath (VT) mL or L
Pressure Force exerted by the ventilator to deliver air (it pushes gas in by raising airway pressure above alveolar pressure) cm H₂O
Flow Rate at which air is delivered (higher flow → set volume delivered faster); Raw = ΔP ÷ flow L/min; normal adult inspiratory 40–60 L/min
Time TI = start of inspiratory flow → start of expiratory flow; TI + TE = 60/f; I:E = TI:TE s
  • Control variables: volume, pressure and time. VC presets volume and flow; PC presets pressure; time control (e.g. HFOV) leaves flow, volume and pressure dependent on mechanics.
  • Phase variables: trigger (starts the breath), limit (held during inspiration; decks name pressure for PSV/PCV), cycle (ends it), baseline (PEEP; flow baseline = zero).
  • With constant flow, VT = flow × TI: 60 L/min = 1 L/s × 1 s = 1 L.
Gap · still not covereda named "limit variable" for volume-control breaths; the decks name it only for pressure breaths.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 27 (notes), 74 (notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 2, 3 (notes), 62 (notes), 75 (and notes), 78 (and notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 3 (notes); RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 5 (notes), 23 (notes), 30 (notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slide 6 (notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#1)

17Independent vs Dependent Variables

"Whatever the ventilator guarantees is called the 'independent variable.'" The one left to float is dependent.

Independent Dependent Deck example (patient develops ARDS)
VC Volume (Hess: flow and VT) Pressure VT 500 at PIP 20 → still 500, PIP 35
PC Pressure Volume (and flow) 20 cm H₂O, VT 500 → still 20, VT 300
  • VC — what raises PIP (Box 20-4): ↑ flow, constant flow, ↑ PEEP, auto-PEEP, ↑ VT, ↑ resistance, ↓ compliance.
  • PC — what changes VT (Box 20-5): ↑ with higher driving pressure, longer TI (while flow continues), more effort; ↓ with auto-PEEP, ↓ compliance, ↑ resistance.
  • RCP 203 deck: ↓ compliance → VC: ↑ PIP and ↑ Pplat; PC: ↓ VT. ↑ resistance → VC: ↑ PIP, Pplat unchanged; PC: ↓ VT only if flow continues to end-inspiration.
SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 75 (and notes), 79 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 52, 53, 54, 55 · Hess Ch 20 — Volume Control Versus Pressure Control, Box 20-4, Box 20-5

18Trigger: What Starts the Breath

"Trigger = Start": the patient or time starts the breath. Sensitivity = how much the signal must change before inspiration starts.

  • Time (machine) trigger: set rate (rate 12 → every 5 s); no negative deflection.
  • Patient trigger: a negative deflection below baseline, sensed as:
    • pressure drop — set 1 to 2 cm H₂O below PEEP/CPAP (Hess);
    • flow drop — base flow 10 L/min, sensitivity 3 → triggers at 7 L/min (Hess);
    • diaphragm electrical activity (NAVA).
  • Too insensitive (−5 cm H₂O): can't trigger, fatigue. Too sensitive → auto-triggering: Ch 11: −1 cm H₂O or 1 L/min may be too low (fix: −2 / 2 L/min); RCP 105 example: −0.5 or 0.5 L/min. (Hess's usual setting is 1–2 below PEEP, above; Ch 11 treats −1 as an example that can be too sensitive.) More sensitive = number closer to zero (Ch 11).
  • Auto-PEEP raises the baseline, so triggering is harder; with significant auto-PEEP neither pressure nor flow triggering may work.
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 5 (notes), 14 (notes), 16; RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 33 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slide 79 (notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#4); Hess Ch 20 — Breath Triggering

19Cycle: What Ends the Breath

"Cycle = Stop": volume, time or flow ends the breath (Hess adds pressure). The deck's line: "Volume Control is volume-cycled. Pressure Control is time-cycled. Pressure Support Ventilation is flow-cycled."

  • Flow cycling: inspiration ends when flow falls to a set value, e.g. 25% of peak flow (PS).
  • Pressure cycling (Hess): mostly an alarm condition — a cough in VC hits the high-pressure limit.
Breath Trigger Limit Cycle
PSV Patient Pressure Flow
PCV Time (or patient) Pressure Time
VC (A/C) Patient or time — Volume
Volume Support Patient Pressure Flow
Dual control — Pressure (volume-targeted) Time
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 5 (notes), 22, 30 (and notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 19 (notes); RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 11; RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slide 31 (and notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#4, #5)

20PEEP: What It Is, What It Does, How Much

"PEEP increases the end-expiratory or baseline airway pressure": it stays elevated (5, 8, 10) instead of returning to 0. Used with another mode; CPAP = PEEP on a fully spontaneous patient; EPAP = PEEP.

  • Chain: ↑ FRC → ↑ V/Q → ↑ oxygenation → ↓ WOB; recruits alveoli, ↓ shunt; number one ventilator adjustment for oxygenation.
  • Indications: shunt/refractory hypoxemia (P/F < 200; Ch 11: PaO₂ ≤ 50 on FiO₂ ≥ 50%), ↓ FRC/compliance, auto-PEEP unfixed by settings. Ch 11: < 50% O₂ → adjust FiO₂; ≥ 50% → PEEP; improving → FiO₂ ≤ 50% first, then PEEP.
  • How much: initial 5 cm H₂O (NBRC range 2–6). Optimal PEEP = "the lowest pressure level that offers the most benefits with the least side effects"; Ch 11: keeps SpO₂ ≥ 88–90% without lowering BP.
  • Finding it (Ch 11): highest static compliance (VC; ignore PIP); PC: largest increase in exhaled VT at constant ΔP; typically 2–3 cm H₂O above the lower inflection point (LIP) (RCP 105: the LIP itself); best-Crs PEEP + 2 after an RM; just before %VTCO₂ falls.
  • ARDS (Procedures deck): FiO₂ 30% → ~5; 60% → ~10; 100% → 20–24.
Gap · still not covered"physiologic PEEP" — no deck or Hess.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slides 6 (and notes), 7; RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 6, 39, 40, 42 (and notes), 44, 46 (and notes), 47, 87; RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 11 (notes), 13 (notes); RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 11, 16; RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 18; RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 37 (notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slide 62 (notes)

21PEEP Complications and the Dangerous Level

Dangerous level (RCP 202 slide, starred): "PEEP > 10 cmH₂O (or P̄aw > 30 cmH₂O; or PIP > 50 cmH₂O) is associated with an increased incidence of alveolar rupture or barotrauma." Chang's "safe" list agrees (PEEP < 10). Also: PEEP > 15 + intra-abdominal pressure > 20 harms the cardiovascular, renal and pulmonary systems.

  • Caveat (conflict): Procedures notes call PEEP < 10 older practice; many ARDS patients need 10–15 or higher. Hess limits PEEP if Pplat > 30 or driving pressure > 15. Exam answer: > 10.
  • Complications: ↓ venous return → ↓ CO → hypotension (the major downside); barotrauma (pneumothorax); ↑ ICP possible (PEEP can impede cerebral venous drainage; the OpModes slide says this applies in patients with normal lungs); ↑ PAP and ↑ CVP with ↓ aortic pressure; ↓ hepatic perfusion; excess PEEP compresses capillaries (dead space).
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 6 (and notes); RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slide 9 (and notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 16 (notes), 37 (and notes), 41 (and notes), 44 (and notes); RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 32; RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 34 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome

22Auto-PEEP

Unintentional PEEP from incomplete exhalation (aka intrinsic, inadvertent, occult PEEP; dynamic hyperinflation). The manometer may read zero while distal pressure is as high as 15 cm H₂O.

  • Causes: high rate, airway obstruction, insufficient inspiratory flow, ~1:1 or inverse I:E, excessive PS, IMV breath stacking, IRV. Highest risk: high Raw and/or high compliance with short expiratory time.
  • Effects: ↑ WOB, harder triggering, hypotension (↓ venous return), barotrauma. In VC it raises PIP; in PC it lowers VT.
  • Detect: expiratory flow doesn't return to zero before the next breath (Ch 11: "likely to appear on the NBRC"); patient rate > ventilator rate; high-PEEP alarm.
  • Measure: end-expiratory pause in a relaxed (sedated/paralyzed) patient; pause pressure − set PEEP: 12 − 5 = 7 cm H₂O.
  • Fix (give more time to exhale): ↓ RR, ↑ inspiratory flow (VC), ↓ VT, ↓ TI (PC), bronchodilator. Then, only for spontaneously breathing patients, applied PEEP up to 80–90% of auto-PEEP (RCP 203 Ch 3; Hess 75% to 85% — conflict); Ch 11 gives no %: extrinsic PEEP "in some cases" to ease triggering.
SourceRCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 32 (and notes), 33 (and notes), 34 (and notes), 35 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slide 72 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slides 41, 42 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 57 (notes), 58; RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slide 66 (notes); RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slide 18 (notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 24 · Hess Ch 20 — Physical Assessment; Box 20-4; Box 20-5; Obstructive Lung Disease

23High Pressures and Their Causes

PIP overcomes airway resistance + compliance; Pplat (end-inspiratory pause, no flow) reflects compliance only. "When PIP and Plateau move together, think lungs. When only PIP moves, think airways." (Normal values: section 21.)

Pattern Meaning Causes
↑ PIP, Pplat unchanged ↑ Resistance Secretions, mucus plug, kinked or bitten ETT, bronchospasm, airway edema, water in tubing, ↑ flow setting
↑ PIP and ↑ Pplat ↓ Compliance ARDS, pulmonary edema, pneumonia, atelectasis, pneumothorax (tension), pleural effusion, mainstem intubation, ascites
  • High-pressure alarm (Ch 11, AARC): 35 cm H₂O initially if PIP is not yet measured, then 10 above observed PIP once stable (RCP 105: 10 to 15 above); causes: water in the circuit, secretions, kinked ETT, tension pneumothorax, coughing. Reaching it ends (pressure-cycles) the breath.
  • Circuit obstruction gives low-volume and high-pressure alarms; expiratory obstruction is the more serious (rebreathing, asphyxia, barotrauma).
  • Ceilings: PIP < 50; Pplat < 30 (Chang prints < 35; use < 30).
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 19 (notes), 67 (notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 2, 13 (and notes), 65 (notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 8 (notes), 9 (and notes), 16 (and notes); RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 27; RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 34 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 53, 55; RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide 72 · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#5); Hess Ch 20 — Volume Control Versus Pressure Control

24Low Pressures and Their Causes

Low inspiratory pressure alarm = 10 cm H₂O below observed PIP (or the set pressure in PC) (Ch 11; RCP 105: 10 to 15 below); with the low VT alarm (Ch 11: 150 mL above and below set VT; RCP 105: low exhaled volume 100 mL below expired VT) it detects leaks or disconnection. Hess: the most important alarm is the patient-disconnect alarm.

  • Causes: circuit disconnection; circuit leaks (among the most common causes of lost volume and pressure); ETT cuff leak (Hess: end-inspiratory squeak over the trachea); ventilator malfunction (run a leak test).
  • Low PEEP alarm: PEEP falls below its limit — usually a circuit or cuff leak.
  • Leak on graphics: expired VT < set on every breath, PIP lower every breath; with PEEP, a leak can auto-trigger. Ch 11 ("likely on the NBRC"): the volume–time curve doesn't return to baseline → find the leak (often the ETT cuff; also loose connections, humidifier chamber, mask, chest tube/BPF).
  • Benign fall: improving compliance or resistance lowers PIP.
  • Rule: alarm → look at the patient → ventilator → cause → fix.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 19 (notes), 71 (and notes); RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 26; RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 32 (and notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 52, 53 (and notes), 54; RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 65 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide 72; RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 53, 55 · Hess Ch 20 — Alarms; Physical Assessment

25Ventilator Circuits

Circuit Exhalation valve Use
Single-limb with valve Near the patient; pneumatic signal closes it for inspiration; residual pressure often sets PEEP Home care/transport
Double-limb ("wye") Inside the ventilator; inspiratory limb → 15-mm connector → expiratory limb back to the valve ICU
Single-limb, no valve (leak port) None; a known leak; ventilator calculates and subtracts it NIV
  • Tubing: adult large-bore corrugated; neonatal small, low-compliance (less compressed-volume loss).
  • Mechanical dead space (dual-limb) = wye to airway (anything added there, e.g. HME, adds dead space).
  • Compressible volume: circuit compliance can compress as much as 3 to 5 mL/cm H₂O (Hess) — keep it low. Corrected VT = expired VT − compressed volume; example 3 × (60 − 10) = 150 mL lost; matters most in neonates and pediatrics.
  • Care: don't change routinely (nor attached humidifiers/HMEs or closed-suction systems); change when visibly soiled or malfunctioning. If a major problem is suspected, bag the patient with a resuscitator and PEEP valve.
SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 63 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 66, 67, 68, 70, 71; RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 36 (notes), 40; RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 14 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 5a — Ch 7, slide 20 · Hess Ch 19 — Patient Circuits; Hess Ch 20 — Circuit

26Exhalation Valves

  • Job 1 — separate the phases: the expiratory valve is closed during inspiration and the inspiratory valve closed during expiration. ICU ventilators have an internal electromechanical expiratory valve; single-limb circuits use a pneumatic line to close the valve.
  • Job 2 — set PEEP: "The expiratory valve controls positive end-expiratory pressure."
  • Active exhalation valve: lets the patient breathe during a mandatory pressure breath (how APRV works) and vents cough pressure in VC+.
  • Safety: an emergency expiratory valve gives a second exit if the expiratory valve is blocked.
  • What goes wrong: expiratory/PEEP valve problems are common circuit problems; expiratory obstruction is the more serious (rebreathing, asphyxia, barotrauma), worst with an obstructed exhalation port on a single-limb NPPV circuit. Ribavirin can crystallize and occlude expiratory valves. An expiratory HEPA filter must be heated to avoid condensate blockage.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 67, 68, 70, 71, 72; RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 59 (notes), 63; RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 18 (and notes); RCP 104 Pharm Ch 13 — Aerosolized Anti-infectives, slide 33 (and notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Introduction (Figure 20-2); Ventilator Modes

27Static and Dynamic Compliance: Calculation and Normal Ranges

Compliance = ΔV ÷ ΔP. The deck rule: use PIP for dynamic compliance and Pplat for static compliance.

  • Static (Crs, Cstat) = pressure with no airflow: Cstat = VT ÷ (Pplat − PEEP). Ch 11: Cstat and Crs are interchangeable at the bedside; in a static-compliance table disregard PIP ("it is there to confuse you"): 600 ÷ (27 − 10) = 35.3.
  • Dynamic: Cdyn = VT ÷ (PIP − PEEP) (the deck rule; Heuer prints the same formula and adds that Cdyn is always less than Crs, the difference being airway resistance). PIP includes resistive pressure, so Cdyn < Cstat when flow and resistance are present.
  • Worked example (Hess Ch 51): VT 320 mL, Pplat 40, PEEP 26 → 320 ÷ 14 ≈ 23 mL/cm H₂O (low). Same patient, PIP 43 → Cdyn = 320 ÷ 17 ≈ 19 (our arithmetic).
Source Label Range (mL/cm H₂O)
RCP 203 Lssn 3a Crs, intubated adults 40–80; < 25–30 → ↑ WOB, weaning difficulty
RCP 105 Principles Adult static 60–100 (about 80)
RCP 202 Burn deck Lung compliance / static 50–100 / 70–100
Hess Ch 51 Acceptable CRS, ventilated 50–100
Any source Dynamic No range given

Use the RCP 203 40–80 for a ventilated adult (conflict file).

Gap · still not covereda normal range for dynamic compliance. No deck, Hess or Heuer gives one. (The printed formula is now filled from Heuer.)
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 40 (notes), 42 (and notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 7; RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slide 25 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slides 37, 41 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 52; RCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slide 37 (notes) · Hess Ch 51 — Respiratory System Compliance

28Minute Ventilation vs Alveolar Ventilation

  • Minute ventilation (V̇E) = VT × f — total gas moved per minute. Hess: 500 mL × 12 = 6 L/min.
  • Alveolar ventilation (V̇A) = f × (VT − V_D) — the "fresh" gas reaching the alveoli per minute. Unlike V̇E, it accounts for dead space.
  • Dead space: assume ~1 mL per pound of PBW (or ~30%). Hess: VT = V_A + V_D: 500 = 350 + 150; normal V_D/VT ≈ 0.3.

Worked example (RCP 203 deck): 125-lb patient, f 15, VT 400 mL → V̇A = 15 × (400 − 125) = 4125 mL/min, while V̇E = 15 × 400 = 6000 mL/min (our arithmetic).

Why it mattersWhy it matters: PaCO₂ varies inversely with alveolar ventilation. A low VT raises V_D/VT (150/500 = 30% → 150/300 = 50%) and lowers V̇A. On the ventilator, ↑ RR → ↑ V̇E → ↑ V̇A → PaCO₂ falls.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slides 15, 17 (and notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 15; RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 12; RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 35 (notes) · Hess Ch 50 — Dead Space Ventilation; Alveolar Ventilation; Hess Ch 20 — Blood Gas Measurements

29Relative Humidity and Rain-Out

  • Absolute humidity = water vapor mass per volume (mg/L). Relative humidity (RH) = content ÷ capacity at that temperature × 100%. At 37 °C, fully saturated gas holds 44 mg/L; 50% RH means half the maximum vapor pressure.
  • Why ventilated patients need it: an ETT bypasses the upper airway, and medical gases are anhydrous. Dry gas → thick secretions and mucous obstruction.
  • Targets: deck — heated humidifier as close to 100% RH at body temperature, probe near the wye, target 37 °C; HME 70% to 90% RH at 30–31 °C. Hess CPG 15-1: 33–44 mg/L, 34–41 °C at the Y, 100% RH; HME ≥ 30 mg/L.
  • Check: condensate near the patient's airway = fully saturated (100% RH); none visible = RH could be 0 to 99%.
  • Rain-out = condensation as heated, humidified gas cools in the tubing (gas at 50 °C holds > 80 mg/L; at 35 °C only 40). The deck calls condensation the most common threat to circuit patency (airflow obstruction). Manage: heated-wire circuit, in-line water trap, HME (temporary); treat condensate as contaminated: drain it with gloves, away from the patient, and discard as hazardous liquid waste (Ch 7 slide 20); use a drainage system in the circuit (Ch 6 slide 48); never let it reach the patient's airway (large-volume nebulizers: collect it in a trap/drain at a low point, never back into the reservoir).
SourceRCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 37 (and notes), 38, 39 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 47, 48; RCP 203 Lssn 5a — Ch 7, slides 20 (and notes), 21 (and notes) · Hess Ch 54 — Calculating Humidity; Hess Ch 15 — Goals of Humidity Therapy, CPG 15-1, Active Humidifiers

30Cuff Pressures

Keep ETT/trach cuff pressure 20–30 cm H₂O; most hospitals (and the NBRC "hospital") set 25 cm H₂O as the high limit.

  • Too high (> 25–30): obstructs tracheal mucosal blood flow → ischemia, ulceration, necrosis.
  • Too low (< 20): leakage-type aspiration, which may lead to VAP.
  • Measure and adjust at least once per shift (more after tube or cuff changes or a leak) with a calibrated manometer, three-way stopcock and 10- or 20-mL syringe (or a bulb device). Attaching a manometer loses volume, so always adjust, never just measure; readjust after settings change peak pressure; document.
  • Needing > 30 to seal → the tube may be too small (evaluate, recommend exchange); progressively rising pressures over time → likely tracheal dilation/tracheomalacia or cuff/pilot-balloon malfunction; consider a subglottic-suction tube.
  • Minimal leak technique is no longer recommended (RCP 203 Ch 4; Ch 9 slide 60 VAP-prevention strategies: avoid it).
  • Device trap (Ch 9): ETT ~20–30, standard cuffed trach the same general target; LMA ≤ 60 (inflate to 60); King LT ~60 cm H₂O. ETT = 20–30, not 60.
  • Units trap: 25 to 35 mm Hg is tracheal capillary perfusion pressure, not the cuff target. The 105 deck's "< 25 mm Hg" (≈ 34 cm H₂O) conflicts; use 20–30 cm H₂O.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slides 57 (and notes), 58, 59; RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 68; RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slides 24 (notes), 28, 31 (notes), 32 (notes), 42 (notes), 60; RCP 105 Airway Management, slide 86 (and notes) · Hess Ch 17 — Airway Cuff Concerns

31The Equation of Motion

The pressure needed to deliver a breath must overcome (1) airway resistance and (2) lung/chest-wall stiffness. The deck: "You do NOT need to memorize this equation, just understand what it means."

  • Hess Ch 19: Pvent = E × V + R × V̇ (E = elastance, the inverse of compliance). E × V = elastic load; R × V̇ = resistive load. With the muscles: Pvent + Pmus = E·V + R·V̇.
  • Hess Ch 20: Paw + Pmus = (flow × resistance) + (volume/compliance) + PEEP + auto-PEEP. Spontaneous: Paw = 0; full support: Pmus = 0.
  • VC fixes flow and volume, so pressure varies; PC fixes pressure, so flow and volume vary.
  • Asthma raises the R × V̇ term; ARDS raises the V/C term — both raise PIP, for different reasons.
  • Worked example (Hess Ch 51): Pplat = VT/C + PEEP = 320/23 + 26 ≈ 40; add R × V̇ = 9 × 0.33 ≈ 3 → PIP 43.
SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 62 (notes), 71, 72 (and notes), 73 (and notes), 74 (and notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#3), Figure 19-9; Hess Ch 20 — The Equation of Motion; Hess Ch 51 — Equation of Motion; Alveolar Pressure

32I:E Ratio — Computing It, Changing It, and the Flow That Follows

In A/C, the I:E ratio can be controlled and altered. Precise control is possible only in pure Control mode (no patient triggering allowed), but adjustments can still be made when some machine breaths are patient-triggered. Anything that changes I-time or E-time changes the ratio.

The two formulas

  • I-time = volume (L) ÷ flow (L/sec) — usually set by the clinician.
  • E-time is simply the time remaining in each breath cycle, determined by the I-time and the frequency of machine and patient breaths.
  • %I-time — the duty cycle — = I-time ÷ (I-time + E-time).
I:E ratio%I-time
1:420%
1:325%
1:233%
1:150%
2:167%
Worked example — setting the flow to hit an ordered I:EAn apneic patient on V/C A/C at a rate of 15/min has a VT of 600 mL. The physician orders an I:E ratio of 1:4. Which inspiratory flow should you set?

1. Minute volume: 15 × 600 = 9,000 mL/min = 9.0 L/min.
2. Convert the ratio: %I-time = 1 ÷ (1 + 4) = 20%.
3. Needed flow = V̇E ÷ %I-time = 9.0 ÷ 0.20 = 45 L/min.

Use the decimal for %I-time or the answer comes out a hundred times wrong. And note the deck’s limit on this: the calculation only works when you are controlling flow in V/C A/C. In P/C or PRVC you set the I-time directly — no calculation needed.

Changing the ratio — and it is almost always downward

  • More frequently you will need to decrease the I:E ratio. This comes up in severe expiratory airflow obstruction, where patients spontaneously show prolonged E-times and low ratios such as 1:4 or 1:5.
  • If similar ratios are not provided during mechanical ventilation, air trapping or auto-PEEP will occur.
  • If auto-PEEP is present and the I:E is high (1:1 or 1:2), the first step is to adjust settings to allow sufficient time for complete exhalation — decrease the ratio toward 1:4 or 1:5 by increasing E-time and/or decreasing I-time.
  • Decreasing the rate lengthens E-time. The deck’s own pair: RR 20 → cycle time 3 sec → 1:2; RR 12 → cycle time 5 sec → 1:4.
  • The memory line from the notes: “Shorter to get VT in, longer to get out … and longer to get VT in, shorter to get out” — the second half being inverse ratio ventilation.

The lung-protective menu the same deck lists

  • V/C with low VT — 4–6 mL/kg PBW
  • V/C with low Pplat — < 30 cm H₂O
  • P/C with PIP < 30 cm H₂O
  • Dual-mode ventilation (e.g. PRVC)
  • Airway pressure release ventilation (APRV)
  • High-frequency oscillatory ventilation (HFOV)
Caution — the permissive hypercapnia floorThese strategies may be recommended in conjunction with permissive hypercapnia: accepting a higher-than-normal PaCO₂ in exchange for the lower lung-injury risk of smaller VT or PIP. The deck gives the floor explicitly: you can accept hypercapnia and respiratory acidosis until the pH falls below 7.25–7.30, at which point you recommend either an increase in rate or administration of sodium bicarbonate. Section 20 elsewhere quotes the RCP 203 version as “if the pH stays below 7.30”; 7.25–7.30 is the same band stated as a range. Either way, pH is the stop sign, not PaCO₂.
One mode the deck defines that this guide did not haveAutomatic tube compensation (ATC) — a mode that reduces the work of breathing for spontaneous breaths through an artificial airway. It uses a closed-loop system to dynamically adjust positive pressure to compensate for the resistance of the tube, which makes breathing more comfortable and helps during weaning. To use it, the ventilator must be programmed with the patient’s airway type and size — which is the detail a question is most likely to hang on.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 8b — Ch 13 Ensure Modifications Are Made to the Respiratory Care Plan, 65 slides (added 2026-10-25), slides 44–48, 50, 51 and speaker notes

PIP, Pplat and PEEP: Normals, Lung Condition, Effects, Auto-PEEP

7 items · Normal PIP, Pplat, PEEP · what changes mean for the lung · venous return and UOP · auto-PEEP and its fix

1Normal (Target) Levels of PIP, Pplat and PEEP2What Each Pressure Tells You3Lung Condition When PIP and Pplat Rise or Fall4PEEP Too Low vs Too High5Effects on the Body When Pressures Rise: Venous Return and UOP6Auto-PEEP: What It Is and How to Find It7How to Resolve Auto-PEEP

1Normal (Target) Levels of PIP, Pplat and PEEP

No deck and not Hess gives a "normal" adult PIP or Pplat range; the sources give ceilings and targets. Know these:

Pressure What the sources give Source
PIP "Safe" < 50 cm H₂O; the notes call PIP < 50 historical — Pplat is the one to watch Initiation slide 32; Procedures slide 34 (and notes)
Pplat < 30 cm H₂O (ARDSNet; "lower if possible", Hess; Ch 11: VC needing > 30 → switch to PC at ≤ 30). Chang prints < 35; Ch 11's PC slide 38 prints Pplat ≤ 30–35 (≤ read from a symbol-font glyph) Procedures slide 34 notes; Initiation slide 13; Ch 11 slides 31, 38; Hess Ch 20
PEEP Normally pressure returns to 0 at end-expiration; initial 5 cm H₂O (NBRC range 2–6); Hess: low-level PEEP of 3 to 5 is often used in ventilated patients to prevent atelectasis; "safe" < 10 RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 6 (notes); Initiation slides 18, 32; Hess Ch 20
Driving pressure (Pplat − PEEP) < 15 cm H₂O Hess Ch 20
Mean airway pressure < 30 cm H₂O Initiation slide 32

Related RCP 203 normals for intubated adults: Crs 40–80 mL/cm H₂O, Raw 5–12 cm H₂O/L/s.

Caution · sources differConflict: Pplat < 35 (Chang) and ≤ 30–35 (a range, Ch 11 slide 38) vs < 30 (ARDSNet, Hess, Ch 11 slide 31) — use < 30.
Gap · still not covereda numeric "normal" PIP or Pplat range for adults (only ceilings exist) — look in Egan's.
SourceRCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slides 13, 18, 32; RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 34 (and notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 6 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 52, 54; RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 31, 38 · Hess Ch 20 — Positive End-Expiratory Pressure; Plateau Pressure and Auto-PEEP; Tidal Volume

2What Each Pressure Tells You

  • PIP overcomes airway resistance + compliance.
  • Pplat is measured with an end-inspiratory hold. No flow means no resistance, so it reflects lung and chest-wall compliance (pressure inside the alveoli). Hess: Pplat = VT/C + PEEP.
  • PIP − Pplat = transairway pressure = the airway-resistance part (depends on the flow setting and airway resistance).
  • Driving pressure = Pplat − PEEP (the elastic pressure across the alveoli).
Caution · sources differConflict: the RCP 203 Ch 3 slide 37 text and Ch 11 (PC modes: "Driving Pressure, termed ∆P (PIP – PEEP)"; its equal-pressure PEEP method holds ∆P at 15–25) call PIP − PEEP the driving pressure; the Ch 3 notes say PIP − PEEP is the total pressure delivered, not the true driving pressure. Exam: in a PC question, VT is changed via ∆P = PIP − PEEP (Ch 11); the < 15 lung-protective limit is Pplat − PEEP (Hess).
SourceRCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 8, 9; RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 13 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 37 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 38, 44 · Hess Ch 51 — Alveolar Pressure

3Lung Condition When PIP and Pplat Rise or Fall

"When PIP and Plateau move together, think lungs. When only PIP moves, think airways."

Finding Lung condition Causes
↑ PIP, normal Pplat (↑ PIP − Pplat) ↑ Airway resistance Bronchospasm, secretions, airway edema, water in circuit, kinked/obstructed ETT
↑ PIP and ↑ Pplat (PIP − Pplat unchanged) ↓ Compliance ("stiff lungs") ARDS, pulmonary edema, pneumonia, fibrosis, atelectasis, pneumothorax (tension), pleural effusion, mainstem intubation, mucus plug, ascites
PIP lower than the previous breath, every breath Circuit leak Leak (pressure still returns to baseline)
PIP/Pplat falling as the patient improves Better compliance or resistance "Opposite changes" (RCP 203)

In PC, pressure is fixed, so a stiffer lung or higher Raw shows as ↓ VT instead (Raw only if flow continues to end-inspiration). With auto-PEEP, Pplat rises in VC; VT falls in PC (Hess Ch 32).

SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 2, 13 (notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 9 (notes), 53 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 52, 53, 54, 55 · Hess Ch 32 — Asthma (Invasive Ventilation)

4PEEP Too Low vs Too High

PEEP Lung condition
Too little Collapsed alveoli → poor oxygenation (shunt)
Optimal Most alveoli open, best oxygenation
Too much Overdistended alveoli → barotrauma, ↓ cardiac output
  • PEEP corrects refractory hypoxemia from intrapulmonary shunting; ARDS may need more PEEP to keep alveoli recruited (Hess).
  • Ch 11: too little PEEP may not prevent collapse; too much → overdistension and worse lung injury. Optimal = the PEEP that keeps SpO₂ ≥ 88–90% without lowering BP.
  • Danger level: PEEP > 10 cm H₂O (or P̄aw > 30, or PIP > 50) → more alveolar rupture/barotrauma. Ch 11's optimal-PEEP examples are 15 and 18 and its RM PEEP is 20–25, so "> 10" is Chang's risk threshold, not a ceiling.
  • Hess: limit PEEP if it makes Pplat > 30 or driving pressure > 15; use it cautiously in unilateral lung disease.
SourceRCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 37 (and notes); RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 16; RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slide 9; RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 40, 44, 85, 88 · Hess Ch 20 — Positive End-Expiratory Pressure; Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome

5Effects on the Body When Pressures Rise: Venous Return and UOP

The chain: PPV/PEEP → ↑ intrathoracic pressure → vena cava compressed → ↓ venous return → ↓ preload → ↓ cardiac output → hypotension (↑ vasopressor need, especially after intubation or a large PEEP increase).

  • Kidneys → ↓ UOP: ↓ CO → ↓ renal blood flow → ↓ urine output, sodium and water retention. The kidneys get about 25% of circulating blood volume. Normal UOP > 400 mL/24 h or > 160 mL/8 h; oliguria < 20 mL/hr. Hess adds: ventilation ↑ ADH and ↓ ANP, which also lowers UOP and retains fluid.
  • PEEP specifics: CVP rises even though venous return is falling ("high CVP does not always mean lots of preload"); hepatic perfusion falls only when PEEP is added; the kidneys are hit first (more sensitive than the liver).
  • Brain: high PEEP/high mean airway pressure ↑ ICP (RCP 203).
  • Ch 11: high intrathoracic pressure in recruitment maneuvers can ↓ CO and tissue perfusion; IRV's ↑ mean airway pressure may ↓ venous return.
  • Hess: the drop in LV afterload can benefit fluid overload or left heart failure.
SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 31 (notes), 37 (notes), 38, 39, 41; RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 46; RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slide 9 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1 Evaluate Data, slides 35, 36; RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 30, 84 · Hess Ch 20 — Hemodynamic Effects of Positive Pressure Ventilation; Box 20-3

6Auto-PEEP: What It Is and How to Find It

Auto-PEEP = residual pressure above atmospheric left in the alveoli at end-exhalation from air trapping — the patient can't fully exhale before the next breath (aka occult PEEP, intrinsic PEEP, dynamic hyperinflation). Common in COPD and asthma.

  • Causes: high rates, large VT, ↑ airway resistance, too long an I:E (Ch 11); greatest risk = high Raw and/or high Crs on modes that limit expiratory time.
  • Consequences: ↑ WOB (harder to trigger), ↓ venous return/CO, hypotension, barotrauma, hyperinflation.
  • Detect: flow–time waveform — expiratory flow doesn't return to zero before the next breath (or the flow–volume loop); Ch 11: gas trapping, "likely to appear on the NBRC."
  • Measure: end-expiratory pause (no flow lets airway pressure equal alveolar pressure); auto-PEEP = pause pressure − set PEEP: 12 − 5 = 7 cm H₂O. The patient must be relaxed; Hess pause 0.5 to 1.5 s (Ch 20) or 0.5 to 2 s (Ch 51).
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slides 41 (and notes), 42 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 57 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slide 72 (and notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Plateau Pressure and Auto-PEEP; Hess Ch 51 — Alveolar Pressure

7How to Resolve Auto-PEEP

Give the lungs more time to empty, then treat the obstruction.

  1. ↑ Expiratory time: ↓ RR (VC or PC); ↑ inspiratory flow (VC — shortens TI); ↓ VT (VC); ↓ TI (PC); adjust I:E.
  2. Treat airflow obstruction: bronchodilators (albuterol, ipratropium), steroids, suctioning.
  3. Extrinsic PEEP up to 80–90% of measured auto-PEEP, only for spontaneously breathing patients (helps them trigger).

Ch 11 lists the same fixes (↓ RR, ↓ VT, ↑ expiratory time, bronchodilators/suction) and extrinsic PEEP only "in some cases" (no %).

Caution · sources differConflict: Hess sets applied PEEP up to 75% to 85% of auto-PEEP and, in asthma, generally no more than 10 cm H₂O, noting PEEP has little role if the patient isn't trying to trigger. Use the RCP 203 80–90%.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 42 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 57 (notes), 58; RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slide 72 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Obstructive Lung Disease; Hess Ch 32 — Asthma (Invasive Ventilation)

Respiratory Drugs

18 items · Acetylcysteine, beclomethasone, albuterol, ipratropium, levalbuterol · NMBAs · LABA vs SABA · sedatives · opioids · half-life · routes · bioavailability · elimination · creatinine vs BUN · leukotriene inhibitors · immunoglobulins · frequencies and sites of action · + inhaled-drug order, device selection, ET instillation, surfactant (Lssn 8a/8b)

1Acetylcysteine (N-Acetylcysteine, NAC, Mucomyst)2Beclomethasone Dipropionate (QVAR)3Albuterol and Levalbuterol (SABAs)4Ipratropium Bromide (Atrovent)5Neuromuscular Blocking Agents: Depolarizing vs Nondepolarizing6LABAs vs SABAs7Sedatives8Opiates (Opioids)9Half-Life of Medications10Routes of Administration11Bioavailability12Organs of Elimination13Creatinine vs Blood Urea Nitrogen (BUN)14Leukotriene Inhibitors (Antileukotrienes)15Immunoglobulins and Monoclonal Antibodies16Standard Dosing Frequency and Sites of Action17Giving Inhaled Drugs — the Order They Go In, and Which Device18Drugs Down the Tube — Endotracheal Instillation and Surfactant

1Acetylcysteine (N-Acetylcysteine, NAC, Mucomyst)

Acetylcysteine (N-acetyl-L-cysteine, Mucomyst) is a mucolytic: it reduces the thickness or viscosity of mucus. Site of action is the mucus itself: mucus contains disulfide bonds, NAC breaks those bonds (free thiol/sulfhydryl groups replace them). It comes as 10% and 20%; the 20% is often diluted with normal saline, essentially making a 10% solution.

  • Hazards: bronchospasm, especially in asthmatics (less common with 10%), so give a bronchodilator before NAC; nausea or vomiting; hydrogen sulfide ("rotten egg") odor; mechanical obstruction of the airway. Incompatible with some antibiotics in mixture ("DO NOT MIX").
  • Does it work? The deck: no data show oral or aerosolized NAC is effective for any lung disease, and it should not be used as a mucoactive medication. Hess calls it adjunctive mucolytic therapy whose benefit is unproven. Answer: mucolytic by mechanism, not recommended clinically.
  • Most important use today: antidote for acetaminophen overdose (liver injury). The deck flags this as "VERY TESTABLE".
Book fill · Heuer (not a deck number)Heuer's inhaled-drug table lists nebulized acetylcysteine (Mucomyst) as 3 to 5 mL of a 20% solution or 6 to 10 mL of a 10% solution, 3 to 4 times daily (SVN; vials 10% and 20%).
SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 9 — Mucus-Controlling Agents, slides 6 (notes), 8 (notes), 34, 35, 36 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 20 — Drugs Affecting the CNS, slide 29 (and notes) · Hess Ch 57 — Secretion Modifiers: Acetylcysteine

2Beclomethasone Dipropionate (QVAR)

Beclomethasone dipropionate HFA (QVAR RediHaler, an MDI) is an inhaled corticosteroid (ICS): a controller, NOT a rescue agent, and not for acute severe asthma. Indications: maintenance control therapy of chronic asthma, and COPD in combination with other agents. Hess: it is a prodrug activated in the lung to beclomethasone 17-monopropionate.

Site of action: glucocorticoids are lipid soluble and act on intracellular receptors, inducing anti-inflammatory proteins and inhibiting the enzyme PLA2. Steroids also restore responsiveness to β-adrenergic stimulation by increasing the number and availability of β-receptors.

Side effects: oropharyngeal candidiasis (thrush) and dysphonia are most common; cough and bronchoconstriction less common. Prevent with the minimal dose, a reservoir, and rinsing the mouth.

Frequency: Hess says ICS are effective "once or twice a day (depending on the drug and asthma classification)". Hess Table 32-1 lists QVAR as 40 or 80 µg/puff with no frequency.

Book fill · Heuer (not a deck number)Heuer's table lists beclomethasone (QVAR) MDI, 40 or 80 µg per puff, 2 puffs twice daily for each strength.
SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 11 — Corticosteroids, slides 5, 8, 32, 34, 39, 41, 45 · Hess Ch 57 — Inhaled Corticosteroids · Hess Ch 32 — Table 32-1

3Albuterol and Levalbuterol (SABAs)

Both are short-acting β₂-agonists (SABAs): "rescue" agents / "relievers" for acute reversible airflow obstruction. Binding the β-receptor increases cAMP, and β₂ stimulation relaxes bronchial smooth muscle. The deck's SABA class slide (albuterol and levalbuterol): onset 15 minutes, duration 4 to 6 hours.

Albuterol Levalbuterol (Xopenex)
Composition Racemic 50:50 R- and S-isomers. R binds β₂ → bronchodilation; S is often cited as nearly 100 times less potent and may add adverse effects Pure (R)-isomer. Hess: likely twice as potent, allowing half the dose
Nebulizer dose 2.5 mg in 3 mL NS (103 Asthma) 0.31, 0.63, 1.25 mg/3 mL; 1.25 mg/0.5 mL concentrate; also MDI
Side effects β-agonist list: tremor, ↑ HR/palpitations, tolerance, CNS effects, fall in PaO₂, ↑ glucose, ↓ K⁺, paradoxical bronchospasm Less tremor and heart-rate change, but similar side effects at a similar dose. Hess: superiority unproven

Other durations printed: 103 Asthma gives levalbuterol ~5–8 hours, and Pharm Ch 6 slide 30 gives albuterol 5–12 hr. Use the class slide's 4–6 hours for both.

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 6 — Adrenergic Bronchodilators, slides 11, 23, 25 (notes), 30, 31, 33, 44, 45, 54 · RCP 103 Asthma PP, slide 36 (and notes) · Hess Ch 57 — Short-Acting β₂-Agonists

4Ipratropium Bromide (Atrovent)

Ipratropium bromide (Atrovent) is an anticholinergic (parasympatholytic) bronchodilator, a muscarinic receptor antagonist; Hess calls it a short-acting muscarinic antagonist (SAMA).

Site of action: parasympathetic innervation causes basal bronchomotor tone, and anticholinergics "block" this tone. The M3 receptor on airway smooth muscle and submucosal glands causes bronchoconstriction; Hess says ipratropium blocks muscarinic receptors without specificity for subtypes. As a quaternary ammonium derivative of atropine, it does not distribute well across lipid membranes, so it stays mostly in the lung.

  • Use: maintenance treatment of airflow obstruction in COPD; may be used in some asthma. With albuterol (Combivent Respimat, DuoNeb) the combination is synergistic.
  • Timing (deck notes): onset several minutes, peak at 1–2 hours (albuterol peaks at 1 hour).
  • Side effects: dry mouth most common, cough, mydriasis (protect the eyes); BP, EKG and HR changes not usually seen.
  • Dose: deck MDI 17 µg/puff, 2 puffs QID; SVN 0.2% (0.5 mg) QID. Hess Table 32-3 gives 18 µg/puff and 500 µg, bid–qid. Use the deck.
SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 7 — Anticholinergic Bronchodilators, slides 1, 7, 8 (notes), 14, 15, 26, 29, 30 · Hess Ch 57 — Anticholinergic Agents · Hess Ch 32 — Table 32-3

5Neuromuscular Blocking Agents: Depolarizing vs Nondepolarizing

NMBAs ("paralytics") cause skeletal muscle weakness or paralysis by interfering with ACh at the neuromuscular junction (site: nicotinic receptors at the NMJ). They do not sedate or relieve pain: order is analgesia → sedation → paralytic, and a paralyzed patient must be on mechanical ventilation.

Depolarizing Nondepolarizing
Drugs Succinylcholine (the only one) Vecuronium, rocuronium, pancuronium, cisatracurium, mivacurium
Mechanism ACh-receptor agonist, resistant to AChE → sustained depolarization; fasciculations Competitive antagonist, blocks without activating; no fasciculations
Onset / duration 60–90 s; 10–15 min → ideal for intubation Longer; Chang: onset 3–10 min. Pancuronium longest, mivacurium shortest. IV only
Elimination Plasma cholinesterase; pseudocholinesterase deficiency → prolonged paralysis Pancuronium kidneys, vecuronium liver, cisatracurium Hofmann degradation (choice in hepatic/renal failure)
Key hazards Hyperkalemia; ↑ intracranial, intraocular, intragastric pressure; malignant hyperthermia Pancuronium: tachycardia, HTN
Reversal None ("ride it out") Neostigmine; sugammadex for rocuronium and vecuronium only

Hess gives succinylcholine 30–60 s, lasting 4–5 min; use the deck's 60–90 s and 10–15 min.

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 18 — Neuromuscular Blocking Agents, slides 4, 13, 14, 16, 17, 18, 19, 21, 22, 23, 24, 25, 26, 27, 28 (notes), 31 · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 42 (notes), 43 (notes) · Hess Ch 57 — Neuromuscular Blocking Agents

6LABAs vs SABAs

SABA LABA
Role Rescue / relievers: acute reversible airflow obstruction Controllers: maintenance bronchodilation, control of bronchospasm and nocturnal symptoms
Drugs (deck) Albuterol, levalbuterol Salmeterol, formoterol, arformoterol; ultra-long indacaterol, olodaterol, vilanterol
Onset / duration (class slide) 15 min / 4–6 h 20 min / 12–24 h
Chemistry Hydrophilic Lipophilic
Frequency (Hess) Albuterol prn, q4h–q6h Salmeterol bid; ultra-LABAs once daily

Both act on the β₂ receptor. Salmeterol has a slower onset than albuterol (20 minutes); the deck gives formoterol 3 minutes to effect, lasting up to 12 hours (Hess: onset similar to albuterol). LABAs are never used alone in asthma: they are not used without a controller (ICS), and they are not intended to replace SABAs for rescue (Hess).

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 6 — Adrenergic Bronchodilators, slides 11, 17, 23, 36, 37, 43, 47 · RCP 103 Asthma PP, slide 37 (notes) · Hess Ch 57 — Long-Acting β₂-Agonists · Hess Ch 32 — Tables 32-1, 32-3

7Sedatives

Sedation = diminished awareness; analgesia = pain relief: "Sedative ≠ Analgesic." Benzodiazepines, barbiturates and propofol work through GABA; dexmedetomidine is an α₂-adrenergic agonist.

Drug Key points Reversal
Benzodiazepines: midazolam (Versed), lorazepam, diazepam Anxiolytic, amnestic; terminate seizures. Alone, no change in respiration, but they augment opioid respiratory depression. Midazolam most important for RTs. No analgesia (Hess) Flumazenil
Propofol IV only; rapid onset, short acting, patients wake quickly. Apnea, hypotension; ↑ triglycerides (lipid vehicle); PRIS > 5 mg/kg/h. Analgesia: Hess none, Chang only at high dose None named
Dexmedetomidine (Precedex) Sedation, anxiolysis and analgesia without respiratory depression; usable before, during and after extubation None named
Etomidate RSI; onset 30–60 s, lasts 3–5 min; the "hemodynamically stable choice"; no analgesia None named
Ketamine Analgesia, amnesia, sedation; preserves respiratory drive; good for hypotension or bronchospasm None named
SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 20 — Drugs Affecting the CNS, slides 19, 20 · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 51, 63, 64, 65, 66, 67, 71 · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slide 42 (notes) · Hess Ch 57 — Sedatives: Etomidate, Benzodiazepines, Ketamine, Propofol

8Opiates (Opioids)

Opioids are narcotic analgesics for moderate to severe pain; they bind receptors in the brain and spinal cord (the receptors of endorphins and enkephalins). Drugs: morphine (standard reference), fentanyl (very potent, fast-acting), hydromorphone (Dilaudid, 5–10 times stronger than morphine), codeine, oxycodone.

  • Respiratory depression is the side effect the deck marks "MOST IMPORTANT"; benzodiazepines + opioids deepen it. Others: constipation, nausea and vomiting, antitussive effect. Doses that depress breathing have minimal effect on cardiac function.
  • Receptors: Chang deck mu (sedation), kappa (hypnosis), sigma (delirium); agonists turn on mu and kappa. Hess lists mu, delta, kappa. Use the deck.
  • Reversal: naloxone (Narcan), also a nasal spray; it blocks all three receptors.
  • Routes: PRN by the RN or PCA (keep a bag-mask and naloxone in the room). Hess: IV infusions are preferred in ventilated patients.
SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 20 — Drugs Affecting the CNS, slides 19 (notes), 31, 32 (and notes), 34 · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slide 58 · Hess Ch 57 — Opioids

9Half-Life of Medications

Plasma half-life (T½) is "the time required for the plasma concentration of a drug to decrease by one half", a measure of how quickly a drug is eliminated. The longer the half-life, the more prolonged the effects; older adults and patients with impaired liver or kidney function may be at greater risk for toxicity because the half-life is longer (Hess). It sets dosing frequency: shorter half-life requires multiple doses per day; longer half-life allows once-daily dosing (Pharm Ch 15, antihistamines).

Numbers the sources print: adenosine ~12 seconds; omalizumab 17–22 days (Hess); benzodiazepine sleep aids > 12–24 h ("hangover"); lorazepam much longer than midazolam (Hess); diazepam about 24 h in a younger adult, approaching 96 h in an older adult (Hess); propofol under 30 min (Heuer); COHb ~4–5 h on room air, ~60–90 min on 100% O₂, HBOT often ~20–30 min.

Gap · still not coveredhalf-life numbers for albuterol, levalbuterol, ipratropium, beclomethasone, NAC, and the other sedatives, opioids and NMBAs (diazepam and propofol are filled above). Hess Table 57-7 is not in the text file. See printed Hess Ch 57.
SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 2 — Principles of Drug Action, slide 18 (and notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 15 — Cold & Cough Agents, slide 12 (notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 21 — Vasopressors, slide 45 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 23 — Sleep Pharmacology, slide 6 · RCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slide 39 (notes) · Hess Ch 57 — Pharmacokinetics; Benzodiazepines; Anti-IgE Therapy

10Routes of Administration

Route (deck) Examples (deck)
Enteral (literally "small intestine"; most common is by mouth) Tablet, capsule, suppository, elixir, suspension
Parenteral ("beside the intestine"; injection) IV, IM, SC, IO, IT (intrathecal)
Transdermal Patch, paste
Inhalation Gas, aerosol; local or systemic effect
Topical Powder, lotion, ointment

Inhaled-route advantages: smaller dose, fewer side effects, rapid onset, targets the respiratory system, painless. Hess: inhalation bypasses the GI system and eliminates first-pass metabolism; lung deposition varies from 1% to 50%. Hess classes intrathecal as percutaneous/mucosal, the deck as parenteral; use the deck.

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 2 — Principles of Drug Action, slides 4 (notes), 5 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 1 — Intro, slide 19 · Hess Ch 57 — Pharmacokinetics, Pharmacodynamics, and Drug Delivery

11Bioavailability

Bioavailability "indicates the proportion of a drug that reaches the systemic circulation"; it is lowered by inactivation caused by stomach acids and metabolic degradation (deck). Hess measures it against the IV route, which is 100% bioavailable.

First-pass effect: an oral drug highly metabolized by the liver has much of its activity terminated in its passage through the liver. High first-pass metabolism reduces bioavailability, so the drug may need higher doses or another route (sublingual, transdermal, IV). Hess: absorption is affected by drug pH, food (slows it), fat solubility (lipid-soluble = faster), contact time and concentration; food cuts zafirlukast bioavailability by about 40%.

Inhaled drugs: L/T ratio = lung dose ÷ (lung dose + GI dose); a higher ratio = more efficient lung delivery. It rises with mouth rinsing, a reservoir device, patient instruction and a breath hold.

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 2 — Principles of Drug Action, slides 2 (notes), 12 (notes), 21, 22 (notes), 23 · Hess Ch 57 — Pharmacokinetics, Pharmacodynamics, and Drug Delivery; Leukotriene Modifiers

12Organs of Elimination

The liver is a primary site of drug metabolism; the kidneys are the primary site of drug excretion (Chang: liver = metabolism, kidneys = excretion). Hess: "The kidney and, to a lesser extent, the liver are the major organs of elimination. The lungs, saliva, and sweat are minor routes." Elimination depends primarily on renal function (CrCl or eGFR). Metabolism converts a drug to a water-soluble inactive form; a drug that is not metabolized can accumulate to toxic levels.

Renal failure → reduced drug clearance and toxicity ("Retention of Drugs"). NMBA choice (deck): renal failure → avoid pancuronium; hepatic problems → avoid vecuronium; liver or kidneys bad → use cisatracurium, avoid Sux. Hess says declines in renal or hepatic function do not require a succinylcholine dose reduction; use the deck.

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 2 — Principles of Drug Action, slides 17, 18 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 18 — NMBAs, slide 33 (notes) · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slide 54 · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 40 · Hess Ch 57 — Pharmacokinetics, Pharmacodynamics, and Drug Delivery; Neuromuscular Blocking Agents

13Creatinine vs Blood Urea Nitrogen (BUN)

Both rise in kidney failure ("BUN ↑; Creatinine ↑"); a rise in both is azotemia.

Creatinine BUN
Source Waste product of muscle metabolism (relates to muscle mass) Urea, formed in the liver when protein is broken down
Kidney handling Filtered with minimal reabsorption Readily filtered; about half reabsorbed (Hess)
What it reflects Reliable for GFR; more specific for kidney function Protein metabolism and hydration; less specific
Other changes Lower in women and the elderly ↑ with high-protein diet, dehydration, GI bleeding
Normal (RCP 202 Renal deck) Men 0.7–1.3; women 0.6–1.1 mg/dL 7–20 mg/dL

BUN/creatinine ratio is normally ~10:1 to 20:1. High BUN, normal creatinine → dehydration or high protein intake; both high → kidney disease/AKI. Hess: creatinine is "relatively insensitive" and may not rise until > 50% of renal function is lost. Other decks and Hess print different normals (BUN 8–18, 10–20, 7–21; creatinine 0.6–1.2, 0.7–1.5, 0.7–1.4 mg/dL); use the Renal deck's.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 3 — Renal A&P, slides 23 (notes), 26 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 53 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 13 — Aerosolized Anti-infectives, slide 12 (notes) · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 39, 40 · Hess Ch 5 — Renal Function

14Leukotriene Inhibitors (Antileukotrienes)

Leukotrienes cause bronchoconstriction, mucus production and airway inflammation. Antileukotrienes block them two ways:

Drug Site of action Key hazard
Montelukast (Singulair), "most tested" Blocks the receptor: binds the CysLT1 receptor with high affinity and selectivity Mostly mild (diarrhea, pharyngitis, sinusitis)
Zafirlukast (Accolate) Leukotriene receptor antagonist Headache, GI upset; Hess: empty stomach (food ↓ bioavailability ~40%); interacts with warfarin and theophylline
Zileuton (Zyflo) Blocks production: inhibits the 5-lipoxygenase (5-LO) enzyme Monitor liver function; interacts with theophylline and warfarin

They are prophylactic controllers, NOT for acute attacks; they protect against exercise-, aspirin- and allergen-induced asthma, are not optimal as monotherapy, and are effective in only about 50% to 70% of patients. Frequencies are in item 16.

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 12 — Nonsteroidal Antiasthma Agents, slides 3, 8, 24 (notes), 26, 27, 28, 29, 31, 33 · Hess Ch 57 — Leukotriene Modifiers

15Immunoglobulins and Monoclonal Antibodies

Immunoglobulins (antibodies) are made by B-lymphocytes; the five classes are IgE, IgA, IgG, IgM and IgD. IgE is the asthma antibody (attaches to mast cells, triggers allergic reactions); IgA protects mucosal surfaces; IgG is the most common and fights infections; IgM is the first responder.

Monoclonal antibodies (biologics) treat severe asthma by injection or infusion. They are add-ons, NOT for acute relief, and not a replacement for ICS. The most severe side effect is anaphylaxis.

Drug Site of action Use
Omalizumab (Xolair) Selectively binds human IgE, blocking IgE attachment to mast cells Allergic (IgE) asthma
Benralizumab, mepolizumab, reslizumab Block interleukin-5 (IL-5) → fewer eosinophils (Hess: benralizumab binds the IL-5 receptor) Eosinophilic asthma
Palivizumab (Synagis) F protein inhibitor; prevents RSV replication Prevents RSV in BPD, premature < 35 weeks, congenital heart disease

Pharm Ch 12 slide 4 calls these antibodies "humanized IgE", slide 34 "humanized IgG", and Hess calls omalizumab an IgG monoclonal antibody; answer IgG.

Gap · still not coveredRSV immune globulin. IVIG appears only for neuromuscular disease: RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders slides 21 and 44 (notes) and Hess Ch 44 (GBS; myasthenia gravis, 1.2–2 g/kg over 2–5 days).
SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 12 — Nonsteroidal Antiasthma Agents, slides 4, 16 (and notes), 34, 35, 36 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 13 — Aerosolized Anti-infectives, slide 36 · Hess Ch 57 — Anti-IgE Therapy · Hess Ch 32 — Anti-Interleukin-5
