17Giving Inhaled Drugs — the Order They Go In, and Which Device

Exam anchor — the sequence, which the deck prints as a numbered listIf the patient is receiving several inhaled medications, the recommended order is:
1. Bronchodilator first — 2. then mucolytic — 3. then airway clearance therapy — 4. then steroids — 5. finally the aerosolized antibiotic.

The logic runs straight down the list: open the airway, thin the secretions, get them out, then put the anti-inflammatory and the antibiotic into an airway that is open and clear so they reach lung rather than mucus. The deck states the rule twice — once as the full five-step order, and once as “if also administering steroids, mucokinetics, or anti-infective agents by inhalation, always give the bronchodilator FIRST.”

Why the bronchodilator really has to lead

  • All mucokinetics are irritating to the airway. To prevent bronchospasm, always precede the treatment with a bronchodilator — and administer mucokinetics in combination with airway clearance therapy so the loosened secretions actually leave.
  • Item 1 of this section already makes the same point for acetylcysteine specifically; this is the general rule behind it.

Picking the drug

  • All beta agonists have some cardiovascular and CNS effects. Recommend those with the least β1 (cardiac) and most β2 (bronchodilation) effect — albuterol or its isomer levalbuterol.
  • SABAs are relievers of bronchospasm; LABAs and/or steroids are controllers.
  • In acute exacerbations of asthma, repeat the standard dose every 20 minutes up to 3 times, or provide continuous nebulization until symptoms are relieved.
  • LABAs may increase the risk of severe exacerbations or death in some asthmatics by masking airway inflammation; they should generally be used in combination with an inhaled corticosteroid, and should not be recommended or used to treat acute exacerbations of asthma.
  • For maintenance therapy of bronchospasm in COPD: a LABA (salmeterol), a long-acting anticholinergic (tiotropium), or the combination (umeclidinium + vilanterol).
  • Aerosolized antibiotics — colistin, aztreonam (Cayston), tobramycin (TOBI) — are generally indicated only in patients with cystic fibrosis and suspected or confirmed Pseudomonas aeruginosa (gram-negative) pulmonary infections.
  • Inhaled corticosteroids control inflammation and are the first-line drugs for mild persistent asthma. With the exception of budesonide (Pulmicort), the common inhaled steroid preparations are all intended for an MDI or DPI — so technique is critical. Rinse the mouth after therapy to prevent pharyngitis and oral candidiasis, and use a spacer or valved holding chamber with MDI steroids to minimise pharyngeal deposition.

Picking the device — the deck’s selection logic, in order

  1. Determine the available formulations. Some drugs come only one way (DPI only, for instance), and that decides it.
  2. SVN administration should be reserved for acutely ill adults who cannot use either a DPI or an MDI.
  3. Most infants and small children should receive aerosolized drugs via an MDI with a holding chamber and mask — an SVN is the alternative if tolerated.
  4. An adult who is not acutely ill but cannot coordinate actuation with breathing: add a holding chamber, or use a breath-actuated MDI.
  5. DPIs are ideal for maintenance therapy in outpatient adults, larger children and adolescents who can generate sufficient inspiratory flow to carry the powder into the lungs.
  6. If several devices qualify, patient preference should be the deciding factor.
Nebulising through CPAP or BiPAPThe T-adaptor for an SVN, or the MDI adaptor, should be located near the mask or other patient interface. For SVNs, the gas source driving the nebulizer — air versus O₂ — and its flow should approximate the FiO₂ being delivered to the patient through the circuit: if the patient is on a high FiO₂, oxygen should drive the nebulizer. For neonates and infants, use a blender to obtain a precise FiO₂ for the driving gas, and monitor pulse oximetry continuously to minimise oxygen risk.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 8a — Ch 12 Administer Medications and Specialty Gases, 32 slides (added 2026-10-25), slides 6, 7, 8, 14, 15, 17, 18, 22 and speaker notes

18Drugs Down the Tube — Endotracheal Instillation and Surfactant

Selected drugs can be administered as liquids, instilled directly into the lungs via a tracheal airway. There are only two situations the NBRC cares about: the emergency drugs when there is no IV access, and surfactant.

Exam anchor — LEAN, and the five rules that go with itLidocaine · Epinephrine · Atropine · Naloxone — instilled in emergency situations when IV access is not available. Some add vasopressin, which gives the alternative mnemonic NAVEL.

The dose administered must be 2 to 2.5 times greater than the IV dose.
Dilute the dose with 10 mL of sterile water or saline for injection.
Put the patient supine — not Trendelenburg.
Halt chest compressions, and instill through a catheter that passes beyond the ETT tip.
Immediately after instillation, provide 5 to 10 rapid inflations with a bag-valve-mask resuscitator.

Surfactant — prophylactic for infants at high risk of RDS, or rescue for infants with clinical evidence of it

  1. Recommend a chest radiograph before instillation to confirm ETT position.
  2. Suction the infant prior to administration if necessary.
  3. Monitor SpO₂ and ECG continuously.
  4. Use a 5-Fr feeding tube or suction catheter to instill the solution.
  5. Insert the instillation catheter up to, but not past, the tip of the ETT.
  6. Split the dose in half and instill half into each dependent bronchus, turning the infant from side to side.
  7. Administer the dose as rapidly as tolerated.
  8. Manually ventilate the infant for 1–2 minutes afterward.
  9. Carefully monitor ABGs and chest movement during the first 3 hours, and adjust ventilator settings and FiO₂ as appropriate — the patient may transiently require higher levels of ventilatory support.
  10. If possible, avoid suctioning for 6 hours following instillation.
What the care-plan deck adds about choosing surfactantRecommend exogenous surfactant for a premature newborn having difficulty breathing or with clinical signs of IRDS. Recommend prophylactic rather than rescue administration, especially in infants of less than 30 weeks’ gestation — it decreases the risk of pneumothorax, pulmonary interstitial emphysema (PIE), and neonatal mortality. Recommend multiple doses if required; they have advantages over a single-dose approach. The agents named: beractant (Survanta), calfactant (Infasurf), and poractant alfa (Curosurf) — whose mg/kg doses are already in section 2 of this guide.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 8a — Ch 12 Administer Medications and Specialty Gases, 32 slides (added 2026-10-25), slides 23, 24 · RCP 203 Lssn 8b — Ch 13 Ensure Modifications Are Made to the Respiratory Care Plan, 65 slides (added 2026-10-25), slides 61, 62
23

Disinfection and Sterilization Methods

3 items · Pasteurization · autoclave · isopropyl alcohol · chlorhexidine

1Sterilization vs Disinfection, and the Spaulding Categories2Pasteurization vs Autoclave vs Isopropyl Alcohol vs Chlorhexidine3Other Methods: Glutaraldehyde, OPA, Bleach, ETO, Gas Plasma

1Sterilization vs Disinfection, and the Spaulding Categories

Spaulding (deck): process equipment by where it goes in the patient. Most RT equipment is semicritical, so reusable devices (e.g., a bronchoscope) get sterilization or high-level disinfection. High-level disinfection is not sterilization: bacterial spores may remain. Noncritical surfaces (tables, floors) are cleaned or disinfected regularly, after spills and when visibly soiled.

Hess adds: sterilization is the complete destruction of all microorganisms, including spores. Disinfection kills most organisms but not all; spores, mycobacteria and viruses are the most resistant. Cleaning comes first, because residue interferes with both.

Category Touches Processing needed Example
Critical In the body (sterile tissue, bloodstream) Sterilize (no viable organisms) Bronchoscope biopsy forceps; Hess: chest tube
Semicritical Mucous membranes High-level disinfection (or sterilization) Bronchoscope, most RT equipment
Noncritical Intact skin Clean / low-level disinfection Stethoscopes, BP cuffs, ventilator surfaces
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 5a — Ch 7 Infection Control, slides 18 (and notes), 19, 25 · Hess Ch 31 — Levels of Equipment Processing; Cleaning; Sterilization

2Pasteurization vs Autoclave vs Isopropyl Alcohol vs Chlorhexidine

Method What it is Sterilizes? Use
Pasteurization Hot-water disinfection. Hess: about 70 °C for 30 minutes, then a dryer No. The deck (slide 19 notes, a milk/juice example) says it does not sterilize: some non-pathogenic bacteria may survive Heat-sensitive items (deck: with glutaraldehyde and OPA)
Autoclave Saturated steam under pressure denatures proteins. Hess: 121 °C for 30 min or 132 °C for 4 min, both at 15 psi Yes, and it is sporicidal Heat-resistant equipment, cleaned and wrapped first; fast, cheap, nontoxic
Isopropyl alcohol Chemical that denatures proteins No. It does not kill spores and may damage rubber and plastic Skin before injections or blood draws, vial tops, thermometers, stethoscopes
Chlorhexidine Antiseptic (skin prep, oral care), not an equipment process No source uses it for equipment Scrub the site before an arterial line; skin prep before thoracentesis; oral care (VAP): the RCP 203 Ch 9 VAP bundle lists daily oral care with chlorhexidine (also RCP 105); Hess Box 36-7 says no longer recommended

Deck: an antiseptic is used on living tissue, a disinfectant on equipment. Chlorhexidine is not on Hess's list of disinfection methods. Hess also says daily chlorhexidine oral care may prove harmful in some patients; for this exam, use the deck's VAP list.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 5a — Ch 7 Infection Control, slides 6 (notes), 19 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 24 · RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slide 60 · Hess Ch 31 — disinfection methods; Sterilization · Hess Ch 9 — thoracentesis · Hess Ch 36 — Box 36-7 · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 46

3Other Methods: Glutaraldehyde, OPA, Bleach, ETO, Gas Plasma

Method Key facts
Glutaraldehyde (Cidex) High-level disinfection in 20 minutes at room temperature; sterilization takes a full 10 hours; deck notes: times vary by product, so follow the manufacturer. Rinse with sterile water. Hess: irritates the nose and airways (epistaxis, rhinitis, asthma)
OPA (ortho-phthalaldehyde) Sterilizes or gives high-level disinfection. Hess: 12-minute exposure; doesn't irritate eyes or nose
Bleach (sodium hypochlorite) Deck: for C. difficile (spores) use an EPA-registered sporicidal disinfectant; for blood spills, if bleach is used, follow product instructions and facility protocol for the dilution. Hess: CDC 1:10 dilution of 5.25% for blood spills and C. difficile rooms; never mix with an acid (chlorine gas)
Ethylene oxide (ETO) Hess: low-temperature gas sterilization for heat- and moisture-sensitive items. It is toxic, flammable and explosive, and items need 8 to 24 hours of aeration
Hydrogen peroxide gas plasma Hess: preferred sterilizer for items that can't take high temperature and humidity
Caution · sources differWhere the sources differ. Glutaraldehyde sterilization: deck 10 hours, Hess 6 to 10 hours. Use the deck's 10 hours. Bleach dilution: the deck gives no number (follow product/facility protocol); only Hess gives 1:10 of 5.25%.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 5a — Ch 7 Infection Control, slides 19 (and notes), 25, 26 · Hess Ch 31 — disinfection methods; Sterilization

Capnography

3 items · How capnography is measured · waveforms · sudden rises and falls

1How Capnography Is Measured2The Normal Capnogram3Sudden Decreases and Increases, and Abnormal Wave Shapes

1How Capnography Is Measured

Capnometry = CO₂ number; capnography = number + waveform; capnogram = waveform. Measured by infrared absorption.

Mainstream Sidestream (aspirating)
How Sensor at the airway (on the ETT); fast response Pump draws a sample through a tube
Best for Intubated patients; on a mask/mouthpiece needs O₂ 6 L/min Non-intubated (cannula or mask; infants to adults); Ch 8 slide 38 text says "Vented pts", but use non-intubated
Problems Weight on the ETT, added dead space; secretions on windows Secretions, water or a kink occlude the tubing; slightly longer lag

Setup (RCP 203 Ch 3): calibrated capnograph; inspiratory PETCO₂ = 0 torr; PETCO₂ runs 1–5 torr below PaCO₂ (more with high dead space); normal PaCO₂ 35–45, PETCO₂ 30–43 mm Hg. RCP 105: P(a-et)CO₂ normal 2, acceptable 5 mm Hg in critical patients.

ETT confirmation: waveform capnography is preferred (RCP 203 Ch 9; gold standard, RCP 105). Colorimetric = acceptable alternative: purple → yellow over six or more breaths = 2.0–5.0% CO₂ (about 15–38 torr); a transient change is less convincing (stomach CO₂ after BVM). It confirms the trachea, not depth (cannot reliably detect a mainstem tube): CXR for depth.

Volumetric capnography (Ch 11, PEEP-optimization method 5) measures the average tidal CO₂ concentration (V_TCO₂) (notes: CO₂ eliminated per breath); optimal PEEP is reached just before %V_TCO₂ begins to fall (overdistension adds dead space).

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3, slide 10 (and notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV, slides 44, 47 (notes) · RCP 105 Airway, slide 49 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 5b — Ch 8 Quality Control, slides 38 (and notes), 41, 42 · RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slide 34 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 47 (and notes) · Hess Ch 2 — Capnometry; Table 2-1

2The Normal Capnogram

Segment (RCP 203 Fig 4-2) What is happening
A → B Exhaling pure dead-space gas: no CO₂
B → C Dead space + alveolar gas mix: CO₂ rises rapidly
C → D Alveolar plateau: alveolar CO₂. PETCO₂ is read at the end of the plateau (D)
D → E Inhalation of fresh gas: CO₂ drops to zero

The RCP 105 deck labels the same curve phase I (dead space, PCO₂ near zero), phase II (rapid rise) and phase III (alveolar plateau; its end = end-tidal PCO₂). Hess: the plateau rises gently, and the peak (PETCO₂) represents alveolar PCO₂.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4, slide 20 · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV, slide 45 (notes) · Hess Ch 2 — Time-based capnogram

3Sudden Decreases and Increases, and Abnormal Wave Shapes

Change Meaning
Waveform and number disappear at once Disconnection
Falls toward zero as the tube kinks ETT obstruction; complete obstruction = zero
Sudden, proportional drop Cardiac arrest (lungs not perfused)
Very low PETCO₂ after intubation Esophageal intubation (Hess: "generally" very low; may be falsely high after antacids or carbonated drinks, which fades over a few breaths)
Abrupt rise during CPR Return of spontaneous circulation (ROSC)
Immediate rise IV sodium bicarbonate (forms CO₂)
Rapid rise with succinylcholine/volatile anesthetics Malignant hyperthermia
Baseline does not return to zero CO₂ rebreathing or a circuit problem
Steeper (upsloping) alveolar plateau Hess: airway obstruction, uneven alveolar emptying; RCP 105 figure: dotted line = bronchospasm tracing
Trend up / trend down Hypoventilation / hyperventilation; confirm with an ABG

During CPR, PETCO₂ tracks cardiac output: < 10 torr = poor blood flow. Trap: a fall from dead space (pulmonary embolism) is not improvement; do not lower the ventilator rate (RCP 105). Hess Table 2-2 also lists ↑ with fever, sepsis, seizures, COPD, exhausted CO₂ absorber and ↓ with hypothermia, hypotension, hemorrhage, cuff leak.

Book fill · HeuerA “shark-fin” capnogram means acute partial airway obstruction (e.g., severe bronchospasm, kinked ETT, mucus plug).
Gap · still not covered"curare cleft" waveform. It is in neither Heuer nor Hess, and RCP 203 Lssn 3a slides 21–22 (PETCO₂ changes and waveform descriptions) are images.
SourceRCP 105 Monitoring in MV, slides 45, 46, 48 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1, slide 47 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3, slide 10 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5, slides 23, 24 · RCP 105 Weaning, slide 48 (notes) · Hess Ch 2 — Table 2-2, Figure 2-18 text, CPR

Intrapulmonary Percussive Ventilation (IPV) for CF

3 items · Intrapulmonary percussive ventilation for CF

1What IPV Is and How It Works2Devices and Settings3Using IPV in CF: Indications and Contraindications

1What IPV Is and How It Works

IPV delivers rapid, short bursts of pressurized air combined with aerosolized medication. It mobilizes mucus, opens airways and improves lung expansion. Its key advantage over IPPB is that it combines airway clearance and lung expansion in one therapy, and the deck says IPPB has been replaced by IPV. The pharmacology deck files IPV under oscillatory PEP with the Percussionator (devices such as MetaNeb and Volara): high-flow mini-bursts at more than 200 cycles/min. The Percussionaire IPV is a pneumatic (gas-powered) device.

Hess adds: IPV injects short, rapid inspiratory flow pulses at the airway opening, and exhalation is passive (chest wall recoil). Hess gives the rate as 300 to 400 pulses per minute, and the patient keeps breathing spontaneously during the oscillations.

Rate: the deck and the Hess glossary say more than 200 cycles/min; Hess Ch 16 text gives 300 to 400 pulses/min (and the Percussionator at 1.7 to 5 Hz). On the exam use more than 200 cycles/min.

SourceRCP 103 Atelectasis, slide 20 (and notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9 — Mucus-Controlling Agents, slide 62 · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 54 (and notes) · Hess Ch 16 — Intrapulmonary Percussive Ventilation · Hess Ch 62 — Glossary

2Devices and Settings

No deck gives IPV settings; the settings below are from Hess Ch 16.

Device Frequency Notes
Percussionator 1.7 to 5 Hz Treatment about 15 to 20 minutes; mouthpiece or mask
Breas IMP2 About 1 to 6 Hz Can deliver aerosol
PercussiveNeb 11 to 30 Hz Single-patient use; cannot be used with a ventilator
MetaNeb Not given Aerosol + CPEP + oscillation; a typical session is 10 minutes alternating the two modes

The deck adds that MetaNeb combines CPEP, OPEP and nebulization, given by mouthpiece, mask or ventilator circuit, and switches between lung expansion and secretion clearance modes. RCP 203 Lssn 6b names the modes CPEP (lung expansion) and CHFO (secretion clearance), a session of about 10 minutes alternating them, and use in-line with a ventilator.

Gap · still not coveredIPV pressure settings (cm H₂O) and how many treatments per day. No deck (including RCP 203 Lssn 6b) or Hess gives them.
SourceHess Ch 16 — Intrapulmonary Percussive Ventilation · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9 — Mucus-Controlling Agents, slide 64 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion, slide 19

3Using IPV in CF: Indications and Contraindications

Indications (deck): atelectasis, thick secretions and poor airway clearance, such as cystic fibrosis or COPD, plus conditions needing lung expansion and preventing pneumonia. Contraindications: untreated pneumothorax, active hemoptysis, recent facial/oral/cranial surgery, unstable cardiovascular status (hypotension, arrhythmias), elevated ICP.

CF context (Hess adds): the CF Foundation recommends regular airway clearance therapy for all CF patients, but no therapy has shown superiority. Individualize it by age, patient preference and adverse events, and use it more often when the patient is acutely sick. Hess's CF therapy table also lists dornase alfa (degrades DNA in secretions) and hypertonic saline. Hess's CPG says IPV cannot be recommended in neuromuscular disease (insufficient evidence).

RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9 slide 64 (notes) lists cystic fibrosis first among conditions for which MetaNeb (filed with IPV on slide 62) is commonly used; RCP 103 Atelectasis slide 20 lists CF as an example of poor airway clearance. RCP 203 Lssn 6b (not IPV): the HFCWO vest is used mainly for CF and bronchiectasis, and CF patients are generally no longer placed head-down for postural drainage (reflux risk).

Gap · still not covereda CF-specific IPV recommendation, setting or schedule. No deck (including RCP 203 Lssn 6b) or Hess gives one.
SourceRCP 103 Atelectasis, slide 20 (and notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9 — Mucus-Controlling Agents, slide 64 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion, slides 8 (notes), 15 (notes) · Hess Ch 16 — Selection of Airway Clearance Technique; CPG 16-1 · Hess Ch 37 — CF therapy table
26

Inhaled Nitric Oxide

3 items · Inhaled nitric oxide: uses and side effects · + iNO dosing and weaning, inhaled prostacyclins (Lssn 8a/8b)

1What NO Is and What It Is Used For2Side Effects3Inhaled NO — Starting, Weaning, Stopping; and the Other Inhaled Vasodilators

1What NO Is and What It Is Used For

Nitric oxide (NO) is an endogenous vasodilator made by endothelial cells. Inhaled, it causes pulmonary vasodilation only: it raises cGMP, relaxing smooth muscle, and it improves blood flow to ventilated areas, which improves V/Q matching and PaO₂. It has no systemic hypotension, because hemoglobin inactivates it immediately (forming methemoglobin).

Used for:

  • Approved: neonates with hypoxic respiratory failure / PPHN, to lower pulmonary artery pressure and raise oxygenation, with ventilatory support. Deck trick: "NO = Neonates Only."
  • Off-label: neonatal cardiac surgery (lower PVR/PAP) and hypoxemia or pulmonary hypertension after lung transplant.
  • ARDS: it improves oxygenation only temporarily and does not improve survival. It is a short-term bridge; prolonged use is not recommended.
  • Preterm infants: not FDA-approved. Deck slide 39 guidelines: advantageous and the preferred pulmonary vasodilator in preterm infants with PPHN and severe hypoxemia; do not use it to prevent BPD ("NO for PPHN, not BPD").

Dose: cylinders hold 800 ppm; the recommended dose is 20 ppm, for up to 14 days. Hess adds: it is approved only for term and near-term (> 34 weeks) neonates, and it reduces the need for ECMO.

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 16 — Agents of Pulmonary Value, slides 28 (notes), 29 (and notes), 30 (and notes), 31, 36, 39 (and notes) · RCP 103 Diffuse Alveolar Disease — ARDS, slide 28 · Hess Ch 14 — Nitric Oxide Therapy

2Side Effects

Side effect What the deck says
NO₂ (nitrogen dioxide) NO + O₂ → NO₂, toxic to the lung. A higher FiO₂ makes more NO₂
Methemoglobinemia NO binds Hb → MetHb, which does not carry O₂ (functional anemia) and shifts the ODC left
Rebound pulmonary hypertension On withdrawal; it can be severe and cause desaturation
Contraindication Neonates dependent on a right-to-left shunt

The risks of MetHb and NO₂ rise above 20 ppm, and there is no added response at higher doses. Monitor NO, NO₂ and MetHb levels and cardiovascular status.

MetHb levels (deck): methemoglobinemia = MetHb > 1% (range 0–3%); 3–15% slight skin discoloration; 15–20% cyanosis; 25–50% headache, dyspnea, confusion, chest pain; 50–70% arrhythmias, seizures, coma; > 70% usually death.

Hess adds: OSHA's NO₂ limit is 5 ppm, but lung injury is reported at 2 ppm or less. MetHb is uncommon at ≤ 20 ppm. NO can also inhibit platelets, and prolonged high-dose NO raises the risk of renal dysfunction, particularly in ARDS. To prevent rebound, wean to 1 ppm for 30 to 60 minutes and raise the FIO₂ before stopping.

SourceRCP 104 Pharm Ch 16 — Agents of Pulmonary Value, slides 29, 31, 33 (and notes), 37 (and notes), 38, 40 · Hess Ch 14 — Nitric Oxide Therapy

3Inhaled NO — Starting, Weaning, Stopping; and the Other Inhaled Vasodilators

Items 1 and 2 cover what inhaled nitric oxide is, what it is approved for, and its side effects. This card is the numbers for running it, and the drugs that do the same job without being a gas.

Dose and delivery

  • The recommended initial dose of NO is 20 ppm, but can often be quickly reduced to 5–6 ppm. Treatment may be continued for up to 14 days.
  • Most common setups: the Mallinckrodt INOvent and INOmax systems. They include storage cylinders delivering NO through an injector placed in the inspiratory side of the ventilator circuit, near its outlet, plus a sensor that measures the ventilator’s inspiratory flow — so the injection rate is automatically adjusted in proportion to the measured gas flow, which is what keeps the delivered ppm constant.
  • NO can be given to spontaneously breathing patients by nasal cannula or mask, but it is more commonly applied to patients receiving mechanical ventilation.
  • When NO contacts O₂, the toxic by-product nitrogen dioxide (NO₂) is produced. The delivery equipment is designed to limit the contact time between the two gases to minimise it.
Exam anchor — the withdrawal checklist, and the rebound trapBenefits — increased oxygen saturation and decreased pulmonary artery pressure — may be seen soon after initiating therapy. Stopping is the dangerous part.

Before withdrawing: reduce the NO concentration to the lowest effective dose, ideally 5–6 ppm or less; ensure the patient is hemodynamically stable; verify tolerance of an FiO₂ of 40% or less and PEEP of 5 cm H₂O or less; monitor closely during withdrawal; and be prepared to provide hemodynamic support if required.

Wean the delivered dose in small increments of 3–5 ppm at a time to avoid a rebound effect. If methemoglobin rises above 1.5%, recommend decreasing the iNO dose as tolerated and continue to monitor. If the upper airway is intact or the patient is extubated, recommend a nasal cannula system to deliver the iNO.

Other inhaled pulmonary vasodilators — aerosolized medications, not gases

  • Iloprost (Ventavis), treprostinil (Tyvaso), and epoprostenol (Flolan).
  • They all dilate pulmonary arterial vascular beds and decrease platelet aggregation and, in doing so, reduce intrapulmonary shunting and improve oxygenation.
  • Used in spontaneously breathing patients with chronic pulmonary hypertension, and more recently in mechanically ventilated patients with acute pulmonary hypertension secondary to acute lung injury, ARDS, or similar conditions.
  • Indications for the class: pulmonary arterial hypertension (PAH) limiting physical activity in adults, and persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn (PPHN) — both carry high morbidity and mortality even with appropriate pharmacologic management.
Caution — handling an inhaled prostacyclinThree recommendations the care-plan deck attaches to these drugs: use the proper delivery device as recommended by the manufacturer; avoid healthcare-worker exposure by using expiratory filters and/or placing the patient in a negative-pressure room; and titrate dosage and/or frequency according to patient response. The instructor’s note is blunt about the last one: “Titration is extremely important, especially with Flolan.” He also notes epoprostenol (Flolan) is currently in use at both AVMC and PRMC, and flags its COVID-19 use.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 8a — Ch 12 Administer Medications and Specialty Gases, 32 slides (added 2026-10-25), slides 28, 29, 31 · RCP 203 Lssn 8b — Ch 13 Ensure Modifications Are Made to the Respiratory Care Plan, 65 slides (added 2026-10-25), slides 55, 56 and speaker notes
27

Pressure-Volume and Flow-Volume Loops

6 items · Pressure-volume and flow-volume loops

1The Pressure-Volume (P-V) Loop: How to Read It2Compliance Changes on the P-V Loop3Overdistension and the Inflection Points4Resistance, Leaks, Auto-PEEP and WOB5Flow-Volume Loop on the Ventilator6Spirometry Flow-Volume Loop Shapes

1The Pressure-Volume (P-V) Loop: How to Read It

  • Ch 11 (the named PowerPoint): ventilator graphics are scalars (pressure, volume or flow vs time) or X-Y loops. The P-V loop shows the pressure needed to deliver a volume, so you judge compliance and resistance "at a glance" ("likely to appear on the NBRC"). The right-side upswing = inspiration; the downswing to the left = expiratory return to baseline.
  • Axes: pressure on the x-axis, volume on the y-axis (RCP 105) — the "compliance" loop.
  • Static P-V curve (Ch 11): static pressure recorded at stepwise volume increases (super syringe or an automated routine); an "S" shape; slope = compliance (ΔV/ΔP).
  • The dotted line inside the loop = alveolar pressure (PAlv). The gap between airway-opening pressure and PAlv is PTA = flow × resistance — pressure spent on airway resistance, not on inflating the lungs.
  • Breath type (RCP 105): spontaneous = oval, symmetric, no sharp corners; controlled = well-defined with sharp angles; assisted = a small trigger effort, then machine assistance.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 45, 46, 69, 73, 74 · RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 55 (and notes), 62 (notes), 63 (notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 14 (notes)

2Compliance Changes on the P-V Loop

Ch 11 Fig 11-8 (slide 73): "the higher the slope, the higher the compliance." On the static curve (Fig 11-2, slide 46), a low slope at low pressures means high pressure is needed to open collapsed alveoli; the steep middle is the "sweet spot."

Change Loop Pressures Conditions
↓ Compliance Shifts toward the pressure axis (right), flatter ↑ PIP and ↑ PAlv, PTA unchanged ARDS, IRDS, pulmonary edema, atelectasis, pneumonia, fibrosis
↑ Compliance Shifts toward the volume axis (left), steeper ↓ PIP and ↓ PAlv, PTA unchanged Emphysema (stretches easily, poor recoil)
  • Example: the same VT ≈ 600 mL needs ~30 cm H₂O with normal compliance but a little over 40 with decreased compliance.
  • Pearl: "right is bad and left is good" — but this is about compliance only; emphysema shifts left.
  • RCP 203 Ch 4: ↓ Crs → ↓ slope in VC and PC (in PC, ↓ VT too); improvement (e.g. PEEP, surfactant) = a more vertical curve.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 46, 73 (and notes) · RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 56 (and notes), 65 (notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 14 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 52, 53 · Hess Ch 51 — Compliance; Hess Ch 33 — Pulmonary Disease

3Overdistension and the Inflection Points

  • Inflection points = where the curve's slope changes (Ch 11).
  • Upper inflection point (UIP) = overdistension: the change from high to low compliance at high volume. Beyond it, small volume changes cause very large pressure rises, so the loop looks like a bird's beak ("beak sign"; RCP 105: "duckbill") = excessive VT. Overdistended alveoli risk VILI (barotrauma, volutrauma). Fix: reduce VT until the beak disappears (or use pressure control).
  • Lower inflection point (LIP, aka critical opening pressure) = alveoli begin to open. Below it the curve is flat (under-recruitment). Ch 11: optimal PEEP = the lowest pressure that exceeds the LIP; PEEP is typically set 2–3 cm H₂O above it. (RCP 105 says "at or slightly above," in homogeneous "stiff" non-ARDS lungs, and less commonly applied in ARDS.)
  • Hess caveats: the UIP might represent the end of recruitment; P-V curves need sedation, often paralysis; pressure-control loops cannot identify optimal PEEP or VT.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 46 (and notes), 74 (notes), 75 · RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 58 (and notes), 59 (and notes), 64 (notes) · Hess Ch 51 — Pressure–Volume Curves; Hess Ch 19 — Waveforms and Loops

4Resistance, Leaks, Auto-PEEP and WOB

  • ↑ Airway resistance (Ch 11 slide 74 notes): the wider the loop, the higher the resistance. ↑ Inspiratory resistance shifts the inspiratory limb right (bronchospasm, kinked tubing, secretions); ↑ expiratory resistance widens the loop on the way out (air trapping, possible auto-PEEP; COPD, asthma). RCP 105: ↑ PTA and PIP, no change in PAlv.
  • Conflict inside Ch 11: slide 73's caption, tagged "LIKELY TO APPEAR ON THE NBRC EXAM", reads the other way: "(Loop C) the thinner the loop, increased airway resistance." Its figure is an image (Loop C can't be checked); no other deck or Hess says "thinner". Use wider = more resistance (slide 74 notes; RCP 203 Ch 4 slides 54–55).
  • Leak: loops don't return to zero volume (RCP 105); Ch 11 shows it on the volume–time scalar: exhaled volume doesn't return to baseline (ETT cuff, loose connection) — "likely on the NBRC."
  • Auto-PEEP: Ch 11 flow–time scalar: expiratory flow doesn't return to baseline before the next breath (also the flow–volume loop, RCP 203 Ch 3).
  • WOB: no ventilator-loop method in any source. Normal WOB: Hess 0.3 to 0.7 J/L; Heuer 0.5–0.7 J/L for healthy adults, obtained from the area of an esophageal pressure–volume curve during spontaneous breathing. The books differ on the lower limit; 0.5–0.7 is the overlap.
Gap · still not coveredhow to read work of breathing from the area of a ventilator pressure-volume loop; no deck (including Ch 11), Hess or Heuer gives that method (Heuer uses an esophageal pressure–volume curve).
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slides 71 (and notes), 72, 73, 74 (notes) · RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 57 (and notes), 66 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 41; RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 54, 55 · Hess Ch 51 — Work of Breathing

5Flow-Volume Loop on the Ventilator

"Volume/Pressure Loop = think compliance; Flow/Volume Loop = think resistance."

  • Look at the expiratory limb: inspiratory flow is preset by the ventilator.
  • ↑ Resistance (bronchospasm, mucus plugging): ↓ peak expiratory flow, a flatter, narrower expiratory limb; lungs empty slowly.
  • ↓ Resistance (bronchodilator, suctioning/secretion clearance): ↑ peak expiratory flow, a more peaked, wider limb.
  • Bronchodilator response (Ch 11 Fig 11-10; the slide does not say ventilator or spirometry): compare before and after — PEFR improves after the bronchodilator. Hess: F-V loops are useful for identifying the response to bronchodilators.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slide 76 · RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 60 (and notes), 61 (notes), 65 (notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Waveforms and Loops

6Spirometry Flow-Volume Loop Shapes

No deck text describes these shapes (Ch 11 slide 76 labels normal, obstructive and restrictive loops, but the loops are images); the answer below is from Hess Ch 8. Axes: volume horizontal, flow vertical, exhalation above baseline (opposite to the ventilator loop).

Pattern Shape
Normal High inspiratory and expiratory flows — a "sail"
Obstructive Concave downward expiratory slope; "ocean wave" or letter L when severe
Restrictive High flows and rapid emptying of the vital capacity — "upside-down ice cream cone"
Variable upper-airway lesion (vocal cord paralysis) Flow ↓ only on forced inhalation, not on exhalation
Fixed upper-airway obstruction (tracheal stenosis) ↓ flow equally on inspiration and expiration

Significant bronchodilator response: RCP 203 deck = FEV₁ ↑ ≥ 12–15% and ≥ 200 mL (adults); Hess = ≥ 12% (minimum 200 mL). Use the deck.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11, slide 76 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 76 · Hess Ch 8 — Forced Vital Capacity; Flow-Volume Loops and Volume-Time Graphs; Bronchodilator Testing

Transcutaneous Monitoring

2 items · Transcutaneous monitoring: electrode placement and troubleshooting

1Electrode Placement2Troubleshooting

1Electrode Placement

TcM gives continuous, noninvasive estimates of arterial PO₂ and PCO₂ (PtcO₂, PtcCO₂) from a heated sensor on the skin. The heating element "arterializes" the blood by dilating the underlying capillary bed, and O₂ and CO₂ diffuse through the skin into the sensor's contact gel. The RCP 203 steps, in order:

  1. Membrane the sensor and calibrate per the manufacturer.
  2. Temperature: usually 44°C for PtcO₂ and PtcCO₂; as low as 37°C if monitoring PtcCO₂ only.
  3. Site with good superficial circulation: side of the chest, abdomen, or inner thigh; clean it with alcohol.
  4. Adhesive fixation ring on the sensor, contact gel on the sensor face and inside the ring; avoid air bubbles.
  5. Edges sealed, sensor flat; allow 5–20 minutes to stabilize.
  6. Set high/low PtcO₂ and PtcCO₂ alarms (varies by patient/protocol; high PtcO₂ for infants typically 80 torr).
  7. Change the site every 2–6 hours (by sensor temperature, infant size, manufacturer) to avoid burns/skin damage.

Chang (RCP 105) says every 4 hours and 44°C (40–42°C in neonates, maximum 45°C); Hess says change sites "as often as necessary". Use the 203 deck's 44°C and 2–6 hours.

CCHD screen (dual sensors): right upper chest (pre-ductal) and lower abdomen or thigh (post-ductal); CCHD may be present if pre-ductal PtcO₂ is at least 15 torr higher.

Book fill · Heuer and HessHeuer: newer sensors need less frequent changing/calibration (twice daily) and membrane replacement at least every two weeks, run at 42°C and can arterialize capillary blood in as little as 3 minutes. Hess (combined earlobe sensor): the sensor is removed after 8 hours, recalibrated and fixed on the other earlobe. The two describe different sensor designs. The decks say only “calibrate per manufacturer”.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slides 11, 12 · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide S115 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 19 · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slides 50, 51 (notes) · Hess Ch 2 — CPG 2-2

2Troubleshooting

Most common problem: air leaking around the adhesive ring. Air leaks always cause a dramatic fall in PtcCO₂; a large leak makes values mimic room air (PO₂ ≈ 150 torr, PCO₂ ≈ 0 torr). Fix: reapply the sensor with a new adhesive ring. Calibration is two-point with precision gas mixtures; failure to calibrate is usually a leaking membrane or excessive trapped air under the membrane.

Problem Effect
Poor perfusion / vasoconstriction (low cardiac output, shock, dehydration) PtcO₂ underestimates PaO₂ (203 deck; Chang: a disproportionate fall in shock). Chang/Hess add: PtcCO₂ reads higher than PaCO₂ in low perfusion (203 deck: PtcCO₂ closely approximates PaCO₂ under most conditions)
PaO₂ > 100 torr PtcO₂ underestimates PaO₂
Thicker skin (children, adults), underheated sensor, bony surface, too much pressure or gel PtcO₂ underestimates PaO₂
Skin edema, hypothermia (Chang) Accuracy affected
High probe temperature (Chang) PtcCO₂ higher than PaCO₂

Chang separately says PtcO₂ becomes less accurate above 80 mm Hg; use the 203 deck's 100 torr.

Do not use TcM in shock or poor peripheral circulation, with poor skin integrity or adhesive allergy, or in emergencies (5–20 min setup). Hess adds thick or edematous skin.

Check accuracy: after setup or a site change, correlate PtcO₂ with an arterial or capillary sample (Chang); Hess's CPG compares ABG values with tc readings taken at the time of sampling, then periodically. If PaO₂ is substantially higher than PtcO₂, poor peripheral circulation is the likely cause.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide S115 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 18, 19 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 11 · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5, slide 21 · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slides 49 (notes), 50, 51 · Hess Ch 2 — CPG 2-2, Transcutaneous Blood Gas Monitors
29

APGAR Scores and Home Apnea Monitoring

3 items · APGAR scores · home apnea monitoring

1APGAR Score2Home Apnea Monitoring: Conditions Requiring It3How the Monitor Works, Alarm Settings and Apnea Definitions

1APGAR Score

The Apgar score assesses a newborn at 1 and 5 minutes after birth. Five signs (Appearance, Pulse, Grimace, Activity, Respirations) are each rated 0 to 2, for a maximum of 10 and a minimum of 0.

Sign 0 1 2
Heart rate (Pulse) None < 100/min > 100/min
Respiratory effort None Weak, irregular Strong cry
Color (Appearance) Pale blue Body pink, extremities blue Completely pink
Reflex irritability (to suction; Grimace) No response Grimace Cry, cough or sneeze
Muscle tone (Activity) Limp Some flexion Well flexed

(Descriptors: Hess Table 22-1; the deck gives only the APGAR mnemonic, matched here to Hess's five signs.)

Interpretation (deck): 7–10 = normal; 4–6 = moderately depressed, needs more intensive support; 0–3 = severely depressed, usually resuscitated. Never delay interventions to get the score; the Apgar does not decide whether resuscitation starts.

Hess adds: a 10-minute score if the 5-minute score is < 7; still 0 after 10 minutes of resuscitation = unlikely to survive; less useful in preterm babies (tone, effort and reflexes depend on maturity). Example (Hess's case, our scoring): limp, apneic, HR 85, fully cyanotic, no response to suction = 0 + 0 + 1 + 0 + 0 = 1.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2, slide 28 (and notes) · Hess Ch 22 — Apgar Score, Table 22-1 (table text

2Home Apnea Monitoring: Conditions Requiring It

Deck: apnea monitoring warns caregivers of life-threatening cardiorespiratory events, especially in neonates treated for recurrent apnea with bradycardia or O₂ desaturation. "At-risk babies" may go home with a prescription for a home monitor, as will some older children and adults with conditions affecting the control of breathing.

Hess Box 48-2 indications:

  • Methylxanthine treatment at home; bradycardia with methylxanthine use
  • GERD with apnea
  • Documented apparent life-threatening event (ALTE)
  • Risk of central apnea
  • Twin or sibling with a SIDS-related death
Exam anchorTrap (Hess): the AAP does not recommend home apnea monitoring to prevent SIDS (footnote to the twin/sibling bullet), and apnea of prematurity does not raise SIDS risk. For apnea of prematurity discharged on methylxanthines (Hess): the monitor can be stopped after 1–2 months with no true apnea or bradycardia events. Caregivers learn CPR, stimulation, lead placement and alarm response.
Gap · still not coveredBRUE and a list of false-alarm causes are in no source; Hess Ch 25 only says a home-ventilator patient whose ventilator lacks a needed alarm may need a cardiorespiratory monitor or pulse oximeter.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3, slide 47 · Hess Ch 48 — Box 48-2, Apnea of Prematurity · Hess Ch 25 — Setting Ventilator Alarms

3How the Monitor Works, Alarm Settings and Apnea Definitions

  • How: two chest sensors detect breathing movement by electrical impedance. It senses chest wall movement, not airflow, so it cannot detect obstructive sleep apnea (the chest keeps moving); suspected OSA needs polysomnography. Always confirm the patient's status by looking.
  • Setup: sensors on the right and left chest, midway between the nipple line and midaxillary line (greatest motion); confirm the signals match the patient's heart and respiratory rate.
  • Alarms: heart rate 80–210/min for neonates (lower for older babies); apnea time 15–20 seconds.
Term (RCP 203 Ch 4) Definition
Apnea Cessation of respiratory effort; < 10 s central apnea can be normal
Pathologic apnea > 20 s, or with cyanosis, pallor, hypotonia or bradycardia (< 80–100/min in neonates)
Periodic breathing ≥ 3 pauses > 3 s with < 20 s of breathing between; no cyanosis or HR change; can be normal

Hess describes periodic-breathing pauses as 5–10 seconds; use the deck.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3, slides 47, 48 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4, slide 62 · Hess Ch 2 — Impedance monitoring · Hess Ch 48 — Apnea of Prematurity

Carbon Monoxide

3 items · CO levels in % · treatment · how to measure

1COHb Levels (%)2How to Measure CO: CO-oximetry3Treatment

1COHb Levels (%)

COHb level Meaning Source
1–3% WNL RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 17 (notes); Lssn 3b slide 26 (notes)
5–10% Smokers same
10–15% Heavy smokers same
> 20% CO poisoning same
< 1.5% Normal CO level RCP 203 Lssn 1 slide 17
3–6% "Normal" for smokers, inner-city dwellers, those near industrial areas same
≥ 3–4% (nonsmokers) / ≥ 10% (smokers) Outside the expected range with smoke inhalation/CO poisoning same

The two 203 versions differ on normal (< 1.5% vs 1–3%) and smokers (3–6% vs 5–10%); know both. > 20% = CO poisoning is in both Lssn 3a and 3b.

Why it mattersWhy it harms: CO binds hemoglobin about 200–250× more strongly than O₂ (Burn deck; RCP 203 Lssn 1 says about 210 times) → COHb → less Hb to carry O₂ → tissue hypoxia, plus a leftward shift of the oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve. Hess adds: at HbCO 50%, unconsciousness routinely occurs.
Gap · still not covereda symptom-by-percentage COHb table (headache, nausea, confusion bands) — not in any vault deck or in Hess Ch 2, 14, 42 or 50.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4, slide 17 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5, slide 26 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1, slide 17 (and notes) · RCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slide 39 (notes) · Hess Ch 42 — Carbon Monoxide Exposure

2How to Measure CO: CO-oximetry

Measure carboxyhemoglobin with CO-oximetry (an ABG for CO-oximetry). Smoke exposure + normal PaO₂/SpO₂ does NOT rule out CO poisoning. PaO₂ can be NORMAL because it measures dissolved O₂ in plasma. A conventional pulse oximeter reads misleadingly normal/high because it cannot reliably distinguish oxyhemoglobin from carboxyhemoglobin: SpO₂ > actual SaO₂. CO-oximetry measures the individual hemoglobin components (O₂Hb, COHb).

  • Bedside correction (RCP 103): subtract COHb from SpO₂: SpO₂ 90% with 7% COHb = true 83%.
  • Exhaled CO (RCP 203 Lssn 3b): ppm ÷ 6 ≈ HbCO% (e.g., 48 ppm ≈ 8%), for moderate CO poisoning in conscious, cooperative patients.

Hess adds: the oximeter overestimates HbO₂ saturation by about the HbCO level. Newer multiple-wavelength oximeters give SpCO, but its accuracy is ±6%, so it should not be used for triage or management; a low SpCO never rules out CO poisoning and must be confirmed by blood HbCO.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slides 28, 39 (and notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV, slide 40 · RCP 103 Sleep Apnea, slide 16 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5, slide 26 · Hess Ch 42 — Carbon Monoxide Exposure · Hess Ch 2 — Pulse Oximetry to Measure Hemoglobin, Carboxyhemoglobin, and Methemoglobin

3Treatment

Give 100% oxygen immediately; it accelerates displacement of CO from hemoglobin. Ch 11: in emergency settings, CO (and cyanide) poisoning gets the highest possible FiO₂. Spontaneously breathing: non-rebreather with a tight seal, reservoir kept inflated (Hess: 15 L/min irrespective of SpO₂ and PaO₂). Can't protect the airway or ventilate: intubate, FiO₂ 1.0. Mild–moderate poisoning: 100% O₂ until COHb is less than 5%.

Breathing COHb half-life (Burn deck) Hess Ch 42
Room air ~4–5 hours about 5 hours
100% O₂ ~60–90 minutes about 74 minutes (26–148)
HBO ~20–30 minutes less than 30 minutes (3 atm)

Use the deck's 60–90 min on 100% O₂.

HBOT: consider it for severe CO poisoning, especially with significant neurologic depression or cardiac involvement; the decision is not based on COHb alone. Typical protocol: 3 atm first, then 2 or 3 atm, 90 minutes, two 10-minute air breaks. Don't delay O₂ while deciding: 100% O₂ → COHb by CO-oximetry → assess severity → transfer for HBOT. Hess: HBO is indicated for CO poisoning (Ch 14), but its advantage over normobaric 100% O₂ is debated (Ch 14) and its use remains controversial (Ch 42). Use the deck: consider HBOT for severe CO.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slides 39 (and notes), 40 (notes), 47 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 7 · RCP 105 Nontraditional Settings, slide 22 (and notes) · Hess Ch 42 — Carbon Monoxide Exposure · Hess Ch 14 — Clinical Practice Guideline 14-1, Hyperbaric Oxygen Therapy
31

The 40-50-60 / 70-80-90 Rule

2 items · The 40-50-60 / 70-80-90 rule

1The Rule and How to Use It2Why 60/90 Is the Key Pair

1The Rule and How to Use It

The "40-50-60 / 70-80-90" rule of thumb links SpO₂ to PaO₂. PaO₂ values of 40, 50 and 60 torr are about equal to SpO₂ values of 70%, 80% and 90%, respectively.

PaO₂ (torr) ≈ SpO₂
40 70%
50 80%
60 90%

Use it to estimate PaO₂ from a pulse-oximeter reading. RCP 203 Lssn 1 adds that the rule applies when SpO₂ is < 90%. It is approximate ("about equal", "roughly correspond"). Example: an SpO₂ of 80% suggests a PaO₂ of about 50 torr.

Hess never names the rule; it only describes the sigmoid oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve. At very low PO₂ the curve is flat; as PO₂ increases the slope rises rapidly, and at about 70 mm Hg the curve flattens, with saturation approaching 100%.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 14 · RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1 Evaluate Data, slide 45 · Hess Ch 50 — Oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve

2Why 60/90 Is the Key Pair

The rule's top pair is the O₂-therapy threshold. Hypoxemia is a PaO₂ < 60 torr or an SaO₂ < 90% on room air (RCP 203 Ch 6; Ch 11 says adult, infants < 50 torr), and an SpO₂ < 90% on any FiO₂ means the patient needs supplemental O₂. Weaning wants PaO₂ > 60 mm Hg (or SaO₂ > 90%) on FiO₂ ≤ 0.40. Near the 80/50 pair, central cyanosis first appears: SaO₂ below 80% (PaO₂ 45–50 torr) with normal Hb. Normal SpO₂ is > 93–95% on room air (Lssn 3a s14); Lssn 1 s45 gives an adult RA reference range of 95–98%. Ch 11 targets (not part of the rule): healthy patients PaO₂ 80–100 with SpO₂ ≥ 92%; if that needs ≥ 50% O₂, accept PaO₂ ≥ 55–60 with SpO₂ ≥ 88% (chronic hypercapnia: PaO₂ 55–60).

The rule's limits: pulse oximeters are accurate to only ± 2–4% with good perfusion. SpO₂ overestimates saturation when HbCO is present, and pulse oximetry is not accurate in CO poisoning.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide 6 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 5, 7 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 14 · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy, slide 13 · RCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slide 22 · RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1 Evaluate Data, slide 45
32

APGAR vs Glasgow vs Ballard vs Silverman Scores

4 items · APGAR vs Glasgow vs Ballard vs Silverman

1The Four Scores Side by Side2Glasgow Coma Scale3Ballard Score4Silverman Score

1The Four Scores Side by Side

APGAR Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) Ballard Silverman
Measures Newborn's condition right after birth Level of consciousness Gestational age after birth Magnitude of respiratory distress
Who Newborns Patients with altered consciousness / brain injury Newborns, especially premature Infants
When 1 and 5 min (Hess: also 10 min if < 7 at 5 min) Neuro assessment; Hess: the "D" (deficits) step of the primary survey By 12 hours of age (Hess) When distress is assessed (Hess gives no timing)
Scored items Appearance, Pulse, Grimace, Activity, Respirations, 0–2 each Eye (4), Verbal (5), Motor (6) Neuromuscular + physical maturity Upper chest, lower chest, xiphoid retraction, nares dilation, expiratory grunt, grade 0–2 each
Range 0–10 3–15 (intubated 2T–10T) Total score → weeks (New Ballard 40 = 40 weeks) 0–10 (5 signs × 0–2; computed)
Direction Higher = better (7–10 normal) Higher = better (≤ 8 severe) Higher = older gestational age Higher = worse distress
Exam anchorExam trap: the Silverman score runs the opposite way from APGAR and GCS: a high Silverman score means more distress. Ballard is not a health score; it estimates gestational age. The full APGAR table is in section 29.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2, slides 8, 28, 29, 30 (and notes) · RCP 105 Critical Issues, slides 20, 21 (notes) · Hess Ch 22 — Apgar Score, Gestational Age, Figure 22-1, Figure 22-2 · Hess Ch 41 — Primary survey

2Glasgow Coma Scale

The GCS "provides the most objective assessment of consciousness." E + V + M = GCS: Eyes up to 4, Verbal up to 5, Motor up to 6; maximum 15 (fully alert), minimum 3 (deep coma; Hess: "suggests brain death"). The lower the score, the worse the injury; motor response is the best predictor of neurologic outcome.

  • Bands (RCP 203): mild 13–15, moderate 9–12, severe (coma) ≤ 8. GCS ≤ 8 = intubate: these patients often cannot protect their airway. It is "a guideline, not an absolute law". Ch 11: GCS ≤ 8 is also a reason to avoid CPAP to treat cardiogenic pulmonary edema (not CPAP in general). The RCP 105 slide text prints "mild 13 to 14" and "< 8", but its notes and the RCP 203 deck say 13–15 and ≤ 8; use those.
  • Intubated: verbal cannot be tested, so the range is 2T–10T.
  • Sedated or paralyzed patients may score low without severe brain injury.
  • Deck example: eyes open to pain E2 + incomprehensible sounds V2 + withdraws from pain M4 = 8 → severe.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2, slide 8 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 16 (and notes) · RCP 105 Critical Issues, slides 20, 21 (notes) · Hess Ch 1 — Table 1-4 · Hess Ch 41 — Primary survey

3Ballard Score

The Ballard method (with Dubowitz) estimates a newborn's gestational age after birth from neuromuscular maturity + physical maturity. Findings are scored, and the total corresponds to gestational age in weeks. It is particularly useful for premature infants. RTs normally do not perform it; the instructor does not expect you to perform or memorize it, only to know what it is, what it evaluates and what the score tells you.

Hess adds (New Ballard): higher score = higher gestational age; 40 = 40 weeks; < 40 = preterm, > 40 = post-term. The maturity rating runs from −10 = 20 weeks to 50 = 44 weeks (each 5 points = 2 weeks). Neuromuscular signs: posture, square window, arm recoil, popliteal angle, scarf sign, heel to ear; physical signs: skin, lanugo, plantar surface, breast, eye/ear, genitals. Assess by 12 hours of age.

Full term: slide text 38–42 weeks; notes: term ≈ 37–42 weeks, and "full term" specifically 39w0d–40w6d.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2, slides 29, 30 (and notes) · Hess Ch 22 — Gestational Age, Figure 22-1 (figure text

4Silverman Score

No deck covers the Silverman score; the answer below is from Hess Ch 22. It assesses the magnitude of an infant's respiratory distress; the higher the score, the greater the distress.

Sign Grade 0 Grade 1 Grade 2
Upper chest Synchronized Lag on inspiration See-saw
Lower chest (retractions) No retractions Just visible Marked
Xiphoid retraction None Just visible Marked
Nares dilation None Minimal Marked
Expiratory grunt None Stethoscope only Naked ear

Hess's signs of distress: expiratory grunting (glottis closes to maintain lung volume; common in RDS), retractions (usually ↓ chest compliance, can be airway obstruction), nasal flaring, tachypnea; see-saw breathing = severe distress.

Book fill · HeuerSilverman-Anderson scale (Heuer Table 20-30): 0 = no respiratory distress; 1 to 6 = mild to moderate distress; 7 or higher = impending respiratory failure.
SourceHess Ch 22 — Physical assessment of respiratory distress, Figure 22-2 (figure text
33

Suction Catheter Size

2 items · Suction catheter size calculation

1How to Pick the Catheter Size: the Rule and Worked Example2Suction Pressures and Time Limits

1How to Pick the Catheter Size: the Rule and Worked Example

RCP 203 Lssn 6b (exam answer): choose a catheter that occludes < 50% of the ETT ID (< 70% for infants), so gas can flow around it and atelectasis is prevented (Hess CPG 17-1 gives the same < 50% / < 70%). Quick estimate in French (Fr): double the tube's ID, then select the next smallest catheter size.

Worked example (deck): 6.0-mm tube → 2 × 6 = 12 → next smallest size = 10 Fr.

Catheter size is the OD in French, a measure of circumference, so Fr ≈ diameter (mm) × 3 (Hess). Conflict: Hess Ch 17 limits the OD to ½ to ⅔ of the artificial airway's ID (a 14-Fr catheter is usually acceptable for adults), and Hess Ch 22 uses Fr = 2 × ETT ID with no step down. On this exam use the deck: 2 × ID, then step down to the next smallest size (6.0 mm: 12 → 10 Fr).

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion, slide 11 · Hess Ch 17 — Airway suctioning, Clinical Practice Guideline 17-1 · Hess Ch 22 — Suctioning (Respiratory Recap)

2Suction Pressures and Time Limits

Age RCP 203 Lssn 6b (recommended starting range) Hess maximum
Adults −100 to −120 mm Hg 150 mm Hg
Children −80 to −100 mm Hg 125 (Ch 22: 100–125)
Infants −60 to −80 mm Hg 100 (Ch 22: 75–100; "some recommend 60 to 80")

Use the deck's Ch 10 ranges. For adults, the Lssn 4 (Ch 6) notes say −80 to −120 mm Hg; both end at −120. Set the pressure to the lowest level needed, with continuous vacuum, by occluding the tubing while watching the gauge (closed system: thumb valve fully depressed). A CASS (subglottic) port uses continuous low suction of −20 to −25 mm Hg.

Time: no more than 15 s per attempt (RCP 203 Ch 11; Hess < 15 s); the RCP 105 deck says no more than 10 sec. Use 15 s. Preoxygenate (Ch 11): 100% O₂ for 30–60 s (Hess: FiO₂ 1.0; neonates +0.1 for 30–60 s).

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion, slides 10, 12, 13 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 86, 88 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 12 · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 41 · Hess Ch 17 — Airway suctioning · Hess Ch 22 — Suctioning

Air-Entrainment Flow Calculation

3 items · Air-entrainment flow calculation

1Step 1: the Air-to-O₂ Ratio (Magic Box or Formula)2Step 2: Total Flow = O₂ Flow × Total Ratio Parts3Matching Total Flow to the Patient

1Step 1: the Air-to-O₂ Ratio (Magic Box or Formula)

Deck "Magic Box": the box uses 100 = O₂ supply, 20 = room air (RA) and the FiO₂. The differences give the parts: air = 100 − FiO₂, O₂ = FiO₂ − 20. For 60%: 40 : 40 = 1:1 (the slide's box shows 40, 40 → 1:1); for 40%: 60 : 20 = 3:1. The subtraction steps are our reading of the box, which is a picture on the slide. Hess formula: Air:O₂ = (100 − O₂%) ÷ (O₂% − 21) : 1, so for 40%: 60/19 = 3.16 → 3:1.

FiO₂ 0.24 0.28 0.30 0.35 0.40 0.50 0.60 0.70 0.80
Air:O₂ (Hess Table 14-2) 25:1 10:1 8:1 5:1 3:1 1.7:1 1:1 0.6:1 0.3:1

Conflict: the box uses 20 for room air and Hess uses 21. They agree at 40% and 60% (the deck's examples), but drift at low FiO₂: our box math gives 19:1 at 24%, against Hess's 25:1. The deck's own ratio table (slide 14) is an image, so memorize the table above. Hess's 35% = 5:1 is rounded (the formula gives 4.6). The acronym "AEF" isn't in any source.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 14–16 (notes) · Hess Ch 14 — Air-entrainment devices, Table 14-2

2Step 2: Total Flow = O₂ Flow × Total Ratio Parts

The deck's formula: Total flow = O₂ input flow (L/min) × total ratio parts (air parts + 1).

Deck example Ratio Parts Total flow
60% at 10 L/min 1:1 2 10 × 2 = 20 L/min
40% at 10 L/min 3:1 4 10 × 4 = 40 L/min
Same patient, 40% at 5 L/min 3:1 4 5 × 4 = 20 L/min

Hess adds a large-volume nebulizer at 15 L/min: 0.6 → 30, 0.7 → 25 (0.6:1 gives 24, so Hess rounds), 1.0 → 15 L/min, and 35% → about 90 L/min. Traps: changing the O₂ L/min changes only the total flow, not the ratio or FiO₂. The higher the FiO₂, the lower the total flow. Bigger ports and smaller jets mean more air, a lower FiO₂ and a higher flow. Blocking a port raises FiO₂ and lowers flow.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 12–16 (notes) · Hess Ch 14 — Large-volume nebulizers

3Matching Total Flow to the Patient

The device stays high-flow only if its total flow meets or exceeds the patient's inspiratory flow. The deck gives a target of at least 40 L/min. Resting adults draw 20–30 L/min, children 10–20 L/min, adults in distress 60 L/min or more, and critical-care patients sometimes over 100 L/min. Hess adds: minimum flow = 3–4 × minute ventilation (40 L/min for adults, 4–6 L/min for infants). If the patient out-breathes the device, the mist disappears from the mask or T-tube port during inspiration, FiO₂ falls, and the system becomes low-flow with a variable FiO₂.

Worked example (ours): 40% needs 4 parts, so 40 L/min ÷ 4 = 10 L/min O₂ (Hess states this one). 60% needs 2 parts, so it takes 20 L/min O₂ to reach 40 L/min. For FiO₂ > 0.60 at > 40 L/min, the deck says to use a blending system.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 10, 13, 15 (notes), 18 · Hess Ch 14 — Respiratory Recap, Table 14-3

NPA sizingNares to earlobe (or tragus); largest diameter through the inferior meatus without force (RCP 203 Lssn 6a slide 15 + notes, RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways)Nose tip to ear meatus, or nose tip to tragus plus 2 cm (Hess Ch 17, Hess Ch 17)Use the deck: nares to earlobe
LMA sizingBy weight: size 4 = 50-70 kg, size 5 = 70-100 kg (RCP 203 Lssn 6a slide 25 notes, RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways)Sizes 3 and 4 small to average adults, size 5 large adults (Hess Ch 17, Hess Ch 17)Use the deck: size by weight
