20Mechanical Ventilation

TopicValue A (source)Value B (source)Which to use
MMV: how mandatory breaths are addedVentilator 'automatically increases the mandatory breath frequency' (volume-cycled) when spontaneous VE falls below the preset minimum; 'form of SIMV ... adjusts the frequency automatically' (RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 9 + notes; RCP 105 Weaning slide 24 notes)MMV is IMV variation 3: mandatory breaths delivered ONLY when measured VE drops below a preset threshold; with IMV the mandatory frequency can never exceed the set rate (set frequency = maximum); Hess gives Hamilton ASV as an example of this MMV-type IMV, whereas the deck contrasts ASV vs MMV (Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims #7); the RCP 105 Principles of MV slide 85 slide text carries the same IMV framing ('delivered only when the measured minute ventilation drops below a preset threshold'; 'mandatory breath frequency can never be higher than the set rate'), and its notes name it MMVDeck (RCP 202, instructor's): low VE -> ventilator increases the mandatory rate to restore the preset minimum VE. Both framings agree that low VE brings more mandatory breaths; they differ only on whether the set rate is a ceiling (202 slide doesn't mention one). Know the 'set rate is a maximum' framing (Hess and Principles slide 85) for a question quoting it.
CMV: what the abbreviation means'Controlled Mandatory Ventilation' = control mode: time-triggered, patient trigger and spontaneous breathing NOT possible; 'A/C allows patient-triggered breaths. CMV does not.' (RCP 105 Operating Modes slide 14; RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 4 lists CMV and AC separately); note the same Chang slide also labels it 'Continuous mandatory ventilation (CMV), control mode''Continuous mandatory ventilation, commonly known as Assist/Control' — patient triggers allowed, every breath mandatory, set rate is a minimum (Hess Ch 19 maxim #7; Hess Ch 20 Ventilator Modes; also RCP 105 Principles of MV slide 84 'CMV (aka: Assist/Control)')Don't decide by the spelled-out name (Chang slide 14 says 'Continuous mandatory ventilation (CMV), control mode'). Decide by whether the stem lets the patient trigger: patient cannot trigger / sedated-paralyzed -> control mode (Chang/202 slide 4); patient triggers and every breath gets full support -> A/C (Hess; Principles slide 84).
PRVC: volume control or pressure control?'PRVC provides Volume-Controlled breaths' / 'PRVC = Volume Control with an automatic pressure adjustment' (RCP 105 Operating Modes slide 26 + notes; RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 16)Breath type is 'actually PC or PS' with volume feedback; adaptive targeting (Hess Ch 20 Ventilator Modes; Hess Ch 19 maxim #9); RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 4 itself says 'Pressure-controlled breaths with automatic pressure adjustment to achieve a target VT'Answer by function, which all sources agree on: target VT delivered with automatically adjusted inspiratory pressure at the lowest PIP possible. If forced to classify, use the 202 slide 4 wording ('Hybrid': pressure-controlled breaths targeting a VT).
HFOV frequency rangeHFV 150–900 breaths/min, VT as small as 1–2 mL/kg (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 61); Ch 11 gives an HFOV Hz figure only for recruitment, 3–10 Hz (slide 12 notes); RCP 105 Management of MV slide 20 notes: HFOV 3–15 Hz = 180–900 breaths/min1 to 15 Hz (1 Hz = 60 breaths/min) (Hess Ch 38 — Mechanical Ventilation); 'up to 900 breaths/min in the adult' (Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes)Use Ch 11 (RCP 203 deck): HFV 150–900/min. The 900/min ceiling agrees across Ch 11, RCP 105 and Hess Ch 20/38. If asked for HFOV in Hz, Ch 11 has no general range, so use RCP 105 3–15 Hz; Hess Ch 38 says 1 to 15 Hz; Hess Ch 22 (neonatal) gives 400–2400/min generically.
HFOV: when to use itHFV indicated primarily for hypoxemic respiratory failure not responding to conventional methods; severe IRDS or ARDS with or without air leak; neonatal BPD, MAS, PIE, CDH, pulmonary hypoplasia (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slides 61, 63); main application is to minimize lung injury (RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 26); traditionally neonates, adult acute respiratory failure (RCP 105 Management of MV slide 20 notes)Strong recommendation AGAINST routine HFOV in moderate/severe ARDS; RCTs showed no mortality difference or increased mortality (Hess Ch 38 CPG 38-1); routine use in ARDS not supported, might be considered for refractory hypoxemia in experienced centers (Hess Ch 20)Deck for 'what HFOV is for'; if a question asks about routine use in adult ARDS, the Hess answer (not routine) is the evidence-based one.
PAV percent assist'PAV is set to overcome 80% of the elastance and airflow resistance'; % assist e.g. 50%, 70% (RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 15 notes)Clinician adjusts percentage of support from 5% to 95%, typically so WOB is 0.5 to 1.0 J/L (Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes)Deck's 80% is the Chang/instructor figure; Hess gives the adjustable range. Use the deck figure if a single number is asked.
PEEP: level where danger startsPEEP > 10 cmH₂O (or Paw > 30; or PIP > 50) associated with alveolar rupture/barotrauma (RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 6; RCP 105 Operating Modes slide 9); 'safe' PEEP < 10 (RCP 105 Initiation slide 32; RCP 105 Procedures slide 34); PEEP > 15 with IAP > 20 harmful (RCP 105 Principles slide 44)Aggressive PEEP of at least 13-15 cmH₂O had outcomes comparable to conservative (at least 7-9); higher PEEP may benefit moderate/severe ARDS (P/F ≤ 200); limit PEEP by Pplat > 30 or driving pressure > 15 rather than a fixed PEEP number (Hess Ch 20 - ARDS); Procedures slide 34 notes call PEEP < 10 'older practice', many ARDS patients need 10-15 or higherFor 'dangerous levels start at': PEEP > 10 cmH₂O (the RCP 202 slide, starred). Know that ARDS practice individualizes PEEP and often exceeds 10.
Plateau pressure ceilingPplat < 35 cmH₂O (RCP 105 Procedures slide 34 slide text; RCP 105 Initiation slide 32 'safe' pressures; RCP 105 Management of MV slides 20 and 64)Pplat < 30 cmH₂O (ARDSNet, on the same Procedures slide and in its notes; RCP 105 Initiation slide 13 notes; RCP 202 Burn slide 32 notes ≤ 30; Hess Ch 20 and Ch 51 'below 30 ... lower if possible')Use < 30 cmH₂O. The Procedures notes call it today's accepted goal. Recognize < 35 as Chang's printed 'safe' value.
Applied PEEP to counter auto-PEEP: how muchExternal PEEP up to 80-90% of measured auto-PEEP; only for spontaneously breathing patients (RCP 203 Lssn 2b slide 42 notes)PEEP up to 75% to 85% of auto-PEEP in obstructive disease to facilitate triggering (Hess Ch 20 - Obstructive Lung Disease)Deck (RCP 203): up to 80-90%. If a question quotes Hess, 75-85%. Both say partial, not full, matching, and only after setting changes (RCP 105 Management of MV slide 35).
Normal static compliance (Crs) for a ventilated adult40–80 mL/cm H₂O, normal Crs (aka CStat) for intubated adults; < 25–30 = ↑ WOB/weaning difficulty (RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 52)60–100 mL/cm H₂O adult static, about 80 normal (RCP 105 Principles of MV slide 7); 70–100 static and 50–100 'lung compliance' (RCP 202 Burn deck slide 37 notes); acceptable CRS 50–100 in ventilated patients (Hess Ch 51)RCP 203 deck 40–80 for an intubated adult on this exam; recognize the others. No source gives a dynamic-compliance range.
Cuff pressure target and units20–30 cm H₂O (RCP 203 Lssn 2b slide 57 notes, Lssn 3a slide 68; RCP 105 Airway slide 86; Hess Ch 17); hospital/NBRC high limit 25 cm H₂O (RCP 203 Lssn 2b slide 59)'Cuff pressure should be < 25 mm Hg' (RCP 105 Airway slide 86 notes); 25 mm Hg × 1.36 ≈ 34 cm H₂O. 25–35 mm Hg is tracheal capillary perfusion pressure (Airway slide 86 notes; Hess Ch 17), not the cuff targetUse 20–30 cm H₂O (high limit 25 cm H₂O).
Minimal leak technique'The minimal leak technique is no longer recommended' (RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 68); 'avoid using minimal leak technique' (RCP 203 Lssn 6a slide 60, VAP-prevention strategies)RCP 105 Airway slide 86 notes and Hess Ch 17 describe minimal occlusion and minimal leak techniques as options (Hess: neither assures 20–30 cm H₂O)RCP 203: do not use minimal leak; measure and adjust with a manometer.
Heated humidifier temperature / humidity at the airwayTemperature target 37 °C, as close to 100% RH at body temperature, probe at the wye (RCP 105 Management of MV slide 39)33–44 mg H₂O/L, 34–41 °C at the circuit Y piece, 100% RH (Hess Ch 15 CPG 15-1)Deck 37 °C; Hess range contains it.
HME performanceEfficiency 70% to 90% relative humidity and 30–31 °C (RCP 105 Management of MV slide 38)HME should provide a minimum of 30 mg H₂O/L (Hess CPG 15-1); ideal HME operates at 70% efficiency or higher (Hess Ch 15)Deck 70–90% RH.
APRV starting settings (Thigh, Tlow, Phigh)Initial Thigh 3–5 s, Tlow 0.2–0.8 s (≈10–20 cycles/min), Plow 0–8, Phigh for 4–8 mL/kg PBW but below 30–35 cm H₂O (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 26); Hess Ch 20 also Tlow 0.2–0.8 s“Open lung” APRV in the recruitment notes: Phigh 25–30 cm H₂O, Thigh 4–6 s, Tlow 0.4–0.8 s (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 12 notes, repeated slide 82 notes)Use slide 26 for initial APRV settings; the slide 12/82 values describe an open-lung recruitment strategy. Both decks agree the patient spends < 1.5 s at Plow.
HFOV adult weaning: mPaw before switching to PCFiO₂ to 0.40, then reduce Pmean 2–3 cm H₂O every 4–6 h until it is 20 cm H₂O or less; then consider switching to PC at pressure limit ≤ 30 and PEEP 10–12; then standard SBT (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 96)Wean FiO₂ to 40%; reduce mPaw in 2–3 cm H₂O increments every 4–6 h to a 22–24 cm H₂O range; switch to PCV with PEEP 12 (RCP 105 Management of MV slide 20)Use Ch 11 (RCP 203 deck): ≤ 20 cm H₂O before switching to PC with limit ≤ 30 and PEEP 10–12.
Patient–ventilator asynchrony: the three typesThree major categories: trigger, flow, cycle (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 78)Three forms: trigger, cycle, mode asynchrony (Hess Ch 20 — Patient–Ventilator Interaction)Use Ch 11: trigger, flow, cycle. Hess’s mode asynchrony is still covered in item 15.
Over-sensitive trigger: example value-1 cm H₂O or 1 L/min “may be too low” (too sensitive) → autocycling; fix by decreasing sensitivity slightly to -2 cm H₂O or 2 L/min (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 79 notes)Too sensitive example -0.5 cm H₂O or 0.5 L/min (RCP 105 Management of MV slide 33 notes); typical pressure trigger 1 to 2 cm H₂O below PEEP (Hess Ch 20 — Breath Triggering)Use Ch 11 for this exam (-1 / 1 L/min may be too sensitive → -2 / 2 L/min). All sources agree: auto-triggering → make the trigger less sensitive and check leaks/condensate.
High inspiratory pressure alarm setting35 cm H₂O initially if PIP not yet measured, then 10 cm H₂O above observed PIP once stabilized, per AARC (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slides 19 and 67 notes)10 to 15 cm H₂O above the observed PIP (RCP 105 Initiation of MV slide 27)Use Ch 11: 35 initially, then 10 above observed PIP.
Low inspiratory pressure alarm setting10 cm H₂O below observed PIP or the PI setting in PC; on BiPAP, LIP 2–3 below IPAP (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slides 19 and 67 notes)10 to 15 cm H₂O below the observed PIP (RCP 105 Initiation of MV slide 26)Use Ch 11: 10 below observed PIP.
Tidal volume / low exhaled volume alarmVT alarms 150 mL above and 150 mL below the set VT (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slides 19 and 67 notes)Low exhaled volume alarm 100 mL lower than the expired VT (RCP 105 Initiation of MV slide 26)Use Ch 11: ±150 mL of set VT.
Optimal PEEP and the lower inflection pointOptimal PEEP is the lowest pressure needed to exceed the LIP (slide 46); once measured, PEEP typically set 2–3 cm H₂O above the LIP (slide 46 notes) (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11)Lower inflection point = best PEEP level (RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis slide 62 notes)Use Ch 11: set PEEP 2–3 cm H₂O above the LIP.
Double triggering: which asynchrony typeShown under Trigger Asynchrony: 'Figure 11-13: ... Showing Double Triggering' (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 79)Cycle asynchrony: premature cycle (I-time too short) -> the patient might double-trigger; breath stacking up to twice the set VT (Hess Ch 20 — Patient–Ventilator Interaction)Use Ch 11 for a 'which category' question (trigger asynchrony). The Hess mechanism (I-time too short) is still the cause to fix (item 14).

21PIP, Pplat and PEEP: Normals, Lung Condition, Effects, Auto-PEEP

TopicValue A (source)Value B (source)Which to use
Plateau pressure ceilingPplat < 35 cm H₂O (RCP 105 Initiation slide 32 'safe'; RCP 105 Procedures slide 34 slide text); RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 38 (PC adjustments) prints 'Pplat ≤ 30–35 cm H₂O' (the ≤ is a Symbol-font glyph, U+F0A3, in the text file; a range, not a clean < 35)Pplat < 30 cm H₂O (ARDSNet: Procedures slide 34 and notes 'accepted goal'; Initiation slide 13; Hess Ch 20 'lower if possible'; Hess Ch 51; RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 31: Pplat > 30 in VC → switch to PC at 30 or less)Use < 30 cm H₂O (Ch 11 slide 31 agrees); recognize < 35 as Chang's printed value and ≤ 30–35 as Ch 11 slide 38's range.
Driving pressure: which subtractionPIP − PEEP called 'Driving Pressure or delta P (total pressure delivered!)' (RCP 203 Lssn 2b slide 37 slide text); 'In PC modes, alter the delivered VT by increasing or decreasing the Driving Pressure, termed ∆P (PIP – PEEP)' and equal-pressure PEEP method holds ∆P (PIP – PEEP) at 15–25 (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slides 38, 44)Driving pressure = Pplat − PEEP; PIP − PEEP is total pressure delivered, 'NOT the true lung driving pressure' (RCP 203 Lssn 2b slide 37 notes); Hess Ch 20 target < 15For a PC question about changing VT, the decks' ∆P = PIP − PEEP (Ch 11). For the lung-protective driving-pressure limit (< 15 cm H₂O), use Pplat − PEEP (Hess; Ch 3 notes).
Applied (extrinsic) PEEP to counter auto-PEEPUp to 80–90% of measured auto-PEEP, only for spontaneously breathing patients (RCP 203 Lssn 2b slide 42 notes)Up to 75% to 85% of auto-PEEP in obstructive disease (Hess Ch 20 Obstructive Lung Disease); generally no more than 10 cm H₂O in asthma (Hess Ch 32)Use the RCP 203 80–90%.
PEEP level where danger startsPEEP > 10 cm H₂O (or Paw > 30; PIP > 50) → alveolar rupture/barotrauma (RCP 105 Operating Modes slide 9; Initiation slide 32 'safe' < 10)Limit PEEP by its effect: avoid Pplat > 30 or driving pressure > 15 (Hess Ch 20 ARDS); Procedures slide 34 notes say many ARDS patients need 10–15 or higher; RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 optimal-PEEP examples 15 (slide 44) and 18 (slide 88), RM PEEP 20–25 (slide 85)Exam answer > 10 cm H₂O; know that ARDS PEEP is individualized.
Normal airway resistanceIntubated adults 5–12 cm H₂O/L/s (RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 54)Normal Raw 0.6–2.4 cm H₂O/L/s, about 1–3 (RCP 105 Principles of MV slide 2; population not stated)Use the RCP 203 5–12 for an intubated, ventilated adult.
Pause length for auto-PEEP (and Pplat)End-expiratory pause 0.5 to 1.5 s (Hess Ch 20 Plateau Pressure and Auto-PEEP)End-expiratory pause 0.5 to 2 s (Hess Ch 51 Alveolar Pressure)No deck number; 0.5–1.5 s fits both.

22Respiratory Drugs

TopicValue A (source)Value B (source)Which to use
Succinylcholine onset and durationParalysis in 60–90 seconds, clinical duration 10–15 minutes (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 18 NMBA deck, slide 13 and notes)1 to 1.5 mg/kg IV push → complete relaxation in 30 to 60 seconds, lasting 4 to 5 minutes (Hess Ch 57); "fastest onset (about 1 minute)" (RCP 105 Weaning deck slide 43 notes, Chang)Use the NMBA deck: onset 60–90 s, duration 10–15 min (the instructor's pharmacology deck). Know Hess's 30–60 s / 4–5 min as the book figure.
BUN normal range7–20 mg/dL (RCP 202 Renal deck slide 23/26 notes; also "around 7–20" in RCP 104 Ch 13 notes)8–18 mg/dL (RCP 103 Clinical Data slide 53); 10–20 mg/dL (RCP 105 Principles of MV slide 39); 8–20 mg/dL (RCP 104 Ch 13 azotemia note); 7–21 mg/dL (Hess Ch 5)Use 7–20 mg/dL (RCP 202 Renal deck = this course).
Serum creatinine normal rangeMen 0.7–1.3, women 0.6–1.1 mg/dL (RCP 202 Renal deck slide 26 notes)0.6–1.2 mg/dL (RCP 103 Clinical Data slide 53); 0.7–1.5 mg/dL (RCP 105 Principles of MV slide 39); women 0.5–1.1 / men 0.6–1.2 and "about 0.6–1.3" (RCP 104 Ch 13 notes); 0.7–1.4 mg/dL (Hess Ch 5)Use men 0.7–1.3 / women 0.6–1.1 mg/dL (RCP 202 Renal deck = this course).
Propofol and analgesia"Propofol provides analgesia only at high dose" (RCP 105 Weaning deck slide 67, Chang)"it does not provide analgesia" (Hess Ch 57); Weaning deck notes also say Propofol "does NOT provide pain relief"Treat propofol as a sedative with no useful analgesia; give an opioid for pain (both sources agree on that practice).
Opioid receptor subtypesmu (sedation), kappa (hypnosis), sigma (delirium) (RCP 105 Weaning deck slide 58, Chang)mu, delta, and kappa receptors (Hess Ch 57); naloxone competes at mu, kappa and sigma sites (Hess Ch 57)For the deck-based exam use mu/kappa/sigma; naloxone blocks them, with greatest affinity for mu (both sources).
Succinylcholine in liver/kidney failure"If liver/kidneys are bad → use Cisatracurium / Avoid: Sux" (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 18 NMBA deck, slide 33 notes)"Declines in renal or hepatic function do not necessitate dose reductions" (Hess Ch 57)Use the deck: avoid Sux and use cisatracurium in liver/kidney failure.
Classification of intrathecal routeParenteral (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 2 slides 4 notes, 5)Percutaneous or mucosal (Hess Ch 57)Use the deck: intrathecal = parenteral.
Albuterol duration of action4 to 6 hours (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 6 slide 23 class slide; slide 29 saligenin slide; RCP 103 Asthma slide 36 '~4–6 hours')Duration: 5-12 hr (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 6 slide 30)Use 4–6 hours for albuterol as a SABA (three places agree). The 5–12 hr figure sits on the albuterol slide that also lists the extended-release tablet.
Ipratropium MDI and nebulizer dose and frequencyMDI 17 µg/puff, 2 puffs QID; SVN 0.2% solution (0.5 mg) QID (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 7 slide 14)pMDI 18 µg/puff and nebulized 500 µg, both bid–qid (Hess Ch 32 Table 32-3)Use the deck: 17 µg/puff, 2 puffs QID and 0.5 mg SVN QID.
What the monoclonal antibodies are (IgE or IgG)'DNA-derived humanized IgE antibodies' (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 12 slide 4)'DNA-derived humanized IgG antibodies' (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 12 slide 34); Hess Ch 57 calls omalizumab 'an immunoglobulin G (IgG) monoclonal antibody' that inhibits IgE binding Answer IgG: IgG-class antibodies that target IgE (omalizumab) or IL-5. Slide 4 is the outlier.
Levalbuterol side-effect advantage over racemic albuterolSide effects of tremor and heart rate changes were less; but side effects were similar at a similar dose (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 6 slide 33)Superiority of the L-isomer over the racemic mixture is unproven; at higher doses tachyarrhythmias are similar (Hess Ch 57 and 646-649)Use the deck wording for the exam (less tremor and HR change, but similar at similar dose); know Hess calls the advantage unproven.
Formoterol onset of actionLABA class onset 20 minutes (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 6 slide 23); salmeterol slower onset than albuterol (20 minutes) (slide 36)Formoterol: short time to bronchodilatory effect (3 minutes) (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 6 slide 37); Hess Ch 57: formoterol 'has an onset of action similar to albuterol' Formoterol 3 minutes (deck), salmeterol 20 minutes; the LABA class slide's 20 min does not apply to formoterol. Hess agrees formoterol is fast (similar to albuterol).
Is acetylcysteine a mucolytic?Mucolytic drug that breaks disulfide bonds and thins mucus in the lab, but 'has not been shown to improve mucus clearance in patients' and 'should not be used as a mucoactive medication' (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9 slide 34 notes and text)'Adjunctive mucolytic therapy used for abnormal or viscous mucus secretions' in COPD, TB, bronchiectasis, fibrosis, CF (Hess Ch 57), yet benefit 'unproven' and it 'has fallen out of favor' (Hess Ch 16)Say: mucolytic by mechanism (breaks disulfide bonds), but not recommended clinically; its best-supported use is the acetaminophen antidote.
Benralizumab site of actionBenralizumab, mepolizumab and reslizumab 'block interleukin-5 (IL-5)' (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 12 slides 34, 35)Mepolizumab and reslizumab bind IL-5; benralizumab binds the alpha subunit of the IL-5 receptor on eosinophils and basophils (Hess Ch 32)Use the deck for the exam (all three block the IL-5 pathway and reduce eosinophils); know benralizumab targets the receptor.
Levalbuterol duration of action~5–8 hours (RCP 103 Asthma slide 36)Short-acting class (albuterol AND levalbuterol): onset 15 minutes, duration 4 to 6 hours (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 6 slide 23)Use the Pharm Ch 6 class slide (4–6 h) for the SABA class as the instructor taught it; know 103 Asthma gives levalbuterol ~5–8 h.

23Disinfection and Sterilization Methods

TopicValue A (source)Value B (source)Which to use
Glutaraldehyde sterilization timeFull 10 hours (RCP 203 Lssn 5a — Ch 7 Infection Control, slide 19; notes: follow manufacturer, times vary by product)6 to 10 hours (Hess Ch 31 — disinfection methods)Deck: 10 hours
Oral chlorhexidine for VAP preventionDaily oral care with chlorhexidine in the CDC VAP bundle (RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9, slide 60); listed among VAP-prevention strategies (RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV, slide 46)Oral care with chlorhexidine no longer recommended (Hess Ch 36 — Box 36-7)Deck: listed as a VAP-prevention measure (note Hess says no longer recommended)
Bleach (sodium hypochlorite) dilutionNo number: C. difficile → EPA-registered sporicidal disinfectant; spills → follow product instructions and facility protocol for the dilution (RCP 203 Lssn 5a — Ch 7, slides 25, 26)1:10 dilution of 5.25% for blood spills and C. difficile rooms (Hess Ch 31 — disinfection methods)Deck principle (follow product/facility); know Hess 1:10 only as Hess's number

27Pressure-Volume and Flow-Volume Loops

TopicValue A (source)Value B (source)Which to use
PEEP relative to the lower inflection pointOptimal PEEP is the lowest pressure needed to exceed the LIP (aka critical opening pressure); once measured, PEEP typically is set 2–3 cm H₂O above the LIP (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 46 and notes)'Set PEEP at or slightly above the LIP'; lower inflection point = best PEEP (RCP 105 Waveforms slides 58, 62 notes); PEEP set slightly greater than the lower inflection point (Hess Ch 20 Box 20-6)Use Ch 11 (the RCP 203 deck the ask names): PEEP 2–3 cm H₂O above the LIP. All sources put PEEP above the LIP.
Meaning of the upper inflection pointUIP = changeover from high to low compliance at high volume, overdistension occurring; beyond it the loop forms a 'beak sign' = excessive VT (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slides 46, 75); onset of overdistension, reduce VT until the beak/duckbill disappears (RCP 105 Waveforms slide 59)UIP indicates overdistention but 'might represent the end of recruitment' (Hess Ch 51)Use the decks: UIP = overdistension.
Significant bronchodilator responseFEV1 improves by at least 12–15% and by 200 mL or more in adults (RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 76)FEV1 increase of 12% (minimum 200 mL) or more (Hess Ch 8 Bronchodilator Testing)Use the RCP 203 deck (≥ 12–15% and ≥ 200 mL).
P-V loop width and airway resistanceSlide 73 caption (Fig 11-8): '(Loop C) the thinner the loop, increased airway resistance.' (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 73)Slide 74 notes (Fig 11-7): 'The loop’s width reflects resistance — the wider the loop, the higher the resistance'; increased expiratory resistance widens the loop (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 74 notes); RCP 203 Lssn 3a slides 54–55: increased Raw → increased width of the P-V loop; RCP 105 Waveforms slide 57: ↑ resistance widens the PIP–PAlv gapUse wider loop = more resistance (slide 74 notes, agreed by RCP 203 Lssn 3a slides 54–55; RCP 105 slide 57 shows only the PIP–PAlv gap widening, not loop width). The slide 73 caption sits under the 'LIKELY TO APPEAR ON THE NBRC EXAM' tag, but its loop figure is an image, so the 'thinner' wording cannot be checked against the picture.

28Transcutaneous Monitoring

TopicValue A (source)Value B (source)Which to use
Sensor temperatureUsually 44°C if monitoring PtcO₂ and PtcCO₂; as low as 37°C adequate if PtcCO₂ only (RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures slide 12)Chang: 44°C for PtcCO₂, 40–42°C in neonates, maximum 45°C (RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9) slide 51 notes; slide 51 text says 42°C in neonates, up to 45°C). Hess: the combined earlobe SpO₂/PtcCO₂ sensor is heated to 42°C and PtcCO₂ agrees reasonably with PaCO₂ at 37°C (Hess Ch 2). Only Chang's figures are in the guideRCP 203 deck: 44°C, or as low as 37°C for PtcCO₂ only
Site rotation intervalEvery 2–6 hours, depending on sensor temperature, infant size and manufacturer (RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures slide 12)Chang: every 4 hours (RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9) slide 50). Hess CPG 2-2: "as often as necessary", alternate sites, follow manufacturer (Hess Ch 2); Hess earlobe SpO₂/PtcCO₂ sensor removed after 8 hours and moved to the other earlobe (; not in the draft)RCP 203 deck: 2–6 hours
Threshold above which PtcO₂ is inaccuratePtcO₂ underestimates PaO₂ when PaO₂ is higher than 100 torr; equals PaO₂ only when PaO₂ is less than 100 torr (RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results slide 18; RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1 Evaluate Data slide 46)PtcO₂ becomes less accurate in operating range greater than 80 mm Hg (RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9) slide 50)RCP 203 deck: 100 torr. Keep the two numbers in separate sentences; do not merge them

33Suction Catheter Size

TopicValue A (source)Value B (source)Which to use
Suction catheter size ruleOcclude < 50% of ETT ID (< 70% infants); double the ID, then select the next smallest catheter (6.0 mm -> 12 -> 10 Fr) (RCP 203 Lssn 6b slide 11, RCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion)OD no more than 1/2 to 2/3 of airway ID, 14 Fr usually acceptable for adults (Hess Ch 17, Hess Ch 17); Fr = 2 x ETT ID (Hess Ch 22, Hess Ch 22)Use the deck: 2 x ID then one size smaller; CPG 17-1 < 50% / < 70% agrees with the deck

Adult suction pressure-100 to -120 mm Hg recommended starting range (RCP 203 Lssn 6b slide 10, RCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion); -80 to -120 mm Hg adults on clinical evals (RCP 203 Lssn 4 notes of S86/S88, RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment)No more than 150 mm Hg in adults (Hess Ch 17)Use the Ch 10 deck: -100 to -120 mm Hg; recognize -80 to -120 (Ch 6 notes)

22Respiratory Drugs

AskWhat is missing
Acetylcysteine (dose and frequency)No nebulized NAC dose, volume or frequency in any text. Pharm Ch 9 slide 8 points to Table 9.1, an image. Hess gives only the oral BRONCUS dose (600 mg twice daily, a trial dose in COPD, not a nebulized frequency).
Beclomethasone dipropionate (dosing frequency)No deck frequency. Heuer's inhaled-drug table gives QVAR 40 or 80 µg/puff MDI, 2 puffs twice daily. Hess Table 32-1 gives strength (40 or 80 µg/puff) and medium daily dose (>240-480 µg) but no frequency; Pharm Ch 11 Table 11.1 and Hess Table 57-3 are images or blank in the text file. Hess's class statement is 'once or twice a day (depending on the drug and asthma class)'.
Levalbuterol (dosing frequency)No deck frequency; Pharm Ch 6 Table 6.1 is an image. In Hess Table 32-3 the levalbuterol rows are misaligned in the text file: the strings '45 µg/puff prn, q6h', '0.63-1.25 mg prn, q6h-q8h' and '0.31 mg and 0.63 mg prn; q4h-q6h' sit near the levalbuterol labels but cannot be assigned to a drug and form with certainty. No frequency should be given for levalbuterol until the printed table is checked.
Half-life of meds (numbers)No numeric half-life for albuterol, levalbuterol, ipratropium, beclomethasone, NAC, the ICU sedatives, opioids or NMBAs. Hess Table 57-7 (Pharmacokinetics of Neuromuscular Blockers) and the Weaning deck Table 13-9 are images; only the table title survives. The numbers that exist: adenosine ~12 s (Pharm Ch 21), benzodiazepine sleep aids >12-24 h (Pharm Ch 23), omalizumab 17-22 days (Hess Ch 57), COHb 4-5 h / 60-90 min / 20-30 min (Burn deck), plus qualitative statements (lorazepam much longer than midazolam, theophylline highly variable, milrinone shorter than inamrinone).
Standard frequency of all respiratory meds (drugs with no frequency in any source)No deck frequency for acetylcysteine, beclomethasone, levalbuterol, cromolyn or the anti-IL-5 antibodies. Books fill them in: acetylcysteine 3 to 4 times daily, beclomethasone (QVAR) 2 puffs twice daily, cromolyn 4 times daily, levalbuterol SVN three times a day (all Heuer); mepolizumab and reslizumab every 4 weeks, benralizumab every 8 weeks after three doses (Hess). Still none in any source: the NMBAs (given by bolus or infusion, not a standing frequency), the ICU sedatives (midazolam, propofol, dexmedetomidine, etomidate, ketamine, barbiturates) and the opioids (only 'PRN by RN' or PCA). DuoNeb has no frequency in a deck (Hess gives 'every 6 hours' for the albuterol-ipratropium combination without naming the brand; 103 Asthma slide 36 gives '~6 hours' as a duration).
Sites of action (receptor sites), Hess Table 57-1Hess Table 57-1 'Receptors Affecting Respiratory Function' has no body in the text file; only the Respiratory Recap bullets survive (beta1 heart; beta2 lungs and blood vessels; muscarinic in bronchial tissue). Receptor sites for the other drugs were taken drug by drug from the decks.
Immunoglobulins (drug class)No source treats 'immunoglobulins' as a drug class beyond the five antibody classes (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 12 slide 16), the IgE-directed and IL-5-directed monoclonal antibodies (Pharm Ch 12; Hess Ch 32, 57) and palivizumab (Pharm Ch 13 slide 36). Nothing on RSV immune globulin. IVIG appears only for neuromuscular disease (RCP 103 Neuro slides 21, 44 notes; Hess Ch 44 GBS and myasthenia gravis).

Exam Strategy & Word Play

Word play and special precaution words, exactly as the final study guide lists them

§How to use the word-play list

Know what you know; and know you know it!!

NBRC WORLD ONLY – forget about everything else!!

TRUST YOUR “FIRST” INSTINCT WHEN YOU CHOOSE AN ANSWER!! Toooooooo many students, after reviewing the exam, wish they had stayed with their First Choice (answer)!!

Word play to watch for on ALL exams (classroom and NBRC).

Phrases That Mean the SAME THING

PhraseMeans the same thing as
Bronchoprovocation testMethacholine Challenge
Cardio-Respiratory arrestFull Arrest
Chronic HypercarbiaCO₂ Retainer
Controlled O₂ concentrationSpecific FiO₂
Heart FailureCardiac Arrest = Cardiac Failure (O₂ does NOT help!)
HFNCVapoTherm or Optiflow (Fisher & Paykel) (NOT HFNC up to 15L)
Induction AgentsAnesthetic Meds
ImbalanceMismatch
Inclusion CriteriaIndication
Liberation ParametersWeaning
Long-standingChronic
MainstayBest suited for / Therapy of choice (1st choice)
NIPPVNPPV = NIV = BiPAP
No purposeful movement to commandUnconscious
Oxygen-induced HypoventilationKnocked out the Hypoxic Drive
PBW (predicted body weight)IBW (ideal body weight)
Rapid Response Team (RRT)Medical Emergency Team (MET)
SIMVIMV

Special Precaution Words — They’re Trying to “Trick” You

  • Adequate
  • Aggressive pain control does NOT mean too much meds
  • At least
  • Blunted
  • Changes to treatment plan and “actual orders” are NOT the same
  • Eliminate does NOT mean Filter (Lungs vs. Kidneys)
  • Except
  • First
  • Laryngotracheal (Injury)
  • Mechanical Ventilation = Vent or BiPAP; it is NOT specific to Vents only
  • Most commonly used
  • Most likely
  • Next
  • Nonpulmonary
  • Not
  • Observation
  • Obliterated
  • Precedence (Importance)
  • Recommend at this time
  • Routinely
  • Severe
  • Significance
Sources. Your Study Brain lecture decks with speaker notes (RCP 203, 202, 105, 103 and the RCP 104 pharmacology decks), and Hess, MacIntyre & Galvin, Respiratory Care: Principles and Practice, 4th ed. Every card carries the deck and slide, or the chapter, it came from.

What this version is. The RCP 203 final-exam study guide answered in the lean Tier 3 study-guide-only format, updated 25 Oct 2026 for the new and revised PowerPoints: 177 cards across 34 topic lines, 129 source conflicts, 44 honest gaps.

25 Oct 2026 — the Lesson 8 decks. Lssn 8a (Ch 12) and Lssn 8b (Ch 13) added ten cards, into sections 1, 3, 7, 10, 12, 19, 20, 22 and 26. Both chapters are Heuer’s and Heuer’s book text is not on disk, so those ten cards cite the deck slides and speaker notes. Two diagrams are carried over from the RCP 203 midterm Tier 3 deck where the final asks the same thing: breath-sound locations and the static/dynamic compliance pressure curve. Scope: final study guide only — 140 questions, 100 points, 150 minutes.
